madam president, i am deeply sorry for what has happened in australia, the devastating situation, which gives us yet another lesson about climate change. you have to think that australia has emitted more co2 from these fires than the uk has in a whole year. we must learn from this. we are about to launch the european green deal, which also includes a forestry strategy. let us not import the australian forest into our european territory. in portugal, in greece, in galicia there are monoculture eucalyptus plantations that are also imitating that forest that is burning in australia. let's not make climate change easy. when conditions are above 90 degrees fahrenheit, below 30 percent humidity, and with winds of more than 20 miles per hour, forest fires are more likely to occur. so we must learn from what happened in australia so that it does not happen in europe. and let's put in place realistic prevention strategies for this coming climate change.
in writing. the scale and virulence of the australian fires, which in many cases have led to loss of control, made it appropriate for a parliamentary debate to address the devastating effects of climate change and extreme phenomenology and other environmental and international issues. we socialists are aware that climate change is real and that we must continue to fight against it. the remoteness of the oceanic continent from europe did not seem to justify this debate to some political groups. however, these fires are having an impact on europe and the rest of the world. so many toxic gases are being generated that there is even a risk of other weather phenomena being triggered. the debate is another call to meet international climate commitments. it is not acceptable that there are countries that do not comply with the paris agreement or that there are political leaders who deny climate change or ignore its consequences. we are facing a climate and ecological crisis. it is heartbreaking to see thousands of acres destroyed by the flames and how certain types of mammals that exist only in australia are threatened with extinction.
madam president, the subject we are debating today is extremely serious, because it is the only known case so far of border controls being reintroduced because of an escalation of violent and organised crime. crossborder crime has only one answer: crossborder cooperation. and when it comes to organized crime and terrorism, borders do not protect sovereignty: borders protect criminals. europe must be a place of freedom for its citizens and not a place of mobility for criminals. let's not kid ourselves. we know the problems and what needs to be done. we need to talk about more effective confiscation of assets, control of cyberspace, the fight against money laundering, access to evidence in judicial proceedings, harmonisation of legislation, cooperation on instruments for arrest and surrender, strengthening of european agencies. we must, in short, put our deeds where our words put them.
madam president, commissioner, the european union is often accused of not being able to provide solutions to citizens immediately, of being late. and it is true. this directive, adopted in 2014, is a clear example that willpower is sometimes not the best way to achieve the goal we want to achieve. it is not normal that, in the year 2020 and being digital sovereignty one of the great challenges that this next and already existing european commission has, it is one of the issues that we have to deal with in this new legislature. because it is true that in our homes - and many of my colleagues said this - we have a large number of chargers that we no longer use. but also for an important issue, and young people have set a good example for us with climate action. we can't afford to keep buying a charger for every electronic device, and we're not just talking about mobile phones. we talk about watches, we talk about cameras. we're talking about many types of electronic devices that need to be renewed. we need a universal single charger and we also need the cables to stop being effective. (the president cut the speaker off)
madam president, in the european union women work for free for two months a year compared to men, not to mention the many who work unpaid and in conditions of slavery. we know very well why. because it is women who continue to provide unpaid care at home and poorly paid care outside. because social services have been cut. because in the chain of care there are neither men nor institutions. we know what measures we should take, but we do not take them. we did not want to force large companies to undergo pay audits and pay penalties when they do not. we did not want to bet on parental leave which would prevent these companies from discriminating against women of childbearing age and force men to take care of their own children. early childhood education is still privatized in many states and is very expensive, which excludes women from the market. we have not secured a system of care for dependents that is public, universal and free, so that informal care does not fall on them. we're killing women on a double and triple day basis, while a good portion of the gentlemen grow like mushrooms in their cubicles, washed, combed, ironed and reproduced. in europe many women work part-time to remain poor, to have less health coverage and to age on minimum pensions. we still haven't done enough.
madam president, we are discussing today a subject which should be closed: the gender pay gap. because women are paid 16% less than men, and that is why we are asking the commission to be more ambitious in terms of equality. we need the equal pay directive to be reviewed as soon as possible and we need measures at european level to improve work-life balance. but one of the things that i'm particularly concerned about as an engineer is the impact of digitization on the wage gap. there will be a great demand for well-paid jobs in science and technology, and the percentage of women studying these fields is much lower than that of men, at 17%, which will mean that this wage gap will be widened even further. we must work to ensure that girls and women choose these types of studies and occupations. so we ask him for ambition. there are many measures that are needed; consider this a priority and present us with your gender equality strategy as soon as possible
mr president, the wage gap is not an old or modern legend, as was said in this house a moment ago. it's a very serious problem. not only a very serious social justice problem, but also an economic problem because, above all, women are practically 60% of university graduates. it is true that measures have been taken and the gap has been narrowing, albeit very slowly, especially in the last decade. and i also think, commissioner dalli, that this directive, this initiative on transparency is going to be very useful, but i think it is not enough, especially in the middle of a digital revolution. because right now the most demanded and best paid jobs are precisely in the stem field, in the technological ones and, according to data from the european institute for gender equality, since 2009 the presence of women in this field is decreasing and today it is 17%. we must therefore take very concrete measures in this direction and tackle the problem in a very broad way. so my question goes along these lines. what measures are being taken to reduce this gap?
mr president, homelessness is not just a lack of access to decent housing, it also involves serious problems of health, access to the labour market and social benefits and, above all, even personal security. housing first is a model that is working successfully in some member states, and is a strategy that combines accompaniment with access to social and health services. we therefore need a strong commitment from the commission to implement this model in all member states, with sufficient budgetary allocation and the inclusion of homelessness as an ambitious target in the social indicators table of the sustainable half-year. i am aware of your commitment to this issue, commissioner, and that is why i hope that the commission will soon present a draft recommendation setting out an action plan to eradicate homelessness. in conclusion, i would like to point out that this problem affects the citizens who move within the european union significantly. at this point, a new commission communication would be appropriate to ensure that the rights of these workers are respected and they do not end up on the streets.
mr president, i would now like to say, at 11 p.m., what president sassoli, in breach of my rights as a member, has prevented me from saying this afternoon, in accordance with rule 195 of the rules of procedure of this house. article 3 of the rules of procedure of this house establishes a procedure for becoming a member of parliament, and the competent electoral authority of each state must send the corresponding credentials so that one can be subsequently admitted. however, the attitude of president sassoli, in accepting that mr puigdemont and mr comín, who have not appeared before the competent spanish electoral authority, have not respected the constitution and are fugitives from spanish justice, can be in this parliament, constitutes an attack on the european electoral act, an attack on the rules of procedure of this house, and seriously jeopardises the legality and validity of all the agreements which are adopted in this house from now on. i don't know where mr. sassoli wants to go. he has already had to make concessions in relation to mr junqueras, since the supreme court of the kingdom of spain has shown him that we are right, and we also have the law on our side. he'll end up doing it with messrs. comín and puigdemont. i just want to say that mr sassoli is provoking an unprecedented institutional conflict, he is putting this parliament outside the law, and i remind him that without law there is no democracy.
mr. president, depopulation is one of the challenges we have to address in this new legislature. the ageing of the population, the exodus of young people and low birth rates from rural areas are common features of this phenomenon, but not the only ones. the problem is that the imbalance between depopulated areas and urban areas is growing. i would therefore like to stress here the responsibility of the european institutions to prevent these inequalities and to ensure that all citizens have access to basic services and opportunities, regardless of where they live. so far, the cap funds, the erdf funds and the european social fund have financed certain specific activities aimed at curbing depopulation, but now we must give priority to strategies, measures which are fully studied and financed and which are in line with the new needs and realities of 21st century society. for example, promoting new technologies and digitisation in rural areas through high-capacity networks, or also focusing on sustainable growth and development models and on stimulating and incentivising population. in short, what i want to ask for is speed, commitment and that we, once and for all, get down to work to implement all these measures in the light of the commitments we have all made to the demographic phenomenon and the demographic challenge.

madam president, president plenković, i would like to congratulate you. i would like to congratulate croatia on its presidency. i am especially moved because, when, a quarter of a century ago, i was as a journalist in the trenches and destroyed houses of osijek or karlovac, in slavonia or krajina, this was really a dream that seemed unattainable. it is precisely because of the immense beauty of the reunification of europeans, from tallinn to cadiz, from zagreb to amsterdam, that we cannot allow it to fail. the croatian presidency can help by warning all those who are trying to hijack europe for a superstate, of a concrete social democratic ideology, which ignores and ultimately destroys nations and democracy. they should call for common sense against irrational ideological projects or that conference on the future of europe, so suspicious, that it seems to want to deceive the european states with a federalism by the back door, which not all states and, of course, not all europeans want.

madam president, i, like some of my colleagues previously, want to draw the attention of mr barnier to the settled status issue and the physical proof. i’ve interacted several times with spanish citizens in the uk. i’ve gone to london, to oxford, to several places to see them. they tell me that the system is unnecessarily cruel and that it leads to a lot of uncertainty. the basic issue is that they get a pdf by email, which they can print as a pdf, which anybody could fake, which says that they have been granted settled status. that is all. they don’t get any card they can use when they travel. they don’t get any card that they can use when they go to work. they don’t know if the next time they come to the uk, somebody might not find their status. there is a very easy solution to that, which is a card with your photo which they could get, like you get in the us when you’re a permanent resident. our citizens are being subjected to an unnecessarily cruel system and i think that it should be a priority in negotiating with the uk authorities that, as our resolution says in point 10, this physical proof should exist and be given via a certain and simple process.
madam president, commissioners, ladies and gentlemen, we are on the right track with this investment plan for sustainability, with this fund for just transition. in the european parliament we have always said that new priorities must have additional resources and it is very positive that the just transition fund has an additional eur 7.5 billion. my question is: why 7.5 billion euros? will that be enough? it is something that parliament will have to study and verify. in addition, complementarity is also positive with cohesion policy, but it is necessary that cohesion policy is not distorted: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. and then there 's this appeal: the member states must define their investment plans, they must know what they want for their regions. otherwise, once the amounts for each region have been fixed, they will complain, they will say that it is brussels' fault and they will say that the money is not enough after all, when they have not even said what they wanted to say. and it is urgent that each member state, each region, also do its homework in this area.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, a trillion dollars of investment for the sustainable development of europe over the next ten years: this is the important objective that the commission is presenting today for a europe that is climate neutral by 2050 and increasingly socially inclusive. it is certainly a lot of resources, but if we are to successfully tackle the profound changes that these challenges require, we must commit to making more resources available in the coming years. i would like to return to the commission's proposals: i would like to highlight two that contain significant new developments. first, finally a fund with resources dedicated to making the transition socially equitable, because we have to attack climate change but also avoid leaving anyone behind, especially in sectors that will face major industrial conversions, because sustainability has to be for everyone to be achieved. secondly, it is important to note that the investment plan explicitly refers to the opening of a discussion on the exclusion of sustainable investment from the deficit calculation in the stability pact in the member states. i am not unaware of the timidity with which the reference is inserted in the text, but i would like to reiterate, as some colleagues have already done, that the subject must be tackled with determination in order to enable the member states to make their essential contribution to this challenge. this is the way europe must go. to maintain a high level of ambition on key issues such as environmental sustainability, while at the same time reaching out to those at risk of being left behind in society. focusing on environmental sustainability and social inclusion are the keys to getting european citizens excited again.
madam president, commissioners, during the crisis i had a friend who was an economist and i said to him: 'there are people behind the macroeconomic measures that are taken.' and he said, "well, get out of the way". sustainability is a principle, it's a goal. but sustainability, like crises, is always paid for by the weakest; it cannot be that sustainability and the path to it, transition and change, are paid for again by the weakest and most vulnerable, because we are here to make europe more sustainable, but also more just. and there are some who, in front of sustainability, put some concepts and some ideas that forget that there are people behind it. so we need funds, yes, but we also need goals, and values. and that value is to develop social policy, the european social pillar. because this development is not just about funding: it is about concrete objectives to ensure that our citizens never again see crises, changes or transitions as a means of preying on the weakest, the most vulnerable.
madam president, i have to ask you. thank you, mr. vice president, for introducing the sustainable investment plan and the just transition fund to us today. i believe that these are two essential instruments for the european green deal. two comments on that. with regard to the just transition fund - my colleagues have said this - i think we must be sure that it will indeed bring about a transition. and so that criterion of conditionality, of getting off coal and really committing to climate neutrality, must be an essential component. so first of all, i would like to ask that that be incorporated into the just transition fund, which is a core element. we can't leave anyone behind on that. and so i think it's right to make this a real priority. and then, on the investment plan, from what we know, i feel that we are lacking a little bit of ambition when it comes to getting resources for that investment. i think it is a kind of juncker plan 3.0 with national contributions. and i think that's where we need to go a little bit further. we need new sources of resources to be able to undertake a plan that is extremely ambitious. i doubt that with what we have got, what we have on the table, we can reach that billion euros. so my second message would be: please let's put more ambition into getting the resources so that this plan is a true transformative tool.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i think it's a great vision that we become climate neutral in europe. the old commission already showed in 2018 how we can do this: with additional jobs and additional economic growth. but we need a good framework for this, and some regions need to adapt more than others. so it's good that we're taking these people through the just transition fund. i stress that we must do this in all regions of europe. we cannot apply the old criteria to this new objective. even in richer regions, people are facing challenges that we would not have had without the coal phase-out. and that's why we have to do something about it. we should not have the illusion that europe can do this alone. we also need national support, and the commission must approve this under state aid law. we must also discuss own resources from the eec. that is why my group is in favour of extending the eec too. i regret that we still have a lot of resistance here, but i hope that we can reach an agreement. in conclusion, i would like to pay tribute to a colleague who is sitting behind me and who is speaking after me, which is wrong, because he is the creator of the just transition fund. without it we would not have this proposal today and we would not have so much support for it. thank you, jerzy buzek, for your commitment, and we're fighting to make this work.
madam president, mr vice-president timmermans, i want to welcome your announcement of the allocation of €7.5 billion for the just transition funds and at the same time i want to welcome what you announced this morning, namely that the european investment bank will be involved in supporting the projects that are necessary for decarbonisation at the level of the european union. in the case of romania, the presence of the european investment bank is vital because the investment need that i saw in a study presented in december is 80 billion euros for the next ten years, that is 4 percent of gdp, which should be spent every year starting this year; it is an enormous amount and without the support of the european investment bank, it will be very difficult for us, and i hope that this will happen because in the past the european investment bank has supported more the countries that are the richest in the european union and little or none of those that needed the most help. at the same time, mr vice-president, i want to ask you to insist that the projects that are necessary for the regions affected by decarbonisation start immediately, as well as the social projects for these regions, which are vital for many people: in the case of romania alone, there are 170 000 people who are affected.

madam president, when we talk about the european green deal we must start from an unquestionable basic conception. political agreements reached in favour of environmental protection cannot cause irreparable damage to our economy and our citizens. while political agreements have been reached to achieve full decarbonisation in europe, it is now up to us to take the measures that will allow member states to remain competitive and continue to create opportunities for citizens and businesses. it is therefore logical that the european regions affected by decarbonisation and the inevitable relocation of companies and industries demand compensatory measures. spain and its mining regions are among them. i would like to emphasize the case of spain, because it would be illogical for a member state which has fully complied with the european union's rules in this field to be affected now because it has done its duty. the debate must focus on the how and the why. the objective of the just transition fund is to help regions affected by the forced closure of an economic activity to remain competitive. to help them recycle and transform and to offer their citizens other tools with which to build their future. it must never be a help to a lost cause that clears consciences. let us not seek to buy social peace. in conclusion, these funds must be accompanied by a thorough evaluation of their use, productivity and results. concrete plans and programmes promoting training, employment, innovation and the search for a competitive alternative energy source.
mr president, thank you, commissioner, for your presentation today. the just transition fund is key to enabling european regions to finance decarbonisation. we are talking about 100 billion euros over 10 years and we are very concerned about how these funds are going to be distributed. you, mr timmermans, in your presentation, mentioned the miners of asturias, my region. but asturias, castile and león, aragon, are regions of spain that have already closed all their mines, that have done their homework and are now closing the last coal-fired power stations. and these regions are suffering very much in terms of employment and this is affecting the depopulation problem very much. that is why we are asking you today to take these regions into account, the regions which have already done their homework, in this just transition fund. because, as you said, it is about not leaving anyone behind and it is essential that these regions do not lose any more jobs and do not lose any more population.
in writing. the ecological transition is a necessity that brings with it opportunities but also challenges: regions like mine, with energy-intensive businesses, need european support to change their production model and do so in a fair way, leaving no one behind. the citizens need assurances that the cost of the transition will not be borne by the usual people. in galicia and spain we know what we are talking about because we have closed the coal mines at great cost: unemployment, loss of productive tissue, depopulation. european solidarity is needed for those who must close their mines now, of course. but also for those of us who face grave consequences for having done so long before. our regions also need investment to revive their industry. and fair competition both in the internal market and with third countries. it is not possible for the poorer regions to end up paying the bill for the coal transition to rich countries which have not done their homework. we believe in solidarity with those who need it most. but we cannot allow this process to open new gaps. we need a just transition fund. and justice in its design.
in writing. the investment plan for a sustainable europe is the financial arm for implementing the green deal. it is also a response to citizens who have called for a roadmap to make the eu the first climate-neutral bloc. the just transition fund should also address the wider impact on regions and industries that are highly dependent on carbon and fossil fuels of the paradigm shift that climate neutrality will require. the path to climate neutrality is a complicated one and the just transition fund is here to cushion the difficulties of this change. the socialists have advocated that the transition be fair and not create more inequalities, and we will ensure that this is the case during the implementation of all the proposals and measures contained in the investment plan for a sustainable europe and the just transition fund. we are aware of the need to mobilise resources to reduce emissions and renew the necessary infrastructure. but above all, we socialists have a strong commitment to social justice and solidarity with those who have to face the most difficulties, so we will be following up the implementation of the plan and the fund in detail.

i am speaking on behalf of the gue/ngl group. madam president, the recent escalation of tensions that we have experienced in iran following the extrajudicial killing of soleimani has put the world at maximum tension. do you really think that you can give security to european citizens by following the incendiary policy of donald trump? let us not confuse security with military operations again. security is having institutions that provide the right to a dignified life with guarantees, with access to employment, to basic services. but security is also not suffering or participating in military interference in another country, or in coups orchestrated from the white house, or in drone bombings. security is being an actor in ensuring that international law is respected. we need to strengthen multilateralism, but that requires a look at ourselves, unconcerned with external interests. we are at a crucial moment not only for the countries in conflict but for the very fate of the european union and for millions of people in the world. let us give priority to dialogue, diplomacy, democratic control of military operations. let's bet on safety. let us commit ourselves to the just development of other countries, to the care of the planet, to feminism and democracy. then we'll be betting on safety.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, in libya, despite the many declarations of intent, we see that the eu has decided not to take a decision, leaving the field open to russia, the united states in the anti-jihadist vein and turkey. the commitment to an arms embargo has long been openly circumvented by turkey, the uae and egypt. europe's main responsibility is that it allowed the government in tripoli to be supported in practice only by erdoğan, first with weapons, drones and military advisers and now also with regular troops and jihadist groups from syria. the situation is now likely to further destabilise the area, damaging the interests of local companies, especially italian ones, and undermining human rights. a recent associated press investigation into the recipients of european funds, followed by a question from me, then revealed how officials from un agencies are aware that a considerable part of the resources sent to stabilize the area and improve conditions in detention centers are actually going to libyan militias and criminal organizations. this scandal, coupled with the steady increase in influence in the region by russia and turkey, attests to the failure of european policies in libya.
vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – madam president, i agree with some of the comments you have made. others are wishful thinking. you have to... you have to what? you have to ask erdoğan to cancel his agreement with al—serraj about the ... – i can ask. and what? the problem is that we haven’t been united on libya. let’s tell the truth. we europeans have had different positions with respect to libya and now maybe we are paying the price for the fact that we haven’t been able to represent a political approach shared by all member states. it’s true that some regret that turkey has a role, but turkey has a role and russia has a role. the latest news we have is that, after not signing the ceasefire – he is a man who refuses to sign the ceasefire because it seems that it would require them to agree with the agreement between al—serraj, who is heading the internationally recognised government, and turkey. but he will commit to staying in berlin next sunday – so the bad news is that there is no ceasefire and the good news is that it seems that, in spite of not having a ceasefire, they will be at the berlin conference. maybe there we can make the first step towards a political agreement. maybe you have been following all the statements that we have been delivering about libya from the foreign affairs council and by the high representative himself. let’s hope that in berlin there is a possibility of reaching an agreement between the parts and then we will have to deal with the implementation of this agreement. this will require two things: first, to monitor the ceasefire; and, second, to control the arms embargo. i will present to the foreign affairs council concrete proposals for the europeans to be more engaged on fulfilling these two duties. let’s see if we have enough will in order to act all together and start a new period of engagement with libya. that’s what i can tell you right now. other aspects have been dealt with by many of you. i cannot answer all your questions one by one, but i think i can summarise by saying that, if there is an agreement in berlin, subsequently there will be a ceasefire and then it will test the political will of the european union to be more engaged on the libyan issue. thank you for your comments.

the current situation in libya is the result of the aggression launched by the us, the eu and nato in 2011 to gain geostrategic control over the region and dominate the country's natural resources in clear violation of international law. this aggression resulted in a civil war, with untold suffering, violence and destruction. far from bringing freedom, it has only created political instability, social collapse, ethnic strife, religious intolerance, and gross violations of human rights. in the context of eu migration policies, this country has also become the centre of a network of slavery and human trafficking. the struggle for the social and political rights of the libyan people, free from the yoke of the imperialist military presence, deserves our full solidarity. the promotion of peace, sovereignty and independence in libya deserves our full support. these are the consequences of policies of undisguised colonial austerity! we trust that it will be the struggle of the peoples that will defeat them.
in writing. when the eu does not care about its neighbourhood or has other priorities to satisfy, someone else will tend to fill the gap. turkey, with its neo-ottoman expansionist moves, has set itself the goal of intervening in the eastern mediterranean and has found an ally in the collapsing regime of tripoli, which is cooperating with jihadist organizations such as the so-called "muslim brotherhood". it is turkey that has perpetuated the state of war in syria, resulting in the loss of thousands of lives and millions of refugees. today we have a repeat. it is the same conqueror for 46 years in the northern occupied territory of the republic of cyprus in the eu. the same lies. the same crimes. the same hypocrisy and expediency. i wish the voices of the children of kurdistan, in afrin, syria, and in cyprus and elsewhere could reach here. i am confident that the culture and principles of the eu will prevail in the end. i only regret that so many politicians are subjugated to the petty interests and sacrifice the greater good. the values of our culture.
in writing. – a main reason for the ongoing crisis in libya since its inception has been the interference of foreign powers which were – still are – following hidden agendas. among them feature member states of the eu. the hidden agendas include access to oil supplies; strategic interests in sub-saharan africa; occult financial operations, past and present; immigration towards europe; aspirations to achieve greater influence in the mediterranean space. foreign interference has increased in recent months. along the years, its has exacerbated the instability already inherent in a predominant feature of libyan society – its tribal organisation. one can see little prospects of peace in libya unless the hidden agendas are made explicit and aligned with the interests of the libyan people, only they – those living in the coastal cities and in the hinterland. formal conferences that try to bring ‘all sides’ together but retain the hidden agendas are quite likely destined to eventual failure. i for one hope that this will not happen. but if it does, and the current turmoil persists, then only one solution will remain that could over the medium term make sense, again in the interests of the libyan people – a military one that brings stability and internal security to the libyan territory as one whole.
the following is the text of the statement by the vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy on the situation in venezuela following the unlawful election of the national assembly's presidency and the presidency (2020/2507 ((rsp)).
vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – madam president, a few weeks ago this plenary discussed the venezuelan humanitarian migration crisis that has markedly worsened over the past year, because of the political deadlock in the country. unfortunately, we are here today to address a further deterioration of the political crisis. this is a result of the outrageous attacks against the constitutional and democratic functioning of the national assembly, and the takeover of legislative power by an illegitimate president. force and violence were used against president guaido and many lawmakers on 5 january to prevent the legitimate election of the national assembly executive board. impeding access to the national assembly is not a major attack against democracy and the rule of law, it is, but it also polarises further and creates more obstacles to a peaceful solution to the crisis. the so-called election of luis parra is not legitimate. it did not respect the legal procedures nor the democratic constitutional principles. there was no verified quorum, there was no voting majority and general legal provisions were not respected. any acts or decisions taken by the assembly over which luis parra presides cannot be recognised by the european union. the eu and the international contact group reacted swiftly and rejected the attacks against the national assembly, reiterating clear support for juan guaido as the legitimate president of the national assembly. many other international actors qualified the harassment acts as inadmissible and condemned this violation and expressed support for juan guaido. these developments require a strong response, and further restrictive measures targeting those responsible for undermining democracy are being considered. of course, these measures shall in no way harm the venezuelan people that are already dramatically affected by the crisis. targeted sanctions cannot be the only tool, we need other complementary constructive avenues that can contribute to a peaceful solution. even though the future looks bleak, and we don’t see prospects for a short-term resolution of the political impasse, we must continue trying to create political space for negotiations. we continue to believe that a sustainable resolution of the crisis can only arise from genuine negotiation that leads to an inclusive and representative negotiated electoral path. when we talk about restoring public institutions we mean in particular restoring the credibility of the national electoral council and the supreme court of justice. without them, there can be no free and credible legislative and presidential elections and no lasting solution for venezuela. we have said many times the eu will spare no efforts to continue helping a genuine and inclusive process towards a reinstatement of democracy and rule of law with free and fair presidential elections. another dimension of our efforts is to craft a more consultative approach to the venezuelan crisis by the international community. i intend to ask a special adviser iglesias – don’t mistake which about iglesias i’m talking about; i am talking about enrique iglesias – to make a tour to outreach to key international actors and assess what more can be done. as closing remarks, i will say that we will continue to follow closely developments, in contact with other key international actors. one thing is sure, we need more concerted efforts by the whole of the international community and we need them urgently. this is what i can summarise – what they wanted to tell to you – and i’m sure that the debates will go to these conclusions which i am anticipating. we need more concerted efforts by the whole of the international community and we need them urgently. we need them urgently. the stalemate cannot last forever, and that’s what we will try to do in the following days, looking for strong support from all actors involved and asking the contact group to continue working. the so-called oslo process is also in a stalemate, and the suffering of the venezuelan people and the political situation require that we push stronger in order to look for fair and free elections, as has been required for a long time without any success, but what has happened in the election of the presidency of the national assembly puts a marking point on the dynamics of a situation which requires from us a stronger commitment. we hope it’s going to happen in the next week.
on behalf of the ppe group madam president, mr high representative, the truth is that this debate, after having heard the previous one, makes me think that we are not on the same course of doing too little too late. usurper nicolás maduro attempted a desperate coup d'état with military support to prevent the only legitimate power in venezuela, its national assembly, from electing its authorities. that regime has shown its true intention, which is to prevent all attempts at a democratic transition and to remain legally in power. this parliament must ratify, once again, its support for juan guaidó, not only as the legitimate president in the national assembly but also as interim president in the republic of venezuela. more than 60 countries and institutions in the democratic world have already done so. we cannot forget the causes and the perpetrators of this venezuelan crisis, nor of this attempt to overthrow the national assembly. we continue to insist to the high representative on the urgent need to establish - as he himself suggested earlier - sanctions against those responsible for flagrant violations of the rule of law. individual sanctions, which do not affect the general population. let them serve so that the guilty do not go unpunished before their misdeeds and that the citizens, who are suffering the atrocities of the regime, receive justice. the worst sanction we can impose is to fail to act in the face of continuing human rights violations and to leave that people, in the midst of violence, in the grip of the worst kind of hunger and without a future.
on behalf of the s&d group. madam president, as the high representative, josep borrell, has indicated, we are here today to denounce what was an attempt at a parliamentary coup by the maduro regime's government. avoiding the proper functioning of venezuela's democratic assembly, an attempt was made to subtract legitimacy, creating parallel institutional realities, to prevent juan guaidó from continuing as president. moreover, it is a source of legitimacy for many countries in the international community, a huge majority of european countries, to place him as the interim president of the country. we want to remind you that we in the european union are playing a constructive role, sometimes very frustratingly, in a regime that is not finding democratic and peaceful solutions to the situation in a country, venezuela, which is unbearable, with a humanitarian situation, with a humanitarian crisis, without parallel. that is why we will continue to work in a clear place, such as venezuela, to propose individual sanctions and, at the same time, from a constructive point of view, also with the contact group, to find a democratic solution with free and fair elections in the country.
on behalf of the renew group. madam president, commissioner borrell, this resolution is more than a mere parliamentary debate or a statement by this house. this parliament, through this resolution, condemns, recognizes, questions and speaks at a key moment in the history of venezuela. condemns and denounces the tyrannical regime of nicolás maduro for attempting to orchestrate a parliamentary coup d'état to control the only democratic institution that evidences the farce of his regime. and it does so in a way that is absolutely intolerable, from harassment and violence to democratically elected and elected parliamentarians. recognizes and supports juan guaidó as the legitimate interim president of the republic of venezuela and the national assembly as its only chamber of democratic representation, while demanding respect for its deputies, denouncing the harassment and intolerable repression of the maduro regime. it called on the european union and the high commissioner to lead and step up efforts to restore democracy in venezuela, following the roadmap adopted by the national assembly and the various initiatives with the contact group. it called for the extension and intensification of sanctions against those responsible for human rights violations and repression. it calls on the member states that have not yet recognised juan guaidó as the legitimate president to do so. it also calls for its political representatives to be recognised as such. and finally, this resolution speaks directly and fraternally to the venezuelan people and says to them, with a strong and clear message, "you are not alone". this parliament demonstrates, through a large majority of its representatives - something that says that the different can agree on the defence of democracy - that it will work alongside you, alongside the venezuelan people, tirelessly, to return democracy to venezuela, initiating a process of political, economic and social reconstruction that will allow venezuela and venezuelans to look to the future with hope and hope.
on behalf of the ecr group. madam president, i am very pleased with the change of tone i have heard here, and which has also been noted in the broad agreement, which i think we are going to have, for the resolution on venezuela. finally, there are some who have decided that we really need to exert massive pressure on the criminal regime, this regime of communists and drug traffickers who have kidnapped venezuela, and who no longer hide anything, as they demonstrated in their assault on the national assembly. much firmer sanctions are urgent, sanctions for all those involved, maduro, padrino and so many others should not be able to dispose of a single dollar outside of venezuela. in spain there are leaders of the regime who flaunt their wealth. what is more, his links with the spanish communists, who are now in government, are alarming. we must put an end to this dictatorship because it is a danger to the region, because it kills its population every day and because it also has a power of corruption that is crossing the atlantic and is very dangerous.
it is curious that this parliament should be so intent on supporting an individual who has lost the support of even the majority of the opposition, which, not wanting to be represented by a corrupt coup d'étatist, has elected luis parra, another oppositionist, as president of the national assembly. guaidó knew he didn't have enough support and that's why, instead of entering through the door like a normal person, he decided to ride the little number and jump a fence like a goat. then, true to his coup tradition, he again proclaimed himself president, this time in the editorial office of a newspaper. some people here need to start assuming that their candidate is a fraud. let's remember that in addition to his obsession with proclaiming himself president of anything, he has also taken over the funds for humanitarian aid. and he continues to call for sanctions against his people, foreign military intervention and the lifting of the army. it is logical that guaidó is supported by those who call for a military uprising against the government of spain. for example, the honourable member who has preceded me. after all, they are heirs and nostalgic for the spanish fascist dictatorship. what is surprising is that members of this parliament with contrasting democratic concepts share speech and position with a cancer which until recently seemed to be eradicated from europe.
madam president, first of all, i would like to say to our colleagues from the far right that come to this chamber every day to tell us what the citizens think, believe, and to tell the rest of us that we are mistaken. but the truth is that you only represent 20 or 25% of the european people. it is the pro-european groups, the ones that support this resolution, that represent more than 70-75% of the vote in the last european election, so let’s set the record straight on this matter. mr president, let us not get carried away here today. this great conference on the future of the european union is intended either to give an opportunity to the opposition to the ever-increasing transfer of national sovereignty to the european union, a debate which is open and sincere and which is in tune with what has emerged in all elections in europe in recent years, namely less european union and more caution and freedom in european cooperation. either it is a forum, a theatrical performance like the great debate of macron where that resistance is actually set aside and made invisible. we fear that the latter, and the majority which is demanding a resolution here today has explicitly included in three places in the text, despite our amendments, that the eu should become, even more than today, in principle, an ever closer union. that is why i am voting against the report, because i believe that the commission has a duty to take account of the views of the european parliament. you should be ashamed of yourself.
madam president, those who are in charge of the european union, in order to continue to do what they have always done, must at least pretend to listen to what the people of europe are saying - that could well be the slogan of this farce. the same people who have approved treaties behind the backs of the citizens, the same people who have banned referendums when the results have become inconvenient, the same people who have disregarded the result of referendums, now seem very interested in hearing the opinion of the citizens in a process shaped by conclusions drawn from the outset. we are here to discuss the future of europe, yes, and that is why we reject this farce. we are here to discuss the future of europe, but without any staging which is intended to hide conclusions drawn from the outset. we are here above all to fight for another europe, not the neoliberal europe of the multinationals and the economic groups, but the europe of the workers and the peoples. not a militarized europe, but a europe of peace. not a europe of the imposition of the more powerful on the less powerful, but a europe of sovereign states with equal rights. we are here to discuss europe, and above all we are here to transform europe.
madam president, when we ask ourselves about the future of europe, the first thing to solve is what role we should give to citizens. if we don’t solve this, in a few years, with other meps, the same debate will have to take place and it will be a defeat. do we want citizens to be uninterested, passive and distant or, on the contrary, do we want their voice to really count and for them to be able to participate directly in european democracy? i don’t refer only to the elections of members and senior positions, but to habilitate a mechanism in order to participate in debates and decision—taking. we must update the governance of our democracy. the dissatisfaction that runs through europe only benefits europhobic populism. there is a real basis that justifies this dissatisfaction. the alternative cannot be the temptation of euroscepticism. the future of europe is not for conformists or sceptics, but exigencies. president, ten years, president, ten years since the entry into force of the treaty of lisbon and what we have had is a financial crisis, an immigration crisis, the brexit crisis. we haven't been able to solve any of these problems. it is therefore right that we should discuss the future of the union, but beware, because it seems that the solution is to continue on the path that has led to it, which is the federalisation of europe. you keep walking towards the precipice. in my view, the solution must be a free conference in which the views of not more than 20% of this parliament, which has been excluded directly from the wording of the proposal which is being presented to the house, are not excluded. is that the future of europe you want? the future of a europe which begins by sanctioning two member states and which continues to exclude from the debate - i repeat - more than 20% of the european population. there are other europeans who want a europe of free nations, of sovereign nations, cooperating freely in defence of their traditions. and i want to remind you also of something that you seem to forget: the future depends on our traditions and respect for our history, our christian culture. madam president, the truth is that i think this debate is timely because there has been an attempt to falsify history and unfortunately this happens very often. this morning in the house, mrs widdecombe of the brexit party said that the original project of the european union was a free trade association and that if we had not made progress on integration, the united kingdom would still be with us. well, that's a clear and present lie. the project of european integration has been supranational from the outset, and the schuman declaration, back in 1950, said that it was the first step towards a federation for europe. but this debate also highlights that when we look at history from our national perspective, we always see the atrocities committed by others and tend to obscure those committed by our ancestors. that is why we need a shared european history, one that highlights the tragedies and the good things that we have done as europeans.

madam president, commissioner, i have to ask you to stop. in september 2019, on the 80th anniversary of the outbreak of the second world war, the european parliament adopted a resolution clearly indicating that the non-aggression pact signed on 23 august 1939 by nazi germany and the soviet union, known as the molotov-ribbentrop pact, and its secret additional protocol, in which the two totalitarian regimes divided europe and the territories of independent states into their zones of interest, was the direct cause of the outbreak of that war. as a result of this pact, poland was attacked by germany on september 1, 1939, and on september 17, the soviet union also attacked other countries in central and eastern europe. in response to this resolution, the current president of the russian federation, vladimir putin, has repeatedly questioned these historical facts in public statements, trying to blame my country, poland, for the outbreak of the second world war. the european parliament should once again condemn russia's attempts to falsify history and cover up the crimes committed by the soviet totalitarian regime and commit the commission to firmly countering such efforts.

on behalf of the renew group. – madam president, during the financial crisis, we learned that the excessive closeness between the regulators and the regulated – the banks, the financial sector – can have a real cost to taxpayers. we’re not talking about vague ethical standards without implications, we’re talking about rules that actually matter to the taxpayers. why? because if those who are setting the rules are actually not setting the rules for the taxpayers, but they are setting the rules for the system, because they’re hoping to go back to the financial system, etc., what we end up is with rules that are actually not working, with rules that lead to bailouts and with rules that lead to taxpayers footing the bill. the favourite bet of investors is one where heads they win, tails the taxpayers lose. that’s why this case is so important, and that’s why parliament is sending a very loud and clear message. i congratulate my colleagues from all the four main parties – mr tang, ms tingali, chair of the committee, mr ferber, mr giegold – on coming together and passing already a resolution on this case unanimously, the entire parliament. we are continuing working on building and maintaining these boundaries between regulators and those who are regulated. this case goes beyond any conception of those boundaries. it basically makes a laughing stock of those staff regulations you mentioned, mr commissioner, and it basically means, well you know, if you want to ignore those rules and go around them, then you may. that’s why parliament has taken such a decisive action. we don’t take this this problem lightly. we will be policing and monitoring in the future that the staff regulations are maintained and enforced. that ethical body you proposed, mr commissioner, seems like a good idea. anything that can make this work, this prohibition and these boundaries between the two sides of the wall work, and we think that that’s to the benefit of our economies, our citizens our taxpayers.

mr president, commissioner, we are of course pleased that the priorities of the commission's work programme for 2020 include the entire green deal, given the climate emergency declared by this parliament itself. but we believe that the commission should also focus more on its work on external action, on how we lead and defend international humanitarian law, on how we further strengthen development cooperation and humanitarian aid. we know that the countries that have contributed the least to global warming and pollution are the countries that are suffering the most from the effects of climate change and the effects of this climate emergency. other regions of the world have already made progress on how to protect people who are forced to leave their places of origin because of the climate, for example through the kampala convention or the cartagena declaration in latin american countries. we ask you, commissioner, to make progress in this work programme on how to define and protect people displaced by climate change and to indicate how to move forward with european protection of the figure of the climate refugee.
in writing. i welcome the inclusion in the commission's work programme of an action plan for roma social inclusion. it is important that both the croatian and german presidencies play an active role in the development of the pan-european roma strategy, as the situation of roma is a concern in almost all member states. i consider it my own priority to represent the interests of the roma, the disadvantaged and those living in extreme poverty, and i am ready to contribute my experience to the work of the commission and the council to develop a complex, comprehensive programme. i want to avoid the bad practice of 2010 where my proposals are misinterpreted by the institutions. i ask the commission and the council to keep in touch. the stakes are high: we must eliminate the harmful and ineffective programs that have been in place. we need to involve local actors in every process from planning to control, to eliminate corruption, overcharging and redundant bureaucracy. and the commission's new green deal is a guide to how we can create an alternative and sustainable development model for those living in extreme poverty through green economy projects involving renewable energy.
in writing. i welcome the fact that the commission takes the issue of tax crimes seriously. the money that is being embezzled by criminals, or so the people involved in this practice are called, is being taken from the citizens of individual european union countries. the budget cuts mean less good things for health, safety, technological development and a decent life for all europeans. finally, the reduced national budgets also risk putting pressure on the community budget. in 2017, €137 billion was leaked from vat alone. these measures could help address many of the problems we face. there is great potential for coordination between countries at eu level. i would like to take this opportunity to emphasise that poland has done a great job of closing the vat gap in just a few years, and has used the funds raised for, among other things, successful social programmes. does the commission draw on existing and well-functioning solutions when proposing new legislation? inventing the wheel a second time will be a waste of time and money. finally, i would like to stress the tremendous importance of the rule of law in the area of taxation. according to the treaties, tax proposals must be unanimously adopted by the member states. attempts to creatively circumvent this requirement will undermine the commission's credibility and strike at the very foundations of how our community operates.
in writing. in order to eradicate energy poverty, which affects 50 million europeans, we call on the commission to propose an action plan with clear targets and directions and to take into account in the impact studies the effects of new legislative proposals on the fight against energy poverty. i also call for the future industrial policy to contain bold measures to support our industrial base, ensure the competitiveness of european industry, and create and encourage european champions. unfortunately, i have not seen any mention in the commission's work programme of the carbon border adjustment mechanism, which is important both for european industry and as a source of new funds. i welcome the fact that in the commission's proposal for the just transition fund, romania is allocated the sum of eur 757 million, or 10% of the eur 7,5 billion for the period 2021-2027. i want you to ensure that the most affected regions and member states get the maximum benefit from the just transition mechanism, to have economic growth and create sustainable jobs. last but not least, because research and innovation are key to delivering on the scale of the ambitions we set ourselves, i argue that a budget of €120 billion for horizon europe is necessary.
in writing. the ambitions of the programme do not meet the needs of the citizens. the green deal proposal is a travesty and a marketing ploy that will not lead to any real results. without a mea culpa from the eu, on its failed policies, it is useless to continue to discuss reforms that are rooted in the ideological approach alone. in its disastrous method, the commission also insists on foreign policy: it is not enough to be intellectually honest to observe that europe continues to show its fragility in the international context. the conference on the future of europe is also in the dark, and it wants to involve the citizens in a process of alleged change, but the main actors will be the same ones who have brought the european project to the precarious stage we are now in. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i regret that the new commission programme does not provide answers to the questions which concern european citizens: the commission has not yet taken any concrete steps on immigration, internal security and the revival of the european economy. again, this program, it's just a name.
the work programme submitted by the european commission for 2020 is in line with the programme approved by the european parliament for the mandate. the priorities reflect the objective of affirming the european union through decarbonisation, growth and convergence internally and affirming its values externally. the programme reflects a positive development in the conceptualisation of the european green deal, notably through a better articulation of the digital and energy transitions, as well as the inclusion of a social dimension, still insufficient but stronger. i also stress the concern for the robustness of democracy and the commitment to concrete measures and to the mobilisation of citizens through active participation in the conference on the future of europe. the credibility and trust generated by the work programme is, however, clearly undermined by the continued vagueness regarding the financing mechanisms in general and the multiannual financial framework in particular. the key to the success or failure of this programme lies less in the ability of the commission or the support of parliament than in the willingness of the council to take a structural and ambitious stance in the allocation of the financial resources which make the work programme viable.
the following is the debate on the commission statement on the 'eu response to the devastating floods in spain' (2020/2537 ((rsp)). i ask my colleagues to continue to observe the time strictly so that we can proceed to the votes at the scheduled time.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, i think that the topic we are going to discuss now is just clear testimony to the fact that climate change is real, and its impacts are sometimes too real, too dramatic and too devastating. extreme weather events are happening more and more frequently and with greater intensity. drought and forest fires are no longer confined to the mediterranean, tropical hurricanes travel up to the coast of ireland and heavy rain and flash floods occur across the whole of europe. the recent flooding disaster in spain caused by storm gloria is yet another example. our thoughts are with the people of spain suffering from this new disaster. in particular, our deepest condolences go to all those who lost loved ones. europe stands with the people of spain. we have a tool to provide assistance once serious natural disasters have occurred. the eu solidarity fund intervenes to assist the population with the recovery of essential infrastructure, with cleaning—up operations and with the safeguarding of the cultural heritage. however, the solidarity fund is no emergency instrument and it takes some time to mobilise it. most importantly, the european parliament and the council must approve the activation of the fund and the budget resources once the commission has assessed the application from the member state. once immediate assistance to the population in response to the disaster is under control, the spanish authorities should assess the damage caused to see whether it succeeds the relevant threshold of 1.5 percentage points of the regional gdp. this is the essential condition for a successful solidarity fund application. the spanish authorities are well familiar with the conditions and procedures of the fund, but of course, the commission services stand ready to provide further guidance and assistance as required. we are currently in the process of assessing an earlier spanish application received in november, which includes the autonomous region of valencia, murcia, castilla-la mancha and andalusia for the severe weather last september. spain provided an important information update on this disaster only this week, and an advance payment of eur 5.7 million is on its way, but in the future, we know that we must be prepared to do more. what happened in spain is, unfortunately, a foretaste of what is to come as a climate change continues. the eu solidarity fund brings welcome support after disasters have happened, but we must be more proactive. this is why in recent years, the commissioner has been putting an increasing focus on prevention, climate adaptation and the risk management policies. for cohesion policy, disaster risk management is vital because local and regional authorities are the first to face the impacts of disasters because it is a cost—effective investment in preventing future losses and also because it contributes to sustainable development. in this period, 2014—2020 cohesion policy is investing almost eur 8 billion in climate change adaptation and risk prevention. this includes a broad array of support. floods are the main risk addressed, followed by coastal erosions, forest fires, earthquakes and droughts. prevention is the main focus of our investment. actions to improve the modelling of risk, early-warning systems, preparation of strategies and guidelines, awareness raising, grey or green infrastructure for flood protection, sustainable land and forest management, et cetera. in this period, we have also seen a large focus on green infrastructure to manage risk, like flood plains and green urban spaces. the commission also provides guidelines and it encourages member states to compile national risk assessments, which are an essential and necessary tool to fight the impact of floods. besides prevention, we also focus on preparedness with support for equipment, infrastructure and training for civil protection units. european money also supports the development and improvement of the eu early-warning and information systems. fed by a network of sensors and satellites, they provide reliable information about the development of extreme meteorological events, improving the prediction of the impact of events such as floods. these systems also enable the emergency—response community to monitor and share reliable information, to prepare to respond and to better coordinate response efforts. finally, we offer flexibility in our cohesion programmes. programme amendments can be proposed and then swiftly agreed to shift resources towards areas that are most at need, so if changes need to be made, we can still do this within this funding period. the commission’s proposals for cohesion policy after 2020 include disaster risk management and climate adaptation as specific objectives. prevention and preparedness measures include infrastructure, equipment shelters, development of early—warning systems and training for civil protection units. where appropriate, these measures cross borders.
on behalf of the ppe group mr. president, i cannot begin my speech without remembering all the people who have lost their lives to the storm, their families and loved ones. to the missing, the wounded, all of you, i extend our support and affection. spain has suffered the terrible consequences of the gloria storm, but a few months ago also of dana, where so many neighbours have seen their houses, their cars, their streets, their businesses, their farms, their fields, their orchards, destroyed. we have suffered great natural and economic losses in places necessary for our ecosystem and economy, such as the ebro delta - the only place in europe and the third largest producer of rice in spain - where the growth of the sea has caused, in addition to environmental damage, the disappearance of crops worth more than four million euros; or in places such as the valencian community, where citrus, agricultural infrastructure, seasonal vegetables and livestock have suffered losses of up to 63 million euros; or as the balearic islands, which has lost its beaches, and so many spanish regions affected, such as andalusia, murcia, castilla-la mancha or aragon. the spanish people affected need a quick and effective response from the spanish government. we demand that the spanish government evaluate the affected areas quickly, speed up aid, apply for the european union's solidarity fund and declare the affected municipalities as disaster areas. the european union cannot be left behind. it should therefore activate the solidarity fund and the cohesion fund. it must work for a bigger budget in the new cap to have more resources to finance risk prevention for our farmers and livestock farmers. an ambitious green deal, which protects our environment against the effects of climate change, which invests in improving water infrastructure and protecting and cleaning our forests and rivers to prevent future weather events. europe cannot look the other way and must act. the spanish people who have suffered from the storm cannot wait another day, and the partido popular will not rest until this aid reaches those affected. we will always be with them and never leave them behind.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, in just a few months of this parliament's term, this is the third natural disaster we have debated in the european parliament. and i very much fear that it will not be the last because, certainly, climate change is an unmistakable reality, evidenced in storms like the ones we have suffered in spain. in fact, according to the european commission's own figures, more than 500 000 people in the european union are exposed to these phenomena, to a certain risk of river flooding, and 2.2 million people, also in the european union, are at risk of being affected by coastal flooding. so this is not a problem of today, of spain, but it is a problem of all of us. and since it's a problem for all of us, we need to start taking it seriously. and that's why it's very important that we start implementing measures to prevent it. but it is also very important that the solidarity fund should be implemented immediately. we need it to show that this too is cohesion and that our country, like any other country at any time, can get the support of the european union to compensate for all the damage caused by this terrible storm. there are things that you're not going to be able to recover, like human lives, unfortunately. but europe can also stand by us so that we do not leave behind all the families and sectors that have been affected by this storm. i would like to thank you, mr vice-president, for the speed and flexibility with which you have implemented this fund.
on behalf of the renew group. mr president, thank you for your words. hurricane gloria has hit spain hard: fourteen dead, eighty-three injured, two missing. the balance is devastating. there are municipalities in catalonia, the valencian community, the balearic islands, murcia and aragon which have suffered losses of millions in infrastructure and in the countryside. waterways, beaches, citrus crops have been destroyed in regions that depend on agriculture and tourism. and the worst part is that this is not an isolated phenomenon; we have just suffered the consequences of aids, and experts warn us that these phenomena are going to increase. that is why i would like to ask the commission here today to come forward with effective proposals. i propose three actions. first of all, the solidarity fund. we must activate it, but also, if these phenomena are increasing, we need to increase the budget of the solidarity fund so that all the people who suffer from these tragedies are covered. secondly, improving the procedures. we cannot go through long administrative procedures, and in spain our farmers are still waiting for the aid from dana. and thirdly, improving systems for preventing natural disasters and adapting to climate change at all levels. it's critical to saving lives. in short, let us put a value on one of the fundamental values of the european union, solidarity, because we need it now more than ever.
on behalf of the green group, mr president, last week the storm gloria had devastating effects in catalonia and the rest of the state. thirteen people lost their lives. this climate phenomenon has had an unprecedented ecological and agricultural impact and is a clear consequence of the effects of climate change. the gloria pass has washed away a large number of beaches and infrastructure along the coast, and caused serious damage to the ebro delta and the tordera and ter river basins. according to initial estimates, the impact of the damage amounts to, for example, 74 million in the barcelona area alone, or 16 million in the catalan primary sector. in view of the need for a rapid response, we call on the european commission and the spanish government to take three actions: first, to activate urgently the resources of the european regional development fund to co-finance the repairs of the damage; secondly, as you have rightly said, the solidarity fund; but, finally, also to draw up a specific action plan for the mediterranean coast and the delta, in line with the declaration on the protection of the ebro delta adopted yesterday by the catalan government and local actors.
the european union is also concerned about the situation in the mediterranean, and the situation in the mediterranean is particularly serious. we are still mourning the loss of 14 lives and the wounding of dozens. gloria has caused devastating effects on infrastructure, street furniture, beaches, crops, businesses and homes; damages aggravated - which also has to be said - by policies of ball-point penetration and urban speculation by irresponsible and corrupt governments that have done so much damage to the coastline. climate change is already having very visible consequences. this phenomenon is affecting our entire country very intensely, which during 2019 has suffered severe floods and floods throughout the territory, also in andalusia, euskadi or navarra. it is too early to have a global assessment of the damage, valued in millions of dollars, that will be inflicted on our economy and our people. today we are asking this parliament for support to activate the solidarity fund for disasters; for a change in the criteria for the funds so that individual country cases are not collected, but the annual global number of cases due to the same cause: climate change; for stronger measures against urban corruption and a determined commitment to implementing policies against the climate emergency. let's not wait for the next catastrophe to act. we're doing well in it.
mr president, the storm gloria must be a call, once again, for action by the european union. the effects of climate change are beginning to affect the spanish mediterranean. in october 2018, a very intense storm gave the first alarm signal in my region, specifically in sant llorenç des cassadar, in mallorca. also, in october of this year, a dana swept through the region and last week the storm gloria struck the balearic islands, catalonia and valencia, causing fourteen deaths. and in the balearic islands, two people are still missing, whose families i wish to extend my warmest sympathy from this parliament. all indications are that these intense storms will become more frequent. the regions most affected are therefore asking for help, both to recover from the effects and to adapt to the new reality. the solidarity fund should have mechanisms in place to mobilise resources urgently for the affected regions. in the balearic islands, the consequences of the storm have been twofold. in addition to the material and human damage, the food supply has been disrupted. and this is another reason why the european union should take island status seriously as a disadvantage suffered by the citizens of our islands.
mr president, i wanted to express my solidarity with all the victims of the gloria storm that has struck with extreme virulence the east coast of my nation, spain. unfortunately, there have been fatalities and considerable material losses, especially in the agricultural sector. last september, as other colleagues have already said, there was another storm called dana which caused further severe damage to our agriculture. unfortunately, farmers are still not receiving the aid they were promised. the new gloria storm has done nothing but greatly harm these citrus, vegetable, livestock farmers, who are absolutely desperate with this situation. i am grateful for the european commission's solidarity, but i would like to stress the need for a series of quick, forceful and effective responses from what i would call three instruments: the cap, the cohesion funds and the european union solidarity funds.
in writing. despite the deniers, the storm gloria passed through spain, with heavy rains and snowfall that left thousands of people isolated in my region, aragon. now, melting ice threatens many populations because of the flooding of rivers. nearly 4,000 km of roads and 142 municipalities in the provinces of teruel and zaragoza have been affected, leaving thousands of people without electricity, water, internet or coverage. there has also been damage to buildings and ships, and severe losses in the countryside and livestock. cooperation between the central and regional governments has been essential and, together with the security forces, the ume, the regional emergency services, town halls and neighbours are working to respond to the exceptional situation. this further highlights the need for the european institutions to work together with national, regional and local authorities for territorial and socioeconomic cohesion and to find solutions to the challenges of rural and mountain areas, which urgently need improved transport and telecommunications infrastructures. let us work together to mitigate the effects of climate change in the less-favoured areas.
