i am sure that the commission will be able to support the amendments tabled by the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection. and that is also why our group has considered today to raise the objection to projects of common interest, because we believe that in some cases they are not justified either economically or environmentally. one example is the bay of biscay interconnection project. a project of enormous economic cost that only guarantees the profit of the lobbies of the oligopoly, and that in no case will solve the energy problem of our country. i therefore ask you to take up this spirit, this common sense of the young people who are fighting in the streets, and to ensure that public money is allocated to projects that serve to develop our territories in a sustainable way.
mr president, commissioner simson, this whole debate can be avoided if the next list is really consulted and planned with this parliament. it is also sometimes striking to see how countries that do not meet their 2020 renewable targets want to give lessons to those who have, who are, and want to tell us how to do things in the future. the renewables columnists can't say that ditching coal is that simple and that we can't use gas. so we need to plan for that future. over 60 percent of our final consumption is fossil fuels, and that's what we need to change in the next generation. and we have to do it fairly, avoiding frustration in all directions. we need to move forward in renewable energy, electrification, smart grid development, sector coupling and, in addition, offshore wind connections. do we achieve any of those goals by rejecting the fourth list and going back to the third? my answer is no. we're not going to have more electricity projects, we're going to have fewer. we are not going to guarantee more smart grid projects, but less. it's not about guaranteeing fewer gas projects, it's about putting more pipelines. therefore, we do not convey misleading messages to the citizens. it is necessary to align with the european union's climate objectives, guaranteeing energy security to the member states and, in addition, working towards neutrality with ambition but also with rigor. decarbonizing our economy is a process that we all have to do together.
mr president, this is the legislature of the european green deal and also of the just ecological transition. there is political commitment, there is agreement, but, ladies and gentlemen, on this point i wanted to reflect in the house: there must also be resources. the speeches are very important, they are essential, but we have to reinforce them with resources. i would like to draw attention to a number of points. the sustainable investment plan will need more funds; the just transition fund will need more funds. and, in addition, both, greater clarity and precision in their functions and structures. the change in the production and economic model, for a truly sustainable change to take place, will require a tax reform. so, ladies and gentlemen, we now know the challenges and the objectives, but we need more resources, more funds, more precision, more clarity and, above all, a new environmental taxation.
mr president, the european parliament is the house of freedom and that is why i invited eight leaders of the cuban opposition last week to come to this house to explain the excesses that the dictator diaz-canel is practicing and executing, in short, in cuba. but the problem is, when you deal with dictatorships, dictators emerge. and so it came to pass that díaz-canel and his regime prevented the departure of five cuban opponents. they could not prevent three of those opponents, because they live outside cuba, because they live in exile, from being in this house, in the european parliament. but, despite this, a shameful situation occurred and that is that the regime infiltrated this house to a number of people to harass and to verbally attack the opponents. and the most shameful thing of all is that these people were able to enter this house as a result of the fact that the gue/ngl parliamentary group - those who are podemos in spain - allowed them to enter precisely to boycott, to break up our act. in short, mr diaz-canel should know, dictators should know, that as long as this kind of action against human and civil rights exists, this parliament - (the president withdrew the floor from the speaker) - will continue to be the arbiter of human and civil rights.

good morning, colleagues. i greet commission president von der leyen and the minister in the presidency. the agenda includes the debate on the statements by the council and the commission on the proposed mandate for negotiations on a new partnership with the united kingdom of great britain and northern ireland (2020/2557 ((rsp)).
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, following the entry into force of the withdrawal agreement and the actual withdrawal of the united kingdom, we are about to embark on the new phase of negotiations. in order to do so, we obviously need to give a mandate to the union negotiator. such a mandate should be put on sound foundations. i would like in this respect to set out a few considerations that should guide us when preparing this mandate. first of all, given the comprehensive mandate called for by the european council, the scope of the negotiations is very broad. we cannot therefore afford to delay their start. an early start calls for an early adoption of the mandate. we intend to do that at the general affairs council on 25 february. this time constraint is made all the more stringent by prime minister johnson’s decision not to extend the transition period beyond the end of 2020. given the broad scope of the mandate, this will require that several strands of negotiations have to proceed in parallel. this is a negotiating mandate, not the final outcome of the negotiations. therefore, it cannot spell out in full detail our expected landing zones, our final offers and the trade-offs we may have to contemplate. adequate negotiating space should be preserved. besides – and this goes back to my point about unity and time constraints – a very prescriptive mandate would require more time to resolve possibility, diverging interests and priorities. what we should focus on is the union’s interest in achieving an outcome that is fair and equitable for all member states and their citizens. last, we have to factor in the uk red lines. the uk government’s intention is to discontinue free movement, with an obvious impact on mobility and citizens’ rights, and also to diverge from the union’s regulatory framework with no less obvious consequences in terms of access to the single market and trade in general. on the substance of the mandate, regarding the various sectors that should be covered, i think that we largely share the view in the parliament’s position, as reflected in your draft resolution. this means a fair degree of ambition as compared to the union’s relationship with other countries. it should be clear, however, that this cannot amount to giving the uk the same benefit as a member state, especially in the light of its red lines. but let me focus briefly on more horizontal aspects of the partnership: governance and level playing field. there should be no doubt here. the eu might be prepared to offer an ambitious free trade agreement with significant market access, but this cannot be to the detriment of the competitive position of eu companies, social and health standards, or state aid rules. in other words, the partnership should include wide ranging provisions on a level playing field, based on high standards. this is all the more relevant given the uk’s stated intention to diverge from the european union. in my view, this is closely linked to the issue of effective dispute settlement enforcement and remedies. there is no point in developing wide ranging level playing field provisions if they cannot be enforced. i will make two important remarks before closing. first, although we are well aware that the line of the uk’s government is to put an end to the free movement of persons, we should see to it that the future relationship that we will start to negotiate shortly will be ambitious enough in terms of mobility. second, and this is a link to my previous remark, these negotiations will keep both our institutions and the commission busy for quite some time, but this should not make us forget about the withdrawal agreement. we should continue to monitor its thorough implementation. there is therefore once again a shared interest all along the coming negotiations in pursuing the good cooperation that we expected during the brexit phase.
president of the commission. – mr president, it’s just two weeks ago that we bid farewell to our british friends by singing ‘auld lang syne’, and i think it was a most emotional, a very powerful moment in this parliament: a moment to celebrate the good old times, and i thank you very much for the grace and the kindness of this gesture. this was extraordinary. since then, we’ve set our sights on the future of our relations with the united kingdom, and we will enter these negotiations with the highest ambition. because good old friends like the uk and us shouldn’t settle for less than this. prime minister johnson said in greenwich earlier this month that the united kingdom will, i quote, ‘be a global champion of free trade’. frankly, this is music to our ears, because at a moment when the rules-based trade system is so challenged, we need our partners to join us in making the system fairer and stronger. and this is what we europeans have always fought for over the years: a trade system that is open on one side and that is fair on the other side. because what do free trade agreements do? free trade agreements must replace uncertainty with a sound set of rules. they create new markets for small and medium enterprises. free trade agreements must benefit the people. and this is just the rationale that is behind our trade agreements, for instance with canada and japan. they are not just increasing our bilateral exchanges of goods, services, people and ideas. they do that too, but not only. they also raise standards on a broad range of issues, from labour rights to environmental. and this is what makes us proud of them: ask our japanese friends or ask our canadian friends. they are glad that we have joined forces to put fairness into our globalised economic system. they are glad that they could join forces with the european union, because frankly, in today’s world, size does matter – and we have a single market of 440 million people. what i just described – this is the ambition we have for our free trade agreement with the united kingdom. and when we agreed the political declaration with the united kingdom, we ambitioned zero tariffs and a zero-quota trade relation for all goods: something we have never ever before offered to anybody else. a new model of trade, a unique ambition in terms of access to the single market. but of course, this would require corresponding guarantees on fair competition and the protection of social, environmental and consumer standards. in short: this is plain and simply the level playing field. we are ready to discuss all different models of trade agreement. but all these models, whatever you choose, have one thing in common: they all come not only with rights, but also with obligations for both sides. for example, if we take the canada model – and this is a model prime minister johnson referred to – of course, our deal with canada eliminates tariffs on a wide set of goods, but not on all. and of course, our deal with canada eliminates most quotas, but certainly not all. for instance, there are still quotas on beef and sweetcorn. and of course, we still have our standards that have to be respected. and honestly, i was a little bit surprised to hear the prime minister of the united kingdom speak about the australian model. australia, without any doubt, is a strong and like-minded partner. but the european union does not have a trade agreement with australia. we are currently trading on wto terms. and if this is the british choice, well, we are fine with that, without any question. but in fact, we are just in the moment where we are agreeing with australia that we must end this situation, and we work on a trade deal with them. of course, the uk can decide to settle for less, but i personally believe that we should be way more ambitious. and the prime minister’s speech in greenwich is an encouraging starting point. he recalled everything the united kingdom has achieved in terms of social protection, climate action, competition rules, and i commend the uk for all of that. indeed, it is not the time to lower social protection or to be lukewarm on climate action. it is not the time to decrease in terms of competition rules. i have heard ambition in boris johnson’s speech: ambition on the minimum wage, ambition on parental payments, and he has an ally in me where that is concerned. i have heard ambition on cutting carbon emissions. ambition on guaranteeing that our firms are competing in full fairness. this is what we also want. let us formally agree on these objectives. we can trigger an upward dynamic competition that would benefit both the united kingdom and the european union. to our british friends i say: it’s in our mutual interest. and most importantly, it would be consistent with the values we share – values of openness, values of fairness, values of social justice and free enterprise. these are not only values for the good old times. these are values to stay. thank you very much for your attention. i just wanted to inform you that the task force is ready to start the negotiations. we’re all set. (applause)
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the resolution on the draft mandate, which we are debating this morning, is the first proof of work of the newly created uk coordination group. as the chair, i would like to thank all members of our group, as well as the involved committees and the political groups, for their contributions in an extremely short period of time. in my opinion, the result is a very good and balanced resolution only nine days after michel barnier presented the commission’s draft mandate. our resolution contains parliament’s views on the content and on the architecture of a future eu-uk relationship. let me highlight three main principles of particular importance. firstly, the integrity and the correct functioning of our internal market, the customs union and the four freedoms must be maintained. secondly, a third country cannot have the same rights and benefits as a member state of the eu. and thirdly, a level playing field is an overall priority and precondition for any future agreement. we should not, and we will not, enter a race to the bottom. as ursula von der leyen has pointed out, together with our british friends, we should aim for a partnership that goes well beyond trade and is unprecedented in scope: everything from climate action to data protection, fisheries to energy, transport to space, financial services to security. we are indeed ready to design a new partnership with zero tariffs, zero quotas and zero dumping. however a free trade agreement with the uk will not be equivalent to frictionless trade. ladies and gentlemen, besides the negotiations on the future relations, the implementation of the withdrawal agreement is of particular importance. this concerns especially citizens’ rights, financial duties on the protocol on ireland-northern ireland. our chief negotiator michel barnier, whose work is highly appreciated across party lines in the institutions, has already assured us in the uk coordination group’s first meeting to keep parliament fully informed at all stages of the procedure. (applause)
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, on behalf of the socialist group we would like to thank the european commission, and in particular mr barnier's team, for their efforts in drawing up the guidelines for the negotiation of the future framework for the relationship between the european union and the united kingdom. with the resolution which this parliament is about to adopt, we are sending a strong message together: the ambition of the future relationship will depend on the willingness of the british government to accept conformity with community rules on social, labour, taxation and the environment. on 1 february the united kingdom formally became a third country and lost all political rights. but in all other respects nothing will change until this transitional period is over. we have 11 months to negotiate a complex agreement that defines our future relationship. in building this new partnership we must assume that nothing will be as prosperous as the rights and benefits of membership of the european union. the socialist group is committed to reaching a comprehensive agreement with the united kingdom. however, the more barriers the british government raises, the more difficult it will be to achieve an ambitious agreement. on the contrary, if the united kingdom is willing to maintain a level playing field as a fair competitor, we will be in a position to offer a partnership which will ensure the prosperity of citizens on both sides of the channel. and to ensure this prosperity, we are prepared to offer an ambitious free trade agreement, with no tariffs or quotas, for all goods entering the common market and another for services. moreover, if we want this benefit to be real, we also need a strong partnership in judicial cooperation, security and defence, in support of the multilateral order, and in tackling issues such as fisheries, transport, energy and financial services. but our offer cannot be made at any price. let me be clear. the uk must ensure fair competition, allow european fleets access to its waters and maintain its commitment to the european court of human rights in respect of compliance with its judgments. we will also demand that spain has the final say in the discussions between the european union and the united kingdom concerning gibraltar, as has been confirmed in the commission's guidelines. our success in the negotiations will depend on our being able to make prevail what has been our best weapon so far: unity. only with the commission as the sole interlocutor with the united kingdom, and with the full involvement of this parliament for final approval, will the european union be able to preserve the level of unity achieved so far. together we have succeeded in protecting the integrity of the single market, the customs union and the four freedoms - including the free movement of citizens. let us not forget that our project is based on a commitment to values which are the exact opposite of those expressed by some british leaders, imbued with nationalism and a false idea of superiority. as we face the future relationship, we have an obligation to defend the democratic principles and fundamental freedoms that have allowed our european project to go forward.
for the greens/efa group mr president, ladies and gentlemen, two facts: the first is that the united kingdom and the european union are bound by geography. that's not likely to change, so we'd better keep our relationships as close and friendly as possible. secondly, not everyone in the uk is a british nationalist. the european union has many, many friends in the united kingdom. we don't alienate them. in trade matters, the ambitions of the european union and the united kingdom are antagonistic, since the united kingdom wants maximum access to the european market, but refuses the regulatory alignment that the european union requires. so i would urge all of us to be creative in this regard. let me explain: as a general rule, free trade agreements are used by business circles, i.e. capital holders, to force a bottom-up leveling of working and wage conditions, social, environmental or tax standards. we could use the opportunity of a free trade agreement with the united kingdom to do the exact opposite. today, the laws of the united kingdom and the european union are perfectly aligned in areas of european union competence, but not in all. in some areas - i am thinking of privacy, i am thinking of course of social standards - the european union has more ambitious standards than the uk. but there are other areas where the uk has higher standards than the eu for certain aspects of environmental policy, or for banking regulation. we could put a rule in the new agreement whereby the two partners would commit themselves to apply the one of the two standards which is the more ambitious and by the more ambitious i mean the more protective of health, privacy, working conditions and pay, the environment. this would be a form of upward emulation between the uk and the eu. you're gonna tell me that never happened. well, i think that this is the kind of free trade agreement that many of our fellow citizens want today, in other words, in places of the one we will soon vote with vietnam.
on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, the eu and britain have entered a tense year. time is limited and pressure is mounting to agree on an fta this year. formally, the negotiation is aimed at agreeing to a legal set of rules, but there is more to it. there is a mindset that varies on both sides of the channel. every bureaucracy tends to cling to its own rules, awarding them holy status. in that respect the eu bureaucracy is not much different from the mandarins that once ruled the chinese empire. it regards itself as the centre of the world, and of all wisdom. the british mindset is different. having been an empire itself, britain derives cultural self-confidence from the idea that it is still is an empire. the old empire does not want to be run by a possibly new one, so it left. what are the lessons, mr president? the eu should not be bitter. brexit is done and we have to put our citizens first. the eu has to be pragmatic and purpose-driven rather than rules—obsessed; focussed on tangible mutual interests, not on bad feelings. the current british government is the most stable in western europe – a big majority for five years. who else can say that? but it should not overestimate the margins of divide and rule that worked for centuries – but not anymore. results will only be attainable through political will: the will to succeed. this – though wordy – resolution is the starting point to negotiate. mr barnier, you are an experienced negotiator. i hope you will be able to bridge both mindsets – divided by 30 kilometres of water, but still two worlds apart.
i am sorry, but i have to say that i am not in favour of the amendments. the commission is not the negotiations on the future relationship between the united kingdom and the european union are not just about how the future economic and trade relationship will be shaped - no, it is about much more: it is actually about the future of the european union and - do not misunderstand me, this is not the end game, and the next eleven months will not be the end game, but it can be a decisive game changer. the outcome of these negotiations will also determine whether membership of the european union or development outside the union is the more attractive development model. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. that is what brexit is teaching us. the mandate we are discussing here must therefore put the interests of the people of the european union and the people of the united kingdom at the centre of the negotiations. my group welcomes the clear statements on the prevention of tax dumping, the protection of workers' rights and labour standards, consumer protection and environmental protection. no one wants to see more tax dumping, no one wants to see worse working conditions, except for those who benefit from it, and those are not the workers, ladies and gentlemen. in short, we want the highest standards, the protection of peace in northern ireland and democratic control of the negotiation process. we are very critical of many other aspects of the mandate, for example military cooperation, the positive attitude towards nato and intelligence cooperation. we will engage constructively in the planning process in the interests of our constituents, knowing that a hard brexit is still possible and in no one's interest.
mr president, at the end of the transition period, the cost of brexit will have exceeded the cumulative contributions of the united kingdom to the budget since its accession 47 years ago. this fact only confirms the belief of all those who say that brexit is economically insane. the united kingdom is leaving the union, but it is still in europe and will undoubtedly remain one of the most dynamic economies on our doorstep. as rapporteur for the committee on international trade, i am clear that what we are offering the united kingdom goes beyond any free trade agreement that the european union has concluded to date. we offer a relationship free of tariffs and free of restrictive quotas. what is required in return is a level playing field, to avoid a race to the bottom in regulatory, fiscal, environmental and social matters. what we call in our jargon the level playing field must be worthy of the premier league, now and after the transition phase. to this end, we also need a referee who is up to the challenge. for this parliament, it is clear that when it comes to european law, the ultimate authority is the european court of justice. it is clear that a free trade agreement will never reproduce membership of the single market, but let us be clear: the level of ambition of this agreement will also depend on the tight timetable imposed by the uk counterpart.
madam president, we are here to discuss a very important resolution on the political guidelines for the negotiations between the european union and the uk – 21 pages, 103 paragraphs, but perhaps the most important is the front page, the signatures of the leaders of a number of major political groups in this parliament, showing that there is a vast majority supporting this resolution and how united we are in defending our common vision and our common interests. we call on the member states to do the same, to refuse any bilateral negotiations at this stage, and to act as a bloc, with the commission and mr barnier being the sole negotiators on our part. we are clear about what we want to achieve – a sound and ambitious strategic, political and economic relationship – but we also say, loud and clear, that we will ensure open, but also fair, competition, a proper level playing field and no regression on the high european standards of environmental, social and labour protection, and also consumer rights and food safety. we’ve heard some messages of refusal of this by the uk government, but this will have proportional consequences in terms of the access of the uk to the european market, undermining the ambition of our trade agreement. let me conclude by recalling that the first pillar of the future economic relationship with the uk was already agreed in the withdrawal agreement. it has to do with northern ireland, the alignment of northern ireland and border controls in the irish sea. so we will work for a new agreement for the future, but we will also ensure full implementation of the commitments in the existing withdrawal agreement.
i have to say, madam president, it is sad in every way that such a large and close country has chosen to withdraw. it's sad for them, it's sad for us. on the other hand, it is good and strong that the resolution we are discussing here today is so comprehensive and that it has been drawn up by such a large majority in the european parliament as is the case. it sends a very important political signal that we in the european parliament will ensure a level playing field in the future cooperation. why? because we cannot compete on lower standards in the field of the environment, the climate, consumer protection. we cannot do that if we are to compete on lower tax rates, and we cannot do that if britain is to expect full and free access to the eu's single market without at the same time complying with the rules and requirements that may apply here. we must not gamble with the eu's single market. it's a crown jewel in the collection, and we have to look after it. in the short term we have a very particular challenge in fisheries: many jobs are at stake, many people's livelihoods are at stake. it is important to say in this context that the negotiations on the future of fisheries cannot and should not be seen in isolation from the negotiations on trade in general. so it will all be difficult negotiations against a very sad and dramatic background, but the ambition must be that we create a strong partnership for the benefit of all.
madam president, yes, we must of course have a special relationship with the united kingdom. our similar standard of living and our common civilization invite us to it. but that does not mean that we should give in to him. mr barnier, a few days ago you proposed, and i quote, 'the elimination of all customs duties and quotas on goods traded with the continent'. clearly, this would be a free trade agreement and philippe lamberts, a few moments ago, made the same proposal. you are making free trade agreements with the whole world - as you did with vietnam recently - but you cannot stand the fact that the uk is no longer part of the customs union. it is now a third country and we must take account of that. the risk is that tomorrow it will become a gateway for products from all over the world that do not meet our standards and are of poor quality. the priority is to protect our interests, for example those of french fishermen who make 30% of their turnover in british waters. seventy per cent of the uk's fisheries exports are to the european union; we have a huge leverage in future negotiations, so that they are favorable to us. i know you don't like the trump method, but it works in economics. we should be inspired by it. our market, our consumers and our fishermen are entitled to the protections that you systematically deny them. brexit is an opportunity to change the model and to set up a new one.
madam president, i have to be honest: it’s a very special moment i think, because it’s the first time that we discuss brexit here in this house without having our uk colleagues being here, and i must say that apart from a bunch that was sitting over there, i really truly miss them, and i think it shows again how sad it is that this brexit is happening. now, i had the great pleasure and the honour to do my erasmus year in the beautiful city of edinburgh. still, when i go there today, i really feel at home, and i think these are really the ties that we have to cherish also for the future. and the economic and trade relationships are going to be absolutely crucial in the future. but if we want to build strong foundations, we also have to look at other issues, and that is why i think we have to fight to keep the united kingdom in the erasmus programme. young people did not vote for brexit, and i think it would be wrong to take away the opportunities from them that i and many millions of europeans had in the past after brexit. and that is why, if we want to build a strong relationship for the future, we should fight for a strong erasmus programme with the uk in it. let us leave a light on and build bridges so that the brits can find their way back home.
madam president! the draft resolution contains many pertinent statements and i think the ecr group can support it. but what worries me more is the spirit that can animate, or even reverse, negotiations. the latest exchange of views between brussels and london is not very optimistic. it is clear that a third country cannot have the same rights and benefits as a member of the union, an efta member or an eea member, but the uk will not be one of many third countries. even outside the union, it remains a large european country linked to other european countries by history, economy and security cooperation. it should be recalled that in the darkest moments of european history between 1939 and 1945, it was britain, like poland, that heroically defended european values. it is in the interests of europeans on both sides of the channel that we have the best possible relationship with each other. our negotiators should not forget that.
madam president, the withdrawal of the united kingdom from the european union gives us a window of opportunity to learn from past mistakes and not to make them again. the speeches of hatred, disaffection or uncertainty weigh on those who understand that the european project should be a space of progress and solidarity and feel disappointed. we can't delve into failed models that are driving citizens out. we now have a responsibility to guarantee the rights of the people, of the citizens living in the united kingdom - almost 140 000 spaniards are currently residing there. we are concerned about the situation of the workers, the 15,000 people who cross the gibraltar border every day, whose situation is still uncertain. we must fight social and fiscal dumping with determination. we do not agree with much of the content of this mandate in the field of foreign and defence policy. we believe that we must move towards a much more anticipatory approach to conflict and not go into sanctions that are paid for by the people alone. from this parliamentary group we will continue to work to protect the rights of citizens, the rights of the environment and those of the primary sector agriculture, livestock to avoid the depopulation of our villages. we must prevent them from becoming the currency of other interests.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the text we are voting on aims to strengthen the unity of the 27 countries and to give strong support to michel barnier in order to protect our interests during the negotiations. first of all, it is important to protect the internal market. the united kingdom cannot re-enter it like a trojan horse without respecting rules and standards. all this to ensure that more and more citizens and businesses are covered. the health of europeans must be guaranteed by preventing the entry of agri-food products which do not meet our standards or which compete unfairly. we are italians and we want to eat parmigiano and not parmesan and i think all europeans would rather eat parmigiano and not parmesan. citizens' rights must be protected, for example, by ensuring the use of paper documents and not just online. and then our committees in parliament will have to be committed to protecting the key areas, such as fisheries, space, energy, and then the interests of peace in ireland and the spanish interests in gibraltar, and of course defence and the fight against terrorism. in conclusion, we must prevent the united kingdom from becoming a european singapore, which may not have strict anti-money laundering rules. all this in order to have increasingly positive relations with london, which has left the european union but remains europe.
i have to say, madam president, the resolution shows that we are ready to negotiate. and i think we can also manage to organise trade deals within nine months, because the situation is a little different now. usually we have two different partners who are kind of moving towards each other, and you agree on common standards or you recognize standards. now that we have a harmonized system, we have to decide how far we can go apart. in this respect, it is a bit more comfortable than other trade negotiations. that is why the three pillars of normal trade negotiations must be taken into account. the question of customs clearance is the common objective. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the second is the standards of products and services. we have a clear set of rules on consumer protection and health protection. the sps standards will not be attacked in any way. so i think it'll be easy to get along there, too. and the third pillar is the most complicated. of course, rules affecting trade must also be discussed to avoid distortions of competition. so the question of equal rights for workers, the question of equal environmental standards, the question of the same aid scheme - very important - and the question of a harmonised tax rate. otherwise we would have distortions of competition, and that cannot happen. if there is any distortion of competition, then we will of course have to introduce tariffs to compensate for that. it is absolutely absurd to think that there is a dumping island across the channel with full free market access.
madam president, i would like to thank mr barnier for his work and his words. as the eu and the united kingdom embark on this new relationship, which i hope is a fruitful one, i want to send a very clear message to the united kingdom. this parliament will not allow the united kingdom to become a tax haven or a money laundromat just offshore europe. i want to remind you about paragraph 21 of the resolution. it says, ‘we strongly believe that the uk should adhere to the evolving standards on taxation and anti—money laundering legislation within the eu acquis, including tax transparency, the exchange of information on tax matters and anti—tax avoidance measures’. this applies to the territories as well. i would remind you that british overseas territories include bermuda, the cayman islands, gibraltar, the virgin islands: crown dependencies include all of those islands that have no taxes on deposits, etc. mr barnier, this is an existential question for europe. if we allow tax avoidance and money laundering just offshore europe, our way of life is in danger and our welfare state is in danger. this has to be one of the highest, if not the highest, priority for our negotiations.
i have to say, madam president, we are all aware that there are many very important aspects of our relationship with the united kingdom that need to be regulated now. this is our task - in the interests of our citizens, in the economic, social, environmental and cultural dimensions. that is why it is less about a free trade agreement and more about fair and inclusive trade relations. i think it is a worrying idea to try to arrange everything in a single de facto association agreement, as was the case with ttip. questions of cooperation between armed forces, energy and network cooperation, police cooperation and airspace regulation must not be removed from parliamentary control and handed over to committees set up under the agreement. i appeal to the council: create transparency, maintain democratic standards! i very much welcome the commission's approach to enshrining the un sustainable development goals, giving a lot of weight to the paris agreement, the ilo and fundamental rights, including data protection. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter.
madam president, when someone is organising a brexit it is to leave, not to stay otherwise. the united kingdom cannot have more advantages from outside than from inside. we spent three years mourning their passing. now we can't be confused by nostalgia because they're gone. they are now a third country. we deal with them hard - as we do with any third country - and defend our own. we are in favour of the decolonisation of gibraltar; we are in favour of preventing it from becoming a tax haven, of preventing it from remaining a tax haven. we defend our fishermen, who every day have to risk their lives to earn a living. we defend our farmers. we stand up for our products. if they, if the british politicians have wanted to be a third country, let us treat them as what they are in the negotiation, a third country. the united kingdom has left europe. we cannot allow it to become the new south east asia on our doorstep.
the european parliament must clearly set out its priorities on the future relationship with the united kingdom. is it possible to reach an agreement by the end of this year? what deal? it should be stressed that no country outside the union can enjoy the same rights as a member. the negotiations are based on four fundamental freedoms, and the welfare and rights of eu citizens in the uk and vice versa must remain in focus. in the context of foreign affairs and defence, i hope that the united kingdom, as a permanent member of the nato security council, will continue to share our goals and values. the same applies to joint civil-military missions, sanctions compliance, the iran deal and the arms control and non-proliferation agreements. i hope that as the european union, we will also maintain the unity that was successfully achieved in the withdrawal negotiations during these negotiations. thank you again to our british colleagues for their many years of work and their great contribution to european standards that have improved the quality of life for our citizens.
madam president, mr barnier, ladies and gentlemen, the circus of the united kingdom's withdrawal from the community club is over and we must get down to work. we must start negotiating trade and economic agreements that guarantee the rights of our citizens. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. let us negotiate strongly, but let us negotiate no less strongly than we have to negotiate with other countries outside europe with which we also conclude trade agreements. in this sense, i believe that it is the right time to take the fight to end the process of decolonisation of gibraltar, preventing this british colony on spanish national territory from continuing to parasitize the spanish economy with unfair tax practices. under the umbrella of the european union, gibraltar has consolidated itself as a base for laundering money from criminal activities. we therefore call on the commission to lay the foundations for this process of decolonization. we know that this is the responsibility of the spanish government, but we must ask loyal members like this club to cooperate and to respect the dignity of our nation.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, honourable colleagues, distinguished commissioner, the negotiations between the european union and vietnam started in 2012. they were first concluded in 2015, subsequently reopened and then finalised in june 2019. these eight years has witnessed dramatic progress in vietnamese society, economy, labour conditions, environmental protection and sustainability. one of the crucial ferments of these changes is the european union. the perspective of enhancing eu—vietnam cooperation by implementing the free trade agreement and the investment protection agreement gives positive motivation to the vietnamese authorities. just recently we have received a work plan on domestic advisory groups, a work plan and implementation plan on the labour code and a positive answer to the establishment of a joint parliamentary oversight committee between the national assembly of vietnam and the european parliament. this house faces tomorrow a crucial choice: to grant or not to grant consent to the eu—vietnam agreements. postponement would be the 20th century choice. when we give homework to vietnam and then send them home to do the work. granting consent is the 21st century european choice, when we formulate our expectations and then we say to our vietnamese partners: ‘let’s do it together’. as the shadow rapporteur for the epp group, my appeal is to vote for consent tomorrow and then to work together – the eu and vietnam – for a better, freer and more sustainable future.
the commission is therefore proposing to amend the proposal to the effect that the commission should be able to take part in the discussions. the commission is not prepared to accept this. fifty years ago, willy brandt developed the concept of 'change through rapprochement' to enable countries with fundamentally different views to enter into dialogue and discuss ways of improving the situation for the people on the ground. this is exactly the right way to try to improve the situation of the people on the ground through dialogue, also in relation to vietnam - especially with vietnam, a country with which we have a lot of trade. i am sure that many of you, my dear colleagues, wear clothes from vietnam, wear shoes from vietnam, all samsung smartphones come from vietnam, all samsung tablets come from vietnam. and i want us to set up rules here so that production is in order, as far as standards are concerned, as far as the use of chemicals is concerned and, above all, as far as workers' rights are concerned. and, commissioner, with all due respect, these achievements have been made by the european parliament. we have been negotiating intensively for the last two years. and i am proud of this parliament for having used the opportunities that we have by getting the fundamental ilo core labour standards ratified and implemented, for having got a binding implementation plan, monitored by the ilo, put on the table by vietnam, for having got the domestic advisory groups of civil society organisations to be staffed by independent civil society organisations, for having got the space for civil society to be expanded, for having got a real improvement in the human rights situation as well, for having got the european parliament's subcommittee on human rights integrated into the human rights dialogue for the first time - this has never happened before - and for having got parliament's cooperation and implementation of the agreement through parliament. so in that respect we can be really proud as a parliament that we have achieved this. but i also say that the work must start now, because we will continue to fight for the agreement to improve the situation of the people on the ground. so let's get to work!
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, before we make any commercial assessment, we must remember that vietnam is a politically repressive state where the communist party has a monopoly on power and there is no independent media. no dissent is tolerated, criticism of the government is punished by long prison sentences, there are no free trade unions, religious services are controlled by the state. many of the commitments given by the vietnamese authorities are formulated without precise deadlines. it is an unforgivable naivety to settle for overly general statements of intent. the environmental costs of the eu's efforts are clearly in contrast to the sacrifices it imposes on its member countries: in vietnam, coal is the main source of electricity generation, with a coal contribution to the energy mix of over 40% and the completion of around 100 coal-fired power plants by 2030. the commission has also proposed that the community should be given the opportunity to negotiate a new agreement with the united states on the basis of the gatt agreement. this is an indication of the commission's lack of sensitivity to an important and sensitive area such as risk. the question of the protection of intellectual property, which is currently being handled in vietnam by a flimsy and ineffective system, is dangerously in doubt. this could cause damage to our companies. we must not forget these considerations when we vote, lest we betray the values which our europe has always proclaimed and which it should also defend instead of thinking only of economic returns.
after eight years of negotiations and debate, we are almost there. so let me say a few words as the former rapporteur of these treaties from the beginning until last autumn. first of all, i would like to emphasise that this agreement was properly negotiated by the european commission in accordance with the principles of eu trade policy and the mandate given by the european council. i think the eu needs to send a signal that it is for free trade, that it is for the removal of trade barriers in a time of rising protectionism and in a time when the united states has withdrawn from an already negotiated trade agreement with the countries of south east asia. we have a unique opportunity to fill this void. no other deal with any other southeast asian country is on the way, not in sight, at this point. i think vietnam is growing into a geopolitical power, it's a country of over 90 million people. it is the chair country of asean, it is a non-permanent member of the un security council and it is in the political and economic interest of the eu to have this agreement. if we don't close it, the eu won't win, the united states or china will win, but we certainly won't profit from it.
for the gue/ngl group madam president, then, should we deepen our partnership with vietnam? yes, we all agree. does it necessarily have to be a free trade agreement? well, no, and that's where the debate comes in between us. you think - it's a bit of magic thinking - that a free trade agreement will solve all the problems. no, that's not true. firstly, i do not share the commission's optimism that it is a win-win agreement. no, you can't! today, the union's trade deficit with vietnam is 30 billion. in the commission's impact study, we are told that in 10 years' time it will be 40 billion. and who's gonna pay for it? the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the spread of the disease. so, to make vietnam a kind of great workshop of the world, the risk is still relocations. we are told that vietnamese workers will benefit because they will adopt the ilo standards, but we have examples! look at south korea: we were promised things that were never fulfilled. so i think you are either very naive or very hypocritical, a hypocritical human rights, because frankly, we cannot say today that the vietnamese government has met our expectations. in fact, the opposite is true: in the latest ranking of press freedom, vietnam ranks 176th out of 180 countries in the world. but above all, i would like to remind the commission of this incredible double-speak: we vote on the climate emergency, we vote on the european green deal and at the same time we pass, business as usual, a free trade agreement which is clearly climate-destroying and which does not protect sustainable development. frankly, it gives the impression of a european union which always prefers trade to the fundamental values which should normally guide us all in this house.

madam president, we just heard it this morning. overall, the trade agreement with vietnam benefits several sectors in the european union and, in the agri-food sector, meat or wine or cheese may be several of them. it is regrettable, however, that the european commission has once again failed to protect one of its sensitive sectors, rice. vietnam has the lowest rice prices in all of southeast asia and competes directly with varieties produced in spain and italy. spanish rice growers are outraged by this new concession and its impact on prices in a sector already in crisis. the fact that we are running short of rice does not mean that the opening of frontiers will not cause irreparable damage to european production. i cannot understand why, after applying the safeguard clause at the beginning of 2019 with myanmar and cambodia, the european union continues to make the same mistakes and does not assess the impact that these imports will cause on the european market and on a sensitive product such as rice. the european rice sector plays a key role both in the economy and in the conservation of geographical areas of high environmental value. we demand that the european union authorities carry out studies of this impact and take the necessary steps to implement new and more effective safeguard clauses.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, as always, this agreement between the eu and vietnam is full of contradictions. the issue of human rights being repeatedly violated, some significant opportunities for our businesses and a broad protection for our appellations of origin. yet there remains a major unresolved problem in a crucial sector such as rice. italian rice production, the largest in the european union, and european rice production have been under attack for years now because of the eba agreement with cambodia and myanmar. the safeguard clause was applied late and badly and it has recently been announced that rice will not be covered by the blockade of trade facilitation with cambodia. in this context it is clear that the introduction of further large quantities of vietnamese rice at very low prices is likely to bring an already established sector to its knees. our delegation does not therefore intend to be complicit in this draft and will not support this agreement until italian and european rice is safeguarded.
i have to say, madam president, at a time when free trade and cooperation are under pressure from old friends, it is more important than ever that the eu continues to open up markets. we are now the guarantors of fair and sustainable and rules-based trade. that is why we should also approve this agreement, which is the most comprehensive trade agreement ever between the eu and a developing country. vietnam has committed itself to all eight ilo core conventions, six of which have already been ratified. a country under communist rule has now recognized the right to collective bargaining and is moving toward freedom of assembly. 95 million more people today have the right to organize and to fight together. it's a big step that would never have happened if we hadn't reached out. the vietnam deal is far from perfect. but politics is not about perfectionism. it is about concrete progress for concrete people, and we will achieve that by keeping vietnam close.

madam president, commissioner hogan, the free trade agreement with vietnam is an important signal both outside and within europe. the european union is once again proving that it is the world's great trading power; it is proving that, despite the attempts of the populists to lift tariff barriers and return to protectionism, europe is still the greatest defender of free trade. it is also important within europe; it is important for the thousands of companies which see in the disappearance of the 65% of tariffs a great business opportunity, a new market which opens up when others close. for spain, this will be of particular benefit to sectors such as meat, which is becoming more and more present in asia, and to the olive oil, wine and spirits sectors, which are recognised for many of their geographical indications and designations of origin. for those who do not benefit, we will be vigilant to ensure that this agreement is not signed at the expense of anyone and that all spaniards and europeans benefit from it. i therefore call for support for this agreement.

mr president, as has been acknowledged here, the prolonged unconventional monetary policy measures have not been sufficient on their own to arrest the sharp economic slowdown, and they tend to exhaust their scope. we don't know when the next peak of the crisis will come, but we do know that it will find the economies of the eurozone's periphery as unprotected as they were a decade ago. with high debt, itself a consequence of the single currency, and the scope of unconventional measures exhausted, all their vulnerability will be exposed, more exposed, the vulnerability of these countries, trapped in the euro trap, a trap that imposes policies, particularly in the budgetary field, that stops growth, crushes investment and wages, degrades the productive apparatus and public services. the same people who say that we need a fiscal policy which is favourable to growth are doing everything they can to impose stubborn compliance with a revised and strengthened stability pact. this is the euro trap. they say it needs to be deepened. what is needed, however, is to dismantle it.

president of the european central bank. – mr president, i didn’t know that you had to be standing to address this assembly, so i offer my apologies for not having done so in the first place and therefore i will align with the rule that you seem to have amongst you. i would like to first of all thank you very much for this dialogue that we had. it’s very much part of what we at the european central bank, and more broadly within the euro system, want to actually have with public opinions, with civil society representatives, but more importantly, with those who represent the citizens of europe: that is you, members of parliament, who have been elected by them. this is very much part of the accountability that we have towards you and, through you, to the citizens of europe. we have conducted a few changes in the last few months, but certainly one of those that i very much look forward to implementing as we go into the strategy review that has been approved last month, is the constant dialogue that we need to have, and what we have called the outreach with you and, through you, with the citizens of europe. so thank you for that. i thought i would just touch on some items of our strategy review to address some of the points that you have made. first of all, some of you have touched on the monetary policy and the current components of the instruments that we use in order to implement our policy and have been doing so since 2014. to those who argue that this monetary policy, and particularly its unconventional tools, have been counterproductive, i would very strongly encourage them to look at the impact that those unconventional tools have had on the economy – in which way it has actually increased growth and helped our economies around europe and the euro area in particular to fare better than it would have had it not been for those instruments that were used as part of the unconventional policy. it’s obviously very difficult to quantify carefully and precisely by how much growth has been lifted in the euro area, but our models actually indicate that that growth has been probably higher by a margin of about 2.5% to 3% ever since those policies were put in place. in the same vein, it has contributed to inflation which, as you know, ever since the euro was put in place and ever since the strategy was revisited in 2003, is measured with reference to inflation, and clearly those unconventional tools that have been used since 2014 have helped inflation go up a bit. well, certainly we would have liked it a lot more than that, and maybe this would have been the case if other tools had been used in conjunction with our monetary policy. but be it as it was, certainly inflation has increased by a factor of about half a percentage point probably, which is certainly not bad, given the level where we are. to those who were mentioning, by the way, that inflation was hovering around 1%, i would simply remind you that in january inflation was at about 1.3%, which is certainly not the goal that we have, but certainly a bit more than the 1% that was mentioned. those unconventional tools that were used and the instruments that were deployed helped our economies and continue to help our economies. when you look at the volume and the number of new financing that are put in place for the real economy, whether it’s the corporate or whether it’s the households, we are seeing clearly financing that is very low in terms of cost, and i take the point that you made, sir, about the irish banks and how much they charge and what interest rates they include, and we’ll look into that for sure. but the cost of financing has been low and lower for the real economy. and number two, the volume of financing of the economy has increased and has continued to increase. you will have seen some comments recently about the fact that this continuum is slowing down. yes, it is a little bit – for the corporate, not for the households – but it is continuing to grow nonetheless. so i would contend that, in the main, it has had a very positive effect on our economies, and as part of our strategy review, we’ll look at how much and how effective it has been. but we will also look, as some of you have mentioned, to the potential side effects and to the accumulated side effects that it could have on citizens and on the economies as well. so that will be very much part and parcel of our review. the second point that i would like to emphasise briefly, and i’ve focused on the monetary policy tools and how we will look at them and measure them and measure the benefits as well as the negative effect, is climate change. nobody can ask the european central bank to substitute what governments should do and what policies should be in general. as former managing director of the imf, i think i’d be remiss not to remind all of us that one of the key tools in relation to climate change is clearly the proper pricing of carbon, and this is not something that a european central bank or any central bank in the world can actually decide. so as we go through our strategy review, we will determine where and how the issue of climate change and the fight against climate change can actually have an impact on our policies, whether it’s in relation to our price stability primary objective and how it impacts this primary objective, or whether it’s in relation to the management of risk that we are accountable to our european citizens and to you when we look at the composition of our portfolios and the one that we manage, and whether or not risks are actually properly priced and whether the climate change risks associated with some of the collateral that we have in our portfolios, for instance, are measured or not. so in that respect, climate change will be one of the items that we will take into account, together with the more traditional aspects that we will be measuring, and it’s in the context of that strategy review, mr vice-president, that we will continue this dialogue, not only with you, but also with the committee on economic and monetary affairs (econ). the president of econ was here and many members of the committee are present in this room. we will continue to engage: we will have that dialogue, and we want that very much to be a two-way street. now, i offer our apologies to those who had very specific questions on particular countries, but i propose to take that up bilaterally with those who are interested in specific countries or banks going forward.
i can only wish you, personally and in the interests of the european union and the global interest, all the best in your work.

the next item is the debate on the statement by the council and the commission on the violation of council decision (cfsp) 2017/2074 concerning restrictive measures in view of the situation in venezuela illegal entry into the territory of an eu member state of a person included in the sanctions list (2020/2564 ((rsp)).
vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – mr president, the current subject of debate concerns an alleged breach of european union sanctions currently in force vis-à-vis venezuela. these were put in place in 2017 as part of the european union’s efforts to foster a credible and meaningful process that can lead to a peaceful, negotiated solution for the reinstatement of democracy and the rule of law in venezuela. these measures include a travel ban and asset freeze regarding 25 individuals, among them the vice-president of venezuela, delcy eloína rodríguez gómez, included on this list having been designated by the european union on 25 june 2018. a travel ban requires the european union member states to take the necessary measures to prevent the entry into, or transit through, their territories by the listed persons. as you know, there is a division of competences in the area of sanctions within the european union. first of all, member states are responsible in all cases for the implementation and verification of sanctions adopted by the european union in their own jurisdictions. member states, i repeat, are responsible in all cases for the implementation and verification of sanctions adopted by the european union. therefore, when the issue of a possible violation of sanctions arises, for example an assets freeze or, as here, a travel ban, it is for the member states concerned to investigate and determine whether this has in fact been the case. with regard to the wider oversight of the implementation of sanctions, the commission has an overall monitoring role concerning the uniform application of such measures in an area with these competences. for example, this is the case for asset freeze and sectoral measures, but it is not the case for travel bans or arms embargoes. we can discuss the political appropriateness for member states to give this competence to the european union, although this would require a treaty change. but the situation, for the time being, is clear. the commission cannot initiate any infringement procedure regarding a possible travel ban violation. travel bans are, in practice, only contained in council decisions and they do not therefore fall under union law. consequently, the commission does not play a role in monitoring the implementation and cannot initiate an infringement procedure. as high representative, talking here from the side of the council, i am also responsible for ensuring the consistent application of the common foreign and security policy, including sanctions adopted by member states in council. in this context, the european external action service, in consultation with the european union, where competent, would be directly in touch with the european union member states regarding alleged violations in order to ensure that these sanctions are being applied in a homogenous way in all member states.

madam president, this debate in plenary has brought out three major coincidences, apart from ideological differences. we are all aware of the added difficulty and complexity of budget negotiations in a post-brexit context, and that is why we are committed to a swift agreement, which provides legal certainty for the main sectors of our economy. and we demand, beyond figures and percentages, a budget which reflects the new priorities of the european union. the green deal is going to be the legislative protagonist for the next five years. but that pact and all the measures that will follow from it, such as the farm-to-table strategy, will only win the support of european citizens if, instead of cutting budgets and asking for more environmental contributions, we are able to offer farmers the support they need, not only to pursue more ambitious environmental objectives, but also to develop a profitable economic activity. we must address the neglect of our rural areas and the fatigue and frustration of our farmers through a budget that does not marginalise the challenges of the rural world and its remarkable contribution to sustainability.
madam president, 47% of today's jobs will probably disappear in the next 25 years, and 85% of the jobs of the year 2030 have not yet been invented. those are data may move by some percentage, but that's a reality. the digital economy offers opportunities, but it also presents serious risks that we're already experiencing, including the digital divide, a divide that is age-based, that is geographic, that is gender-based, that is social. and in the face of this growing inequality, what can we do? more social europe, that is to say more education, more training, more protection. that means we have to take a holistic approach to the problem. we have moved closer with the green deal to a willingness to transform our economic model. what if we were to bet on a social deal, on a new social contract that is able to link these changes and avoid collateral damage to people, to millions of european citizens who will not be able, without help and without concrete measures, to embrace this transition, this change, and may be left behind. that risk is a certain risk. we have to do everything we can to make sure that doesn't happen.
madam president, europe must prepare for the digital revolution without leaving behind, as has been said, 'any person or any territory, rural or peripheral'. digitalisation must benefit all people, contribute to reducing inequalities, improve public services and create quality jobs. we know that many jobs will be destroyed, but also that many new ones will be created, especially those linked to artificial intelligence, for example, for which 58 million jobs are expected to be created. but it is precisely in these digital sectors that women are lacking. only 22% of those employed in these sectors - you said it, commissioner, there is a very large gender gap - only 22% are women. we need to invest in comprehensive training and a digital culture that supports the development of appropriate skills and competences, but we cannot do without any talent at all. talent is equally distributed but opportunities are not, and we have to work precisely to make those opportunities reach absolutely everyone, including women.
madam president, digitalisation and the implementation of artificial intelligence represent a huge, huge opportunity; we could almost say that this represents a huge evolutionary leap, unparalleled since the industrial revolution. but it's also clear that it brings risks, it brings change, and it's also clear that it brings a lot of fears that have to do with control, that have to do with job loss, and there's only one way to confront those fears and highlight the enormous opportunity, and so seize it, which is digital literacy. the literacy. which is much more than just giving digital capabilities, which is much more than just giving digital tools. it means a humanistic approach to the question to also democratize the implementation of things like artificial intelligence so that people, not just experts, are aware of what those risks are, those contradictions, those values that are opposed, and so make better decisions and somehow be more and more a society that goes hand in hand and in a more integrated way towards the future. so what i ask of the commission and the council is that they make an effort to do this, in a participatory, open process, in which they can highlight and unravel this mystery to transform it into something understandable and that can be shared by everyone.

on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, every year thousands of false documents are detected at the external borders of the european union. in 2018, according to frontex, 7 000 foreigners were detected presenting false travel documents at the external border crossing points of the union. various methods of counterfeiting are used as a genuine multi-purpose tool in the commission of crimes money laundering, terrorism and as a key to illegal entry into the territory of the european union. this fact highlights the great importance that the protection of our borders and immigration control have for the security of our nationals. the regulation, the fado system, will, by drawing on the experience of frontex, promote a rapid and efficient exchange of information, without prejudice to the member states being able to develop better systems for the detection, prevention and prosecution of crime. in this case, our group, concerned about this investigation and prosecution of crimes and about the rigorous control of the legal or illegal entry of foreigners into our territory, will vote in favour, and we encourage the rest of the house to do so.

madam president, a europe with open borders is an absolute nonsense which only the utopia of a very irresponsible left is defending in this parliament. there is not a single country in the world where the borders are open. where anyone can enter depending on their own will. the european union obviously does not have to be different. we must respect those who want to improve their living conditions and we have an obligation to welcome people with humanity, but for this to be possible with such scarce resources, there must be very strict controls on entry at the external borders of the european union. and we have to distinguish what has to be distinguished: one thing is refugees, another is migrants. refugees are subject to different treaties and laws than migrants are subject to different laws, different realities. anyone who is considered a refugee and who flees to save his life must be accepted in accordance with laws or treaties, but anyone who is merely seeking work, who is a migrant, must enter or not, depending on the laws and will of each country, with respect for the principle of subsidiarity. each country knows which migrants it needs. the uncontrolled entry of migrants into europe is unacceptable, it endangers the safety of people, our labour, social and democratic systems. and the acceptance of the real refugees, that yes, must, of course, happen. the honourable member's presidency, the common agricultural policy is the most expensive and least effective european policy. in its long history, several major reforms have been attempted, but without much success because, regardless of the specific model, the high level of state interventionism makes european agriculture expensive, inefficient and globally uncompetitive. we are entering a new era of reform with a focus on circular economy, climate change and green policies. we want to make european agriculture sustainable and climate neutral in the long term, but i fear we will resort to the same solutions that have proven bad in the past. new expensive public programs and subsidies are not a sustainable solution in the long run. we need to encourage innovation in agriculture and make educational programmes available to all european farmers. this is the only way to ensure sustainable growth of this industry, boost competitiveness and avoid overburdening the budget.
madam president, commissioner, we are starting from the premise that a large majority of this house wants agriculture to be more sustainable and to contribute to achieving the objectives of the 2030 agenda. i hope that the farm-to-table strategy will help to achieve this, but i am concerned that some people want to turn european agriculture into a big tailor's box. let me give you an example: some people want to see this happen to agriculture, like the iphone. what started as a phone has now become a gps, a camera, a recorder, an internet browser, a odometer, an alarm clock, etc. a device that serves almost everything and whether or not you make phone calls is of little interest. similarly, some people want european agriculture to serve the preservation of the environment, to combat climate change, to reduce pesticides, to reduce the use of antibiotics, to improve biodiversity, to give citizens better information, to protect forests, to improve animal welfare, and so on. but we must not forget that the main purpose of our agriculture and of the common agricultural policy is to obtain quality products at reasonable prices which encourage the great effort of our farmers. their future will undoubtedly depend on it. let this farm-to-table strategy help those farm workers, farmers and livestock farmers, to achieve the objectives at a fair price and commensurate with the effort they put in.
i'm not going to let you do that. the current system is indeed flawed and destroys some farms in one region and encourages production in another. the dairy sector is a prime example. moreover, the dominance of shopping centres in the marketplace poses a threat to a wide range of consumers. for example, lithuania's largest retail chain, maxim, sold substandard meat last week and covered it up. the new strategy must ensure that consumers have access to natural, safe food that is grown and produced in their own region. today, food often travels thousands of miles. but does anyone ask themselves, do we need this? is it good for the environment and the climate? direct support to farmers in different member states still varies, not by percentage, but sometimes. there are still primary and secondary farmers in europe. where else are barriers created under the guise of free movement of goods and services or trade agreements opening up the way for imports of gmo soybeans or products grown under questionable conditions? by creating favourable conditions for farmers to work in their own land, we will ensure the viability of regions and allow people to stay in the countryside.
madam president, the spanish agricultural and livestock sector has been mobilizing for a month in the face of the crisis affecting the sector: prices at source that do not cover production costs; abusive practices by large distributors; trade agreements with quality standards that are completely opposite to european standards. in short, a lack of profitability which has forced the closure of hundreds of farms. the european union is loudly proclaiming its 'farm to fork' strategy, yet little or nothing is said about the real concerns of farmers because we are only focusing on reducing plant protection products, increasing animal welfare rules, and incorporating new environmental obligations. i wonder how we are going to respond to the market crisis facing the sector in spain and other countries of the european union if we continue to fail to understand that an economic sector that is not profitable can never be sustainable. in this way we will remain alienated from the reality of our field and unable to offer solutions to a sector that needs more understanding and urgent support.

first of all, madam president, it must be remembered that for many years in many member states there has been a policy of total irresponsibility and of eliminating cuts. there was underfunding of health care and overburdening of medical staff. so the fact that individual facilities are still managing the situation is more than a victory for governments, it is a victory for the strong will of the staff, to whom we should be grateful. with them and the ecdc and wto preparedness, there's no need to panic. prevention and objective information are crucial at this time. as regards the ecdc, i would like to point out that i have long called for the strengthening of the agency, both in terms of its powers and, above all, its funding. because if anything proves its importance, it's this epidemic, and i'm very grateful to hear from my colleagues at the epp today that they've finally understood that, too. but what's important to me is that when the dust settles and the epidemic is over, we take something from all of this. because it seems the system isn't set up the way it should be. first, the lack of vital health supplies for frontline workers, such as doctors, nurses, and social workers, is alarming. it's clear that we underestimated our stockpile and our ability to manufacture these things. and that has to change in the future, and there's a lot more that has to change, and i firmly believe that when we get through this crisis, as we get through it, there's a lot of work to be done to strengthen our system.
