on behalf of the renew group. mr president, mr vice-president, thank you for your good intentions. the criminals get 160 billion euros a year, 1% of the union's gross domestic product, and every year we taxpayers lose 825 billion in unpaid taxes and another 50 billion in vat fraud. this money laundering, unfortunately, goes largely unpunished. according to europol, 99% of the profits of criminals are not confiscated and remain at their disposal. we must remedy this problem if we are to build a more just society. recent scandals, such as the 'luanda leaks' or the murder of daphne caruana galizia, show that the member states are not doing enough and that europe is not leading in this field either. we therefore believe that the european commission must take a more ambitious approach and parliament approves this resolution by a large majority, with five demands that i would like to highlight. first, the creation of an anti-money laundering supervisor with sufficient resources. secondly, the creation of a european financial intelligence unit to eradicate the most sophisticated forms of evasion. thirdly, to use the european public prosecutor's office to fight against money laundering. to give the european central bank the power to withdraw banking licences in cases of money laundering. and fifth, to make the ownership records of the shadowy companies public and known to us. mr president, the commission must take the necessary steps along the path laid down by this parliament. criminals have profited from the lack of solutions for too long. mr. dombrovskis, it is time to act.
mr president, mr roth, minister of state, mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your work. the eu commission has put forward strong proposals, and the council of member states has also made considerable progress in the fight against money laundering. these proposals also have the support of parliament: common supervision, common stronger rules, but also an effective european financial police capable of cross-border investigations. but we should not turn a blind eye to the fact that the infringement procedures cannot hide the fact that, in the light of the implementation of the recent reforms of the money laundering directive, an old law dating back 13 years from the money laundering directive 3 is being broken in many member states including germany and that there are no infringement procedures against it. i therefore appeal to the european commission and the member states to finally implement the existing law, to initiate infringement proceedings not only when the letter of the law is not complied with, but when money laundering is actually taking place in the real world. the commission has not yet fully complied with its obligations in this respect. more speed, more pressure, commissioner!
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the fight against money laundering and the financing of terrorism must become a strategic challenge for the european union and cannot be separated from the fight against other criminal activities such as corruption, trafficking in arms, drugs and human beings, and tax evasion and fraud. where rules and supervision are less stringent, individuals and criminal groups are allowed to pollute the economic and financial system, already strained by the effects of the pandemic, with illicit proceeds. we cannot allow divergent interpretations or inadequate and uneven application of european law in this area in certain member states. the existence of european tax havens attracting companies and capital, undermining the fairness of the internal market and distorting competition is intolerable. the implementation of a prevention policy must be comprehensive, identifying high-risk jurisdictions, harmonising different legal systems and strengthening tools for sharing information on beneficial ownership of companies and financial transactions, in order to target the proceeds of crime and the assets of organised crime.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. the money laundering cases in the european financial system in recent years show that we still make it far too easy for money launderers. i would like to mention two very specific problems: firstly, the lack of implementation has already been mentioned. last week, commissioner, you opened infringement proceedings against three member states for the implementation of the fourth directive. i do not want to talk about the state of play with regard to the fifth. secondly, it is a directive. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. the second problem, which i want to address here quite openly, is, of course, that communication between the authorities back and front is not working. we are also experiencing this in germany, where supervisory and investigating authorities have difficulties - i will say this very abstractly - in cooperating with each other in concrete terms. recent cases prove it. the things have been identified, we are now really waiting for proposals from the commission: a central authority with investigative powers and regulations instead of directives. otherwise we will have to have the same debate year after year.
mr president, this debate on combating money laundering is certainly very reminiscent of the debates on combating tax havens, tax evasion and tax avoidance, areas on which there is very broad agreement in parliament, in the commission and in the council. an agreement strongly supported by the citizens of the whole of europe who believe that it is not acceptable for us to continue with these legal loopholes in our tax systems. but while there is this broad consensus, we are not able to put forward new initiatives, to comply with the directives we have adopted or to have efficient coordination between the states, and we contribute to the frustration of european citizens. i therefore believe that it is time not only to look at how directives are transposed or not, but to use community legislation, regulations in this area, to avoid any kind of indiscretion, a regulation that has a common european supervisor that also imposes sanctions that are clearly disincentive to this kind of behaviour.
(start of the intervention off the record) ... most serious: 1% of the union's gross domestic product in financial activities suspected of money laundering stemming from corruption and organised crime, including terrorism. i therefore fully support the commission's action plan, the first objective of which is the implementation of the fifth directive, which is in force and needs to be not only implemented, transposed, but applied by the member states. the second objective is supervision, with a single supervisor who puts all the financial reporting units in touch. the third objective is harmonization, and thus the transition from a directive to a regulation, which ensures uniform compliance with european law by the member states. the fourth is international cooperation, which must be strengthened in a situation as serious as this. the fifth is training and, of course, ensuring that the beneficiaries of money laundered through corruption and organised crime are identified. only in this way will we really achieve the purpose of this resolution, which is to support the commission in demanding that the member states implement the fifth directive as soon as possible.
mr. president, i have to go. there is no doubt, ladies and gentlemen, that money laundering and the financing of terrorism must be tackled by all means possible. we cannot allow commercial, even cross-border, crime to run free. the question is, what is the most effective way to achieve this goal? over the past few years, we've been putting together a set of eu rules. some of them have just come into force, so we don't have any practical experience of how they work. serious financial scandals have happened before, not under the current legal framework. i also have doubts about the transfer of supervisory functions to new eu-level institutions. such a move would be contrary to the principle of subsidiarity, since it would reduce national competences in an area where strengthening national authorities and better cooperation between member states could provide an adequate response to the challenges. parliament likes to run ahead, although perhaps with a little thought we could achieve more in practice in this fight. despite my reservations, i believe that the further development of the system for combating money laundering and terrorist financing is an objective that should be supported. that's why i'm going to support it with my vote, with my fellow mps, with my fellow mps from the hungarian people's party.
executive vice-president of the commission. – mr president, on behalf of the european commission, i would like to thank the european parliament for taking time to debate this important matter and for your commitment to further strengthening our defensive tools against money laundering and terrorist financing. we need to act upon all findings and be ambitious in our efforts to make the european union more effective in preventing damage to the integrity and reputation of the union’s financial system. many points were raised in this debate on effective implementation of current rules. indeed, it’s one of the work directions, so more effective implementation, strengthening preventive capacity on transposition of anti—money laundering directives. the commission has made very clear, including to member states, that we are going to be very assertive about proper and timely transposition of these directives and also, on the way forward, that we need to move away from directives to regulations, at least in a substantial part of the legislation, which will allow for more effective direct enforcement at eu level, and we need eu—level supervisors. my thanks to all the colleagues who were supporting those points. there is the question which was raised by several colleagues on taxation and, indeed, the fight against tax evasion and tax avoidance often goes hand in hand with the fight against money laundering. next week, the european commission will come with a new set of measures to strengthen our framework dealing with tax evasion and tax avoidance, so the tax package will be presented by the commission next week. we are also looking at the scope of operators subject to anti-money laundering rules and there, at the very minimum, we need to include virtual asset service providers, just to give you some examples. in any case, the european commission remains committed to the fight against money laundering. we see it as a priority and we thank the european parliament for the strong support you are providing.
the president of the council. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. i am glad to take this from our debate: we must do more to combat money laundering and terrorism. we are all in agreement on this in principle, with a few exceptions, which i will come to shortly. we need more transparency, we need more regulation and we need more control. i think it's worthwhile that we really consider seriously every proposal that's on the table, to simply and poignantly improve. i am also very grateful to the members who have once again pointed out the financial dimensions of money laundering: 1% of gross domestic product, possible tax losses of 8%. let me be clear, this is not a case of chivalry. i'll give you one hint. i am here for the first time, i don't know if this is common, but i have to break a spear for a committed, critical civil society that is part of a living democracy. now, not all of us like what ngos say. but that too is a sign of a living democracy. i am afraid that the impression is being given that critical ngos, which some honourable members may not like, also have something to do with highly criminal schemes such as money laundering or the fight against terrorism. this is murder! this is a murder case! this is about drug offenses! they have nothing, if anything, to do with the critical engagement of ngos in the european union.
the debate is closed. in accordance with rule 132 (2) of the rules of procedure, two motions for resolutions have been tabled. the vote on the amendments will take place tomorrow, thursday 9 july 2020, and the final vote will take place on friday 10 july 2020. written declarations (article 171) the following documents shall be submitted to the court of justice:
in writing. despite the adoption of the fourth and fifth anti-money laundering directives and the directive on combating money laundering through criminal law, the legislative, institutional and regulatory landscape in the eu on anti-money laundering and countering terrorist financing remains fragmented, thus providing an incentive for businesses to register where the rules are less stringent and generating disparities in interpretation and practice across member states. the commission's communication on an action plan for an integrated union policy in this area must therefore be welcomed and must be followed up urgently, also in view of the danger, as highlighted by europol, that organised criminal groups will exploit the current economic uncertainty to launder money through the on-shore financial system. a single body of rules should therefore be established, including the transposition of the provisions of the anti-money laundering directive into a regulation; to establish an eu-level aml/cft supervisory authority and a european support and coordination mechanism for fius; ensure effective cooperation and exchange of information between them and the law enforcement authorities. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to cover the whole of the european union, including the member states, and to extend it to cover the whole of the european union.
in writing. i thank the european commission for its action plan on a comprehensive union policy to prevent money laundering and terrorist financing. we need such a comprehensive policy a strengthened legislative framework on money laundering and terrorist financing to protect the financial system of the european union. it is essential that this framework is applied in the same way in all member states. this is why the control of the implementation of the money laundering directives must be strengthened. i hope that the european commission will soon translate some of them into a regulation, so that we can harmonise the rules for everyone and close the 'loopholes' in our legislation that criminals use to abuse our financial system. we are calling for a stronger role for our joint police force, europol, and its newly created european centre for financial and economic crime. at the same time, it is important that the newly created european public prosecutor's office starts working as soon as possible and we encourage all eu member states to join it. effective action against money laundering and terrorist financing will increase the security and confidence of our citizens in the financial system.
the next item is the debate on the statements by the council and the commission on the eu public health strategy for the post-covid-19 pandemic (2020/2691 ((rsp)). i would like to point out once again that there are no blue cards or spontaneous statements in this debate.
the president of the council. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. we are all dealing with a health emergency that is having a massive impact on our societies and our populations. we are also thinking of the more than 100 000 europeans who have died from covid-19. we are discussing very intensively in these weeks what we can learn from this serious crisis for our health system and for coordination within the european union in the context of an effective and, as far as possible, preventive response to the pandemic. we are also talking, of course, about the massive social and economic consequences. last but not least, the chancellor has made it clear once again that this is one of the major priorities of the german presidency of the council of the european union, and i am sure that we all, including here in the european parliament, share this commitment to do everything possible to ensure that we emerge from this serious crisis as quickly as possible, in the strongest possible solidarity and as united as possible. i would like to take this opportunity to express my sincere thanks to the nursing and health care workers who have done so much to protect lives and provide the best possible care for patients in these difficult and dramatic times. the commission's proposal is to be adopted by the council at the next meeting of the european council. we must speak openly about what has been successful, but we must also speak openly about what has failed and what was our weakness, especially at the beginning of the fight against the pandemic. only then will we be able to respond better, faster and more comprehensively to another crisis in the future, and we all know that this crisis is far from over. we are still dealing with a very volatile situation. in some countries, the crisis, the pandemic, has been contained by a dedicated approach. but in europe, and in our immediate neighbourhood, we are also experiencing that nothing is safe. we must therefore be vigilant, and this is of course always accompanied by a reduction in the population in liberal democracies such as ours. this is another subject which has been the subject of intense debate here in the european parliament, but also in the member states: how can we protect the health of citizens without disproportionately restricting the freedoms and constitutional rights of citizens? the commission should also be able to provide the necessary information to the member states to enable them to take appropriate measures to prevent the spread of the disease. i would like to thank you once again for the great interest and commitment you all have shown in this matter. globally, and not just in europe, the pandemic continues to spread faster. we have about 200,000 new cases every day worldwide, and we all know -- this is obvious -- that a global crisis of this magnitude requires global responses. no country, no matter how good its health system, can fight this pandemic alone. it is also clear that a policy of national isolation, a policy of small states, a policy which weakens europe and the european union, always involves unacceptable risks to the health of our citizens. it is a very painful experience, ladies and gentlemen, but perhaps it also encourages us to work together even better than we have done so far in a spirit of solidarity and shared responsibility to overcome the pandemic. this means that we have a lot of work to do, because we cannot successfully tackle the economic crisis until we have also tackled the virus in the long term. a comprehensive and successful strategy to overcome the pandemic for europe and, of course, for the world requires the development of safe and effective vaccines and therapies, and equal access to them. the only way to defeat the pandemic once and for all is to eradicate it everywhere. the only lasting solution to this problem is science and solidarity. the european union is committed to ensuring that newly developed vaccines against covid-19 become a shared, public, global commodity available to all those who need them. what we need is a vaccine for europe as well as the rest of the world. there can be no cannibalism here, everyone against everyone, but we must all stand together. here again, we all have a duty to protect the health of people, wherever they live, in europe or elsewhere in the world. i am very pleased that the european union, together with its international partners, has joined forces in the global crisis response initiative. a record €16 billion - this is as of 30 june - has now been raised to accelerate the development, manufacture and delivery of covid-19 vaccines. because we have to act decisively so that we can take the next steps. so we need safe and effective vaccines, and we need to encourage mass production of vaccines so that we can really have equal access for all people. in this context, i would like to thank the european commission for its recent legislative proposal to allow rapid clinical trials of covid-19 vaccines and therapies, and i would also like to thank the european parliament for its decision to take a position on this dossier today. i am pleased to inform you that the council approved this dossier last friday. as regards the components of the future european union strategy on public health, the commission has recently proposed an ambitious eu for health programme as part of the multiannual financial framework and the building package. it reiterates the need to give health a higher priority in the european union and to be better prepared for health crises. one thing the crisis has shown us is that quality health care is still a great protection for our people against such a pandemic. that is why we give this dossier a very high priority in the council and we hope that with your support we can adopt this dossier at the end of the year. we look forward to working very closely and in confidence with you on this important proposal. it is also crucial that we improve the availability of medicines, medical devices and other crisis-related goods in the union. you know how that started at the beginning of the crisis. it was not ideal, but i think that we as the european union and the member states have drawn the right conclusions from it. the covid-19 pandemic has exacerbated the problems resulting from the european union's dependence on third countries for the manufacture of pharmaceutical active substances, raw materials, medicines and medical devices. we are not trying to unravel the mystery of globalisation. but we must ensure that we can, with our own european strength, ensure that the key goods that guarantee the protection of the citizens of the european union are also produced in the european union - that is european sovereignty. we must also be better able to deal with these crises, to gather information, to assess assessments and then to act quickly in the event of a crisis. close cooperation is essential between the member states and the institutions, bodies and agencies of the european union, such as the european centre for disease prevention and control or the european medicines agency, and with international organisations such as the world health organisation. one of our objectives is to strengthen and extend the way in which these european institutions and agencies operate. the eu for health programme should focus on the lessons learned from the pandemic, while supporting longer-term projects. it should focus on the availability of medicines, vaccines and medical devices, the reduction of antimicrobial resistant infections and the eu's role in global health policy. the programme will also help to implement the eu's medicines strategy and the european plan to combat cancer, which the commission intends to adopt by the end of the year. the coronavirus crisis has painfully reminded us once again that we need to expand our digital capabilities in healthcare. we need to overcome the fragmentation of health data and improve eu-wide implementation within the health system. in their recent conclusions on shaping europe's digital future, the members of the council called for the creation of a european health data space. i would also like to remind you of the controversial debate on an eu-wide app that can be used everywhere to protect people even better, and there is also room for improvement, as you all know as well as i do. equally important is the fact that investments in health are also made possible through other instruments of the multiannual financial framework. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. one thing is clear: health will be a key area of activity at the european union level in the future, and i do not think that this is at all painful for the member states, because the pandemic has shown that we need more europe here too, a europe that is more capable of acting, because this is in the interests of the citizens. and the covid-19 pandemic has also given us an opportunity to reflect on what actions we need to take to overcome the current pandemic and prevent future health risks. together we can make the european union and the world a healthier and safer place. many eyes are on us, and we should show that liberal, democratically constituted societies are capable of handling well such serious crises that lead to existential hardship for many people, and that our primary objective is to protect people in the european union, but also to provide solidarity on a global level.
member of the commission. – mr president, this debate comes at a key moment for our management of what is the most severe health crisis of modern times. today we meet as countries relax restrictions and open up for travel: a chance for people and our economies to breathe after months of lockdown. but this is an opening which comes, as we all know, with considerable risk. whilst no one knows with certainty how the coming weeks and months will develop and while it is still too early to speak about life ‘post-covid’ (i speak of the ‘new normal’), it is certainly the right time for us all to reflect. we need to reflect on the painful lessons we are learning. we need to reflect on the areas we need to improve and where our actions should be strengthened to protect the eu from future cross-border health threats. and we need to reflect on what the eu’s role in health policy should be more broadly, in respect of member states’ responsibility for their health policy enshrined in the treaty. next week, on 15 july, we will present an action plan setting out a number of immediate short-term actions to help eu member states prepare for and manage possible new outbreaks of covid-19. i want to be absolutely clear: a full-blown second wave is, unfortunately, not inevitable. it is, however, up to us to control it and prepare, if and when it comes. our actions should focus on reducing the burden on health systems, particularly if new outbreaks coincide with the influenza season in the autumn and winter. covid-19 has shown us very clearly that a united approach is critical, but that we must also strengthen the eu’s current preparedness and response mechanisms to better protect the health of our citizens. many, including members of this house, and our citizens, have called and are calling for a stronger role for the eu on health. i cannot but agree, and say that i stand ready to work on this together. as a first step, we should increase our capacity to support member states. this will require stronger and more operational eu agencies. in the coming months we will be proposing to strengthen the european centre for disease prevention and control, to improve its coordination capacities and its mandate to respond to health crises. we will also be proposing to strengthen the european medicines agency to ensure it can effectively coordinate our actions in preventing medicine shortages. we also want to look at how to strengthen our eu framework on cross border health threats. beyond the immediate priorities, now is also the right moment to reflect on the eu’s competences in the area of public health, and its limitations. i, for one, have, from the very beginning, said that we need more europe in the area of public health. the conference on the future of europe provides an excellent forum to really debate this, whether we need to look again at who does what between the national and the european level, and particularly so as regards threats that touch us all, regardless of borders. even within the deepest crisis, however, come opportunities – opportunities from what we have learned. strengthening the strategic autonomy of our health and pharmaceutical systems is a case in point from the current crisis. with our proposed eu4health programme and the unprecedented eur 9.4 billion envelope we proposed, we send a very clear message that public health is a priority area for our recovery, that we have been listening to citizens, that targeted investment in crisis preparedness and stronger health systems for the future is key. what is now required is very close cooperation and a swift agreement between parliament and the council in the coming weeks and months to ensure it can be delivered. i fully count on your support to take the ambition forward. in parallel, and i am very aware of the importance, our new pharmaceutical strategy will be coming later this year – an opportunity for us to ensure and reinforce the strategic autonomy of our pharmaceutical sector and ensure access and affordability of essential medicines for our citizens. i have been in contact with stakeholders for this strategy over the last few weeks. the next six months are critical. now is not the time to let our guard down. the situation remains very fragile, and we see this in a number of member states. stable numbers change very quickly. a safe and effective vaccine likely remains the only permanent solution to this pandemic, but this is still some time away. together with the member states, we are fully engaged in negotiations with vaccine manufacturers to ensure access to vaccines when they become available. we will ensure that the eu continues to play a leading role in international efforts to develop, manufacture and deploy a universally-accessible vaccine for all member states of the eu, leaving no one behind, and for the world. we are not going to say ‘us first’. we will continue to support countries inside and outside the eu to protect all people from new outbreaks, especially the most vulnerable. solidarity will remain key, because it saves lives. and here i just want add that from the beginning of this pandemic, i have held over 26-27 meetings with all the health ministers and i am in constant communication with them, because this is the only way that we can build up this level of coordination, consensus and working together so we can deal with what it means to move forward. we need to pool resources, we need to tackle jointly the issues that affect all of us. this is the first and possibly the most important lesson learned from this crisis: we need to respond to the expectations of our citizens, ensuring a stronger role for the eu when citizens’ health is at stake from threats – threats which do not stop at national borders. and we all have a collective duty to ensure that.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. in march/april, at the beginning of the pandemic, i went back to practice, like many members of the medical profession. and the first thing i noticed when i asked my colleagues was that we didn't have masks. i had to look into the throats of patients with fever and cough, unable to protect myself. it was a mixed feeling for me. for many nurses and doctors in bergamo and madrid, this meant that they had been infected and that they were no longer alive today. this is one of many examples where we simply need to get better: we can't be so dependent on china and others when it comes to vital things for our health. i am therefore very pleased that we have the opportunity to discuss this resolution today. i would like to thank mr manfred weber for being here and for helping us to have this resolution, and i would also like to thank the members for their good cooperation. mr president-in-office, we support the priorities of the german presidency and the proposals of the european commission. we want to achieve a lot together in the coming months, but we also want to see more european involvement in health in the long term. the member states have often said in the past, in a way of reflection: health is national, europe should stay out of it. that was always wrong, and it's even more wrong now. a virus and other health threats don't stop at the border. that is why we need a european health union.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, as a pharmacist i expected this resolution on public health to be a technical document, with details of how to deal with this pandemic. and no: it speaks to us of new bodies, new institutions... another attempt by parliament to increase the power of brussels and take more sovereignty away from our nations. they repeat words without substance: green pact, ecological transition, gender equality, resilience, etc. mantras that numb the conscience, in order to impose a new world order, which mrs merkel has just mentioned in her speech today. a new world order or a new normal (as they now call it), which only serves supranational interests. however, our needs are different. in this health crisis, we need clear, precise, immediate protocols of action that really protect us from future outbreaks, from potential new viruses. i would remind you that the union is not here to replace the member states, but to facilitate free, voluntary and, above all, sensible cooperation. only then will europe be stronger; only then will we emerge from this crisis.
mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank all the other co-authors in advance for their good cooperation. the text of the joint resolution presented this time really reflects broad agreement across the political spectrum. today we face an unprecedented situation and from this must also emerge new unprecedented solutions. the old guidelines and the tired business as usual are not going to help us now, we need a fundamental reform. healthcare on the premises of the european institutions has always been such an ashtray, as evidenced by the fact that it is carried out by about 10 people on the premises of the european parliament, all of whom know each other well. the covid-19 pandemic has exposed the fact that the eu currently lacks strong tools and competences to address emerging health challenges. the establishment of a health union along the lines of other deeper integration projects seems absolutely necessary in the light of today's events. however, i am afraid that we will be able to achieve something similar under the incoming german presidency, because it is the german government that has been blocking the completion of negotiations for years, for example on the hta regulation, which is, from today's point of view, a completely childish step in the field of enlargement cooperation. the question for me is where we need to go from here, in the joint procurement of medicines by member states, at least for rare diseases and vaccines. as regards vaccines, intellectual property rights such as patents should not restrict rapid access to vaccines and further treatment. scientists around the world developing these vaccines and treatments often use huge amounts of public money. big pharma shouldn't be profiting off this pandemic at the expense of people's health, and they shouldn't be paying for research that's already been paid for with public money. the gue/ngl wants to ensure guaranteed access to diagnostics, therapeutics and vaccines related to the covid-19 virus for all. that's why my colleague botenga and i, and others, started the right2cure petition two weeks ago, and i'm inviting you to sign it. i won't drag this out, but i'm asking you to pass a joint resolution, which could be a good start for the future.
mr. president, we are experiencing the worst health emergency of our lifetimes in terms of health, economic and social consequences. but we have the opportunity and the obligation to achieve better coordination and strengthen our health systems against future threats. with the recovery plan, we renew our commitment to the well-being of europeans and all health professionals to ensure greater protection and response capacities, both in the face of health emergencies and in the fight against chronic diseases and, for example, cancer. we want a europe for health, with a health approach in all policies, with more funding from the european budget and with the ema and the ecdc with more powers and with decision-making and coordination capacities, for an effective response throughout europe. this strategy is the first step towards effective prevention, towards more and better research to have effective medicines and treatments, and also a vaccine accessible to all. we also need to support the healthcare production sector and its activities in europe, so that it can create new quality jobs and, above all, autonomy in production. europe has the best national health systems in the world. that is why we are the health reference in a global world, increasingly exposed to threats. this is the europe we are defending, the europe of health and the europe of people.
i am speaking on behalf of the gue/ngl group. madam president mr. borrell, the european union says that in 2019 it was a leader in the promotion and protection of human rights. for me, it was the year that the mercosur agreement was closed, while bolsonaro was burning the amazon or promoting the lack of protection of indigenous peoples. 2019 was the year in which carola rackete and other ngos were criminalized and vilified for rescuing people from certain death in the mediterranean, while the western parliament rejected a resolution calling for the resumption of search and rescue operations in the mediterranean; the year in which international law was ignored and a trade agreement with morocco was ratified that includes the occupied territories of the sahara; the year in which human rights violations committed by crimes of governments such as erdogan or al-sisi continued to be whitewashed. judge for yourselves. i'm clear on that. i believe that we must stop lecturing and start setting an example, respecting human rights in our trade agreements, respecting human rights in the mediterranean, respecting human rights also on european territory.
madam president, mr high representative, the report we are debating today marks the end of the implementation of the action plan on human rights and democracy (2015-2019). one of the objectives of this plan, as set out in the report, is to combat torture. an important tool for this is the human rights dialogues with third countries, as was done with cuba in october 2019, where there are still, unfortunately, prisoners of conscience, arbitrary detentions and medical missions considered as modern slavery by the united nations. another priority of the union's external action is to promote democracy and the rule of law in the rest of the world. in 2019, eight observation missions and seven expert missions were carried out to support electoral processes. these resources could ensure a democratic and negotiated transition in venezuela, with free, fair, transparent elections, nullifying the regime's attempts to corrupt democratic institutions and illegitimately depose the leadership of political parties. mr high representative, use all the instruments at your disposal to ensure that democracy and human rights are restored in the countries suffering from their absence.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, mr high representative, mr borrell, the relationship with turkey is complex, but it is a relationship. why do i say this obviously? because there is a clear interest on the part of turkey to compartmentalize the relationship, creating a few disconnected and inarticulate pieces. we have offered turkey the maximum, which is to be a member of our club - even if it sometimes sounds like a punishment - and the framework of the relationship must therefore be that of a candidate country. that means a progressive alignment of turkey with all the standards of the european union. the relationship situation is bad - you acknowledged that on monday - because there is a lack of trust, a substrate of lack of trust that affects all areas of the relationship. the exemption is frozen, with a brutal deterioration of human rights, in the matter of visa liberalization. and it has to be said: turkey has not met the standards it has to meet to join; on the customs union, we are fighting at the world trade organization; on migration we are receiving an unfounded accusation of not meeting, when european public opinion thinks just the opposite; and in foreign policy, this idea of assertiveness is being - i think - misused. we cannot be in solidarity with greece and cyprus because we are greece and cyprus and therefore cannot act as a mere mediator.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. the pandemic should have taught us that humanity needs to stick together, and the citizens of turkey are as much to be valued as all the people in this world. but the turkish leadership, which is rejected by many citizens, perhaps by the majority, is doing a lot of things wrong. from north africa to vienna's favourites, to the federal capital of my home country of austria, it's destabilising, it's needlepointing, it's escalating instead of de-escalating, as the pandemic phase should have taught us all at the latest. the arms embargo against libya is being circumvented, bad blood is being spilled on nato partners in the mediterranean, and riots are being carried into the streets in vienna's favourite cities, presumably with the support of the turkish leadership, in the middle of europe. the eu must therefore put an end to the status of candidate countries. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances.
mr president, it is important that we are discussing today turkey's wrongdoing in the european union and in the eastern mediterranean more generally, but that is not enough. the major mistakes of history, my dear mr borrell, are repeated because those in power do not learn from the mistakes of the past. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in the end, humanity paid for this supreme mistake with rivers of blood, because we did not learn from history and the supporters of that policy were condemned by history. the credibility of the european union is therefore being judged in the eastern mediterranean. we are not going to be able to do that, because we are not going to be able to do that. as we speak, germany is selling state-of-the-art weaponry to turkey, which turkey will use against the interests of the european union. that is not credibility or values, my dear mr. borrell.
mr president, mr borrell, to the crisis of last march, when the erdogan government threw thousands of immigrants at the gates of the union, we must add the military incidents with france, greece and cyprus, as well as the new threats made during your own visit to ankara. and that is because, as you are hearing here in this european parliament, the union lacks political determination and does not defend the strong, christian values that make up europe. however, in reply to a letter from all the vox members to you requesting that the multi-million dollar payments to turkey be suspended, you replied that not all the money goes to the government and that you are very much in favour of maintaining these payments. let us continue to pay a bill of 1.7 billion euros to a country which we cannot consider a partner. a similar situation is experienced in the maghreb, where we also have several countries that are showing little collaboration and defiance in controlling illegal immigration. we therefore call on turkey to immediately suspend all payments to third countries which do not clearly cooperate with immigration policies and to put an end to the process of turkey's accession to the european union.
i am speaking on behalf of the gue/ngl group. mr president, let us be clear: you are bringing this debate on belarus here today because elections are coming up in a country that is an ally of russia and you want to influence the results. they are openly practicing the constant interference they denounce in other countries. and let me remind you that these moves have been dangerous in the past. when government changes are promoted at any cost, we can end up supporting governments with nazi ministers, as we have already done in ukraine. the eastern border of the european union is now heavily militarized territory and this year there have been nato exercises on the border with belarus involving more than 36 000 troops, most of them american. is this escalation of interest to the peoples of europe? obviously not. it only serves to fuel mr. trump's new cold war. they must put aside this old-fashioned view of international relations. we need an independent and sovereign foreign policy that is committed to multilateralism and stability on our continent, not to sanctions and constant confrontation with our neighbours.
in writing. the roma, with some six million inhabitants in the eu, are one of the largest ethnic minorities in europe. unfortunately, a significant part of its population still lives in very poor socioeconomic conditions. the creation of the current european framework of national roma integration strategies until 2020 was considered a milestone and represented a turning point for roma communities in europe. however, as a non-binding policy tool, it provides a framework for policy coordination, consultation and monitoring and specific policy recommendations for national roma integration strategies, but its translation into administrative capacities and concrete policy actions is left to the discretion of national, regional and local governments. in order to correct this problem, a directive on the equality and inclusion of people of roma origin needs to be adopted for the new decade. a legislative act binding on the member states, with clear objectives, adapted to the needs of each particular context, with specific indicators, more efficient and robust monitoring and surveillance mechanisms and adequate funding.

on behalf of the ecr group. madam president, commissioner, colleagues, this is an important debate and an important subject, because culture is the foundation of self-awareness, of self-determination. that is why i would like to see at a time like this, a serious discussion about what the european commission should be promoting. should it continue on this dangerous and slippery path of promoting transnational cultural projects that go left, go towards cultural marxism? cultural projects that try to present the european man as a man without roots, without a clear national self-definition, without a clear national self-consciousness, without a clear gender identity and without any cultural support. because, madam president and commissioner, i firmly believe that we europeans owe what we are to our european origins; to our religion; to the fact that most of us are christians, whether orthodox, catholics or protestants, but most of us are christians; and to our sense of self and our self-definition. and when we talk about our roots, what makes us who we are, we can't avoid, commissioner, i'm talking about hagia sophia. hagia sophia is not called the mother of all churches for nothing. it's the foundation of christianity, of eastern orthodoxy. what is happening in turkey at the moment cannot be an internal turkish matter, it is a pan-european issue, a cultural issue, our issue, and i ask you to put it, with all its severity and all its sharpness. president erdogan can no longer provoke the feelings, the feelings, the beliefs, the religion of the christian european peoples.
madam president, the recovery fund and the multiannual financial framework must bear in mind the cultural dimension of the european union. the erasmus+ and creative europe programmes must get that increase in funding that we all want. we must also support europe's cultural heritage, which also plays a very important role - as you have said, mr vice-president - in attracting tourism, a sector which is now suffering so much. the council of europe's european cultural routes programme is an excellent showcase of the richness of our cultural heritage. we must contribute to its financing. i would particularly like to stress that next year the first of these routes, the camino de santiago, will be celebrating its holy year or jubilee year. throughout 2021 xacobeo will probably be the most important cultural event in europe. the european union must help. the roads to santiago are roads to europe. they cross all the countries of the continent and for centuries many european citizens have made pilgrimages to compostela through them, thus contributing to the creation and expansion of a common european culture and also a european identity. and since i am speaking of historical heritage, i would like to join my colleague, mrs šojdrová, in her question on the plan to declare hagia sophia a mosque.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, today's debate is very important for the future of the european cultural and creative sector, which is being severely affected by the covid-19 crisis. thousands of people working in the field have not had the opportunity or the means to adapt to the changes brought about by this crisis, and the solutions implemented by governments leave much to be desired. despite the many appeals from the european parliament, despite the calls from each of us to the national authorities, this sector of which we are proud and which is one of the strengths of the european economy seems to have been forgotten. this was unfortunately also the case in romania, where the authorities did nothing for the three hundred thousand people who work in this field and represent around 10% of the gross domestic product. that is why we are today calling for a european response and clear measures to support this sector. we need to allocate 2% of the european recovery plan to rescuing europe's cultural and creative sector, but the same needs to be done at member state level. our resolution will be a last call to save the sector. i hope that the european executive and the council will listen to the voices of the thousands of people working in this field.
madam president, mr vice president, this is the first time i have spoken in the house since february and i would like to begin by sending my deepest condolences to the families of those who have died from covid-19 and also to express my gratitude to all the people who are working, putting their health at risk, to combat this pandemic. we are here today to talk about culture and the importance of this strategic sector in europe, the figures and importance of which have been mentioned in this house. but i would like to focus on the importance of defending this culture as a european cultural identity, as judeo-christian values, and on the importance of the european commission defending this european identity, defending western values. and we are seeing this with what is happening with hagia sophia, which they want to declare a mosque, or we have seen it with the attacks that have been on monuments in europe, promoted by totalitarian movements and promoted by those who want to attack our identity, our culture and our values. we must therefore reclaim european culture, not only for its economic value, but for the importance it has in defending liberal democracies in europe.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, commissioner, the message is very clear: cohesion policy is not a matter of generosity, but of justice. we must harness the full potential of our regions, of our municipalities, to rebuild ourselves after this terrible pandemic, because it is the most important investment for our future. during the pandemic -- you said it -- we've mobilized over 37 billion euros that have kept jobs and that have helped people who have had the hardest time: groups of people with disabilities, groups of older people, people who are homeless, in metropolitan areas, in rural areas. it is the real europe, the near europe we want, not the europe that defends tax havens. we have many projects pending to relaunch europe, and to do this we must ensure that the allocations for cohesion in the period 2021-2027 are used for direct investments and not for direct investments linked to covid. we want a cohesion policy that focuses on reducing inequalities and that gives priority to territories, that gives priority to municipalities so that no one is left behind and that does not make conditionality. we are therefore very hopeful that the council will reach a good agreement and, with its encouragement, that this will be possible so as not to leave anyone behind.
on behalf of the renew group. mr president, madam commissioner, this pandemic has presented an unprecedented challenge to the institutions. it has left us exposed for the many shortcomings, but if there is anything to be proud of it is that europe has been able to react. the biggest recovery plan ever put forward in europe has been launched, with solidarity and cohesion at the heart of this plan. and if cohesion policy played an important role in the emergency, the new funds will be essential to overcome it. our concern is that they should reach the citizens as soon as possible, that they should be simplified, that they should have the flexibility they have never had and that bureaucracy should be eliminated. but this crisis should also make us think about the destination and use of these funds. because these funds are the opportunity that our autonomous communities, our municipalities, our towns have to carry out reforms that will lead us to a more digital, greener and more sustainable model; that will make our smes, our trade, our companies, our tourism much stronger and more competitive, and having a clear idea of what our priorities are and what europe we want. i want a europe that is much closer to the citizens, that invests in education for young people, but also for those who work and have to recycle; a europe where rural areas are not emptied but are attractive to young people and families; a europe where the digital divide does not exist; a europe with a digital and agile administration, with livable cities; a europe without inequalities and which leaves no one behind. that europe is possible if we all work for a much stronger, much more ambitious and much more modern cohesion policy.
mr president, the economic and social consequences of the coronavirus are dramatic, and especially in countries like my own, spain, where mismanagement has multiplied its devastating effect. in the european response to the crisis, the cohesion funds are of vital importance in order to eliminate inequalities and support an economic recovery which will undoubtedly be very long and very complex. we are glad that the commission has adopted our idea that member states should be able to transfer funds between programmes. however, two tasks remain. in terms of coherence and simplification of the different existing mechanisms, the transition fund cannot replace the cohesion funds, but rather complements them in very specific respects. and secondly, on the budget. many colleagues have said this earlier this morning: an ambitious cohesion policy, more important than ever at the moment, does not need cuts, but a budget that meets the needs.
mr president, commissioner, a few months ago, when we did not even know about the existence of covid, we were here debating the possibility of achieving a more ambitious financial framework. we were alert to the threat of cuts in cohesion funds and we called for less rigidity and more flexibility. sometimes it seemed like we were even asking for a chimera. today, however, we have shown that it can be done if we want to and that we have been able to react with diligence and speed to the most urgent questions. but what now? because unfortunately, this is just the beginning. there may still be a long way to go, and the european union cannot remain in first aid. the message is clear: we need sufficient and fair resources to ensure that no one is left behind and that no conditionality of any kind is imposed to reproduce the mistakes of the past. how can we guarantee both? and, no less important, what comes next? cohesion policies are the keystone of the european project and the principles that inspire it. this vault cannot be damaged or affected by the impact of this crisis; on the contrary, it must be strengthened, and we will only achieve this if we are able to give it the role it deserves, in the quality and quantity it deserves. you can count on our support to do so.
mr president, madam commissioner, first of all, i would like to thank president omarjee for having driven this debate and for having put this question so that we can be debating something as important as the role of the cohesion funds in this post-covid recovery process. first of all, i would like to stress that this parliament and the commission have been able to react in good time. we have reacted in time and it is important to tell the citizens about this. secondly, i wanted to applaud the ambitious plan that the commission has put forward, the next generation eu, where we are talking about 750 billion, coming from the tax dollars of european citizens, that are going to go to cohesion policy in this post-covid era. also, thirdly, i wanted to put the focus on the importance of this money being spent and used in those regions that have been most affected by the covid crisis. and that must be important: it must go to those regions that are most affected. and secondly, we need to re-frame priorities so that they are directed towards the most important objective, which must be job creation, wealth creation. it is small and mediumsized enterprises that will be able to create jobs. we must not lose sight of the fact that it is small and mediumsized enterprises which should be able to benefit most from the funds that are to be approved in the next generation eu.
mr. president, we have made it. many in this house and many in the commission, mrs von der leyen, and in the council, mr michel, have worked tirelessly to achieve an ambitious recovery fund which would enable europe to face the way out of this crisis in a united way. the european parliament showed the way with two resolutions in april and may, which showed that a pact was possible if we all thought like europeans. europe has done its job, and now the countries, all the countries, must do theirs. some are already celebrating as if they won the world cup, but the reality is that all the work is still to be done. the governments that are going to receive these funds have not yet done anything to solve the problems that these funds are going to be able to solve. we are only at the beginning of a long journey that we must take to create the changes and transformations that our countries need. nothing is free, not even the 750 billion euros financed by debt that will fall on the shoulders of future generations. our responsibility to those future generations is to use this money to build a better society. that is why, and only for that, we must use these resources. we must repeat this message in every corner: every corner of europe, every corner of our countries. we must explain that the reforms that have been so long delayed and the investments that are needed must be made now. we must use this opportunity to move towards a more humane europe, where all citizens have the capacity to enjoy true freedom, respect the environment, embrace knowledge technology and put technology at the service of people. how we invest this money will determine whether europe emerges from the crisis stronger or weaker. it is a time of rigour, commitment and responsibility. we in the european parliament must do everything we can to ensure that this important investment that europe is making is well spent.
mr president, the polish government does not seem to be concerned that the european union has for the first time ever activated its so-called 'nuclear option' under article 7. and this is because, in our opinion, it has been raised in a complex way in this case, while other flagrant situations are also avoided, such as the violence in france against the yellow vests or, in spain, the police repression in catalonia and the basque country. we believe that the council and the commission are still not up to the task of protecting the polish people from the continuing violations of their fundamental rights. we want them to stop reinterpreting the rule of law in a restrictive way and to be just as severe when attacks on women's rights or lgbtqi people happen, for example. the latter are being subjected to increasing harassment and we find it unacceptable that the polish president himself should speak out publicly against them. something must be done and it must be done now, and that is why we are asking the council to declare the existence of a serious and persistent violation of article 2 and to issue recommendations, and the commission to use all the instruments at its disposal to address the situation in the country.
mr. president, i have to ask you... in recent years, it has become a common practice for the eu institutions to use the rule of law as a political weapon, as a cudgel, against certain member states, without any objectivity. poland and hungary have been subject to a process for years, which has been based on ideological issues, serves exclusively political purposes and thus does not meet the requirements of the rule of law, thus trampling on the principles of due process and attachment to the prosecution. the rule of law cannot be taken into account by applying different standards to member states, by accepting reports of accusations instead of dialogue, by subjecting them to a land-locked procedure that lasts for years. the reality is that wherever a government is formed that has a characteristic right-wing politics, sooner or later, the allegations of the rule of law will come up. this is the case with hungary and poland, but we have also seen this recently with slovenia and bulgaria. your comrades, the socialists and liberals, are silent in cases of violation of the rule of law, such as the death of the slovak jozef chovanec here in belgium, following an uninvestigated police operation. or when the hungarian left is launching an openly anti-semitic politician in the midterm parliamentary elections in hungary this early october. when we ask you this, there is no rule of law problem, and there are no answers. what is this, ladies and gentlemen, if not double standards? thank you very much.

mr president, international law, and only international law, is the solution to the problem of turkey. the united nations convention on the law of the sea was signed in the early 1980s and has been ratified by 167 states. it's a matter of customary law. the european union is not only a member of the un but also a member of the un, and the un is not only a member of the un but also a member of the un. international law and the law of the sea are not fair to a la carte, they are not fair to turkey, they are not fair to an oriental bargain. the convention on the law of the sea, in article 121, refers to islands with economic activity. these islands have their own shelf, their own exclusive economic zone, and territorial waters within twelve miles. under article 3 of the convention, greece has the inalienable right to extend its territorial waters to 12 miles. but turkey, since 1995, when greece ratified the convention on the law of the sea, has threatened "casus belli", that is, cause of war, if greece extends its territorial waters to twelve miles. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is prepared to accept this amendment. but turkey wants dialogue with a gun to the head of greece and cyprus. this is not dialogue; it is blackmailing its neighbours in the eastern mediterranean. the european parliament and the european union cannot accept this.

mr president, mrs von der leyen, it has been a very, very difficult few months. many citizens have lost loved ones, lost their jobs or had to close their businesses. but i think we should be proud of the european union's response to this crisis. when we adopted the eu recovery plan, we sent a very important message to our fellow citizens: europe is with you, europe will not leave you alone, we will get out of this together. but not everything is solved. money must be spent well; some governments want to spend it foolishly. the spanish vice-president of the government, for example, this week in an interview, suggested that the recovery plan funds should be used to create new business elites. and i want to make this absolutely clear: from this european parliament, we will do what is necessary to ensure that money is spent well, not spent on helping friends and girlfriends; that it is used to support families in education, health; to fight climate change and protect our planet. because europe is not an atm machine, but an opportunity to fight for the well-being of our families.

the next item is the debate on the council and commission statements on combating sexual abuse and sexual exploitation of children (2020/2702 ((rsp)). i would point out to the members that there will be no spontaneous exchange of words at any of the debates of this sitting and that no blue cards will be accepted.
member of the commission. – mr president, we as adults are responsible for the safety of children. we as politicians are responsible for legislation to protect children, and, to be honest, i don’t think we are doing enough. there are people out there who use and abuse children, and we have the obligation to protect our children from these predators. in june, i spoke at the webinar jointly hosted with the european parliament intergroup on children’s rights. many experts and decision makers and our allies in the struggle spoke there – the dutch government, europol, the united states national center for missing & exploited children (ncmec) – but most impressive was the testimony of sarah. sarah is a survivor. she was groomed online as a teenager and forced into sex slavery. she said to us, ‘let my story not become a story we would hear from a friend, sister or mother’. so let’s do her justice. i know that you all feel as strongly as i do about this, but just let me repeat some of the terrible statistics. in the last 10 years, reports by internet companies of child sexual abuse increased exponentially. from 1 million to 17 million worldwide in 10 years, and from 23 000 to 725 000 in europe. european servers host the majority of this content. reports of abuse increased during covid. in the country i know best, a perpetrator posted, ‘thank you, corona. now it’s so easy to find the children.’ behind the statistics, there are real children, sometimes very small children. from the country i know best, we are seeing that, during corona, small children around the age of seven were groomed and by march the grooming of children at that age had been growing; children of that age forced to film themselves. and they were told, ‘if you tell anybody, i will kill your dog or your hamster’. you can imagine how that is for a small child. these predators have a business model that fuels this increase. closed and secret groups on the internet have an entrance fee of new and unseen material. to see videos of raped children, paedophiles must rape children. that’s how the business model works. most of the perpetrators are men, but not only men; 10% to 20% of offences are committed by women, or with the aid of women. most of the victims are girls, but not only girls. around 15% to 20% are boys. supported by europol, police forces in our member states are cracking down on abuse. the darkscandals website was taken down with more than 2 000 videos and images. this website used the slogan ‘real blackmail, rape and forced videos of girls all around the world’. police have taken down this site. the owner is now behind bars. but we need to do much more to support the police. in your resolution, this house asked us to work with industry and the big ict companies, because detection and reporting by internet companies is necessary to save children. last week, i presented a regulation, an interim solution, to make sure internet companies can continue to detect, report and remove child sexual abuse material. why am i proposing interim legislation? because of an unintended effect of the electronic communications code, and this comes into force in december this year. that would have an unintended effect: companies will no longer be allowed to pursue their voluntary efforts to detect and report child abuse online. that would pull the rug out from under our entire law enforcement effort against predators. last year, law enforcement in europe received 40 000 newly produced images and 100 000 newly produced videos, images that made it possible for authorities to find children and prosecute perpetrators. if we do not adopt that interim legislation, this will no longer be possible. i call on you to support this important regulation, which is a temporary solution until our long-term sector-specific legislation is in place. we are now working on this permanent legislation and will present it by next june, proposals that would make it mandatory for companies to detect and report known child sexual abuse materials to public authorities. current eu legislation puts strong obligations on internet companies to protect copyright – but not children who are being raped. and of course we must protect children more than we protect copyright. we also want to set up a european centre to prevent and counter child sexual abuse. in ireland, police get 80% of their tip-offs from the us-based national center for missing & exploited children, and only 20% from parents and guardians. just last friday, two men were sentenced to 13 years in prison in germany. they had had abused two girls for five years and that was thanks to a ncmec report that saved them. it’s time for europe to have its own centre, to build on europol’s work, to work with companies and law-enforcement bodies, to identify victims and bring offenders to justice. i will soon launch a study as a first step in setting up our own european centre against child sexual abuse. the eu directive on child sexual abuse dates from 2011. i will launch a study to assist its implementation and to see how and where we need to upgrade and update our legislation. we cannot allow paedophiles or perpetrators to win an online high-tech arms race. to keep one step ahead, europol will set up an innovation hub and lab. we will support member states with funding. the commission is also working with experts and industry to find technical solutions to detect and report encrypted online child sexual abuse. i must underline that this is, of course, important to do without prohibiting or weakening encryption, and in full respect of privacy. i must say, i do not accept that we have to choose between protecting children or privacy. it’s absolutely possible to combine the two. but we must also work on prevention. very often children are abused by someone they know and trust. we will create a prevention network of practitioners and researchers to share expertise to stop perpetrators from acting. we also need to encourage children, to tell them, ‘it’s not your fault’. it’s not okay what these adults are doing. we need to empower children to speak out. and i want europe to take global leadership, because abuse doesn’t stop at national or european borders. we will work together with governments, tech companies and civil society organisations worldwide. i need your help to pass this legislation. i need your help to protect children. a child has no power, and children that are abused least of all. but we do have power, so let’s use that power to protect children. (applause)
on behalf of the ppe group thank you, mr president. and thank you to the commissioner for her introduction. it's to keep you quiet. i became a father for the first time this year myself, and there's nothing like that feeling. the love, the responsibility, the need to protect that you feel for your child is very special. it makes me sick to think that there are people in this world who make fun of or make money off of abusing children. and yet we see that abuse is increasing, and increasingly so, as you said. i am also glad that the commission is showing that urgency here today. i miss that urgency in the member states. it is scandalous that 23 of the 27 member states are under infringement procedures for failure to implement the 2011 child abuse directive. what's the point of making deals with everyone here if no one's gonna follow through? child pornography must be discovered, reported and removed as soon as possible. hosting companies have a critical role to play in this, and they have to take that responsibility, whether they want to or not. we have to force them to do it. and that has to be with rules and with "naming and shaming". in the netherlands, a blacklist of non-cooperative hosting companies will soon be published, and i would also like to propose to support such an initiative in europe. in conclusion: encryption. you've already said, encryption is extremely important to protecting our privacy. but at the moment it is also a shield behind which child molesters can hide. we'll have to figure that out. a child who is abused is marked for life and as the commissioner rightly said, we are not doing enough at the moment to prevent that. please let today be the last day we have to say this.
the commission is therefore proposing to amend the proposal to the effect that the commission should be able to take part in the discussions. the commission is not sexual offences against children are heinous crimes with serious consequences for the victims. and we need to fight the sharing of images online, without forgetting that child abuse happens in our neighborhood - by parents, neighbors, caregivers, priests. looking the other way and not saying anything is not an option here or there. in 2011, we already adopted a directive on the protection of children against sexual abuse. but the vast majority of member states have not managed to implement it in nine years. there is also a massive need to catch up in the areas of prevention, support and protection of children, and the harmonisation of offences and penalties. the commission has therefore had to initiate infringement proceedings against 23 member states last year after nine years. child protection is an important issue. and we need to do more online. but at the same time, we must not give rise to false expectations, for there is no magic bullet that will make the images disappear. confidentiality of communications, privacy is important for our children, too, online. for example, encryption can prevent images that young people exchange with each other from being intercepted by perpetrators and shared with third parties. so it's very clear: we need to do a better job of protecting our children online as well as offline. laws need to be implemented, prevention agencies need to be funded, law enforcement agencies need to be adequately staffed, funded and equipped to better protect our children and their playgrounds offline and online.
on behalf of the renew group. – mr president, what’s in a number, dear colleagues? if i told you that the number of reports of child sexual abuse has increased to nearly 70 million in 2019, would you believe it? or if i told you that, according to the council of europe, one out of five children falls victim to sexual violence. would you believe it? what if i told you that europe is the biggest hub for child pornography, that can’t be true, right? the list of these numbers goes on and on. sixty per cent of worldwide child sexual abuse content is circulating in europe. isn’t that staggering? let it be clear. sexual abuse of children is not just one of the 99 problems. it is ‘the’ problem that all european decision makers have to face, and the fight against it must be our priority. therefore, i would like to thank you, commissioner johansson, for the bold and concrete steps forward in the fight against child sexual abuse. this is a strong, balanced and comprehensive strategy. but this alone is not enough. we all know that, we all have to play a role in this fight. i am fully convinced that a dedicated eu centre will be a major added value in the fight against these terrible crimes. commissioner johansson, i fully support your strategy. you can count on me, on us, on renew europe and on the intergroup on children’s rights to implement it. if we don’t do it, who will do it? so european leadership please, you can count on us.
on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, it is painful to be involved in a debate on paedophilia and the sexual abuse of minors the day after this european parliament condemned poland for trying to pass a law against paedophilia. you've spent years and years exposing sex as something banal, hypersexualizing society, considering sex not part of that inviolable dignity of the human being. look, stop talking, this is a serious problem affecting hundreds of thousands of children in europe and it has developed with the complicit silence of authorities and the media. there are the cases of telford and rotherham, which were shamefully silenced by the media because the pimps and abusers were of pakistani origin; or in my country, spain, in the balearic islands, where we have a case of dozens of girls prostituted in social care centres which the leftwing governments are rejecting and in which they are paralysing judicial and administrative investigation. the european commission also said nothing about this case of child prostitutes in the balearic islands when i asked it, on behalf of vox, in january. less talk, more law and order, harsher penalties, full compliance. we are lagging behind european civilization.
