i would like to stress, madam president, as rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on employment and social affairs on the report on the ethical aspects of artificial intelligence, drawn up by mr garcía del blanco in the committee on legal affairs, the importance of this dossier for the dignity and rights of workers. the greater flexibility or efficiency that the use of these technologies may bring cannot be synonymous with more sophisticated forms of exploitation or supervision of workers. as a result, the law, our legislative framework, must advance a set of regulations that protect workers from monitoring and metric mechanisms that violate their privacy, their reputation, or their chances of promotion or staying in their jobs. we have to ensure that the data of the workers belongs to the workers, even after the employment relationship has ended. the development of this technology must be free from discriminatory bias and the reproduction of stereotypes of gender, race, class or sexual preference. this is just a first step and should be followed by specific legislation on labour issues.
mr president, madam commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. first of all, i would like to highlight the role played here today by the european parliament's plenary session. many months of work by different committees of the european parliament are reflected here today in an intense, technical and competent debate by different committees. i therefore think it is important that the commission listens to the european parliament and makes clear the proposals that we are bringing to the european commission through the various reports that the european parliament is presenting. so you will agree with me that the european union has changed a lot from 1985 to 2020. that is why i want to emphasize the directive on product liability, which deals with that year. but society has changed so much that this directive does not at any time mention mobile phones, computers or teleworking, which are fundamental elements in the european economy, in the way we relate to one another and in the way we work today in the 21st century. it is therefore very important for european socialists to highlight this 21st century update. it is true that around 50% of european product manufacturers are now using artificial intelligence in their product development processes; and it is also true that a large proportion of european citizens do not feel that artificial intelligence is entirely trustworthy in their day-to-day use. we have therefore, as legislators, the challenge of being able to work on a directive which gives confidence to the european institutions, but above all to our citizens. that safety must be safeguarded, no doubt, by the safety of many of our products. and, as has been said here too, it is essential that this burden of proof does not fall on the consumer. it is true that we need to empower our european consumers. we need european consumers to be more aware of everything that has to do with new technologies, but they cannot be held responsible for what should be the producers.
mr president, this is a historic moment, the first time that the plenary of the european parliament has been asked to speak on helsinki and on the best topic yet: digitalisation and artificial intelligence. the current coronavirus pandemic has meant a major digital shift across europe, and some of these changes will surely be permanent. at the same time, it has also highlighted the very large disparities between regions and people and demonstrated that we need to invest more in high-speed modern infrastructure and connectivity, in people's digital skills and that we also need to invest more in digital services and research and innovation in this area in order to be competitive in the future. this is also a message for the financial negotiations now under way. further investment is needed in modern high-speed connections, digital skills of people and research and product development in the sector. it is the way europe can remain competitive and successful in the future. but apart from investment and innovation, we must ensure that we have legislation to support new innovation and to encourage the introduction of new technologies and new applications. at the same time, we must ensure that good consumer protection and fundamental human rights are also realised in the digital world, and it is important for us to find the right balance in legislation now. we must not over-regulate digital services or artificial intelligence, but we must ensure that consumer and business confidence in digital services is maintained. this means that we must reform the regulation on platform liability and we must also create our own european ethical principles for artificial intelligence.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, tomorrow's vote in parliament lays the foundations for modern digital standards by reforming the e-commerce directive which dates back to 2000, well before the power of the big platforms, when online commerce was much less widespread. at the same time, we will also have the first fruits of the work on artificial intelligence that we have started together with the european commission, by defining a general framework on the ethical aspects of civil liability and intellectual property, which are fundamental issues to ensure the confidence of citizens in this technology and to give legal certainty to companies that use it. with the adoption of these reports, we are establishing a genuine european model for the digital world, based on the centrality of people and technology, on our fundamental rights and freedoms, on democracy itself. a framework that ensures the protection of citizens and consumers, but also the promotion of innovation and excellence, in order to take full advantage of the great opportunities offered by new technologies and thus minimise the risks to our society. i am particularly pleased with the specific focus on counterfeiting in electronic commerce that i have worked on, by enshrining the principle 'what is illegal offline is illegal online' and by opening up the mandatory nature of some now voluntary provisions on this matter, we will be even better able to protect european consumers and our businesses from the proliferation of fake and dangerous products, a trend that has unfortunately increased in scale during the covid-19 pandemic. let us therefore continue this work for an inclusive europe at the forefront of the digital transformation.
mr president, commissioner, i have to ask you to leave. it's time to accelerate when it comes to digital technologies. the covid-19 pandemic has shown how important this area is for all of us in this difficult situation. we certainly need less bureaucracy and more digitization. so we should be looking at innovation in this new perspective, and there is absolutely no doubt about that. but with the rise of digitalisation and access to online services, we also need to pay close attention to protecting children from online abuse. and this is an absolute priority for me, because we know that this practice is unfortunately very widespread in the european union. and i would also like to point out that when designing solutions, we must keep in mind the balance between, on the one hand, the need for security and, on the other hand, protection against censorship on the internet, and also the appropriate safeguards in terms of freedom of expression and the right to information.
mr president, intellectual property protection is an increasingly important issue in the age of artificial intelligence. it is clear that the importance of mental work will continue to grow, and its protection will become increasingly important. until recently, the concept of intellectual property was more or less clear. someone made something, or created something, and if someone else copied it without permission, that was breaking the law. however, in the age of digital technologies, more and more uncertainties are entering this area. we have to answer questions that would have recently been classified more as science fiction. when artificial intelligence can create, who will actually own the results of that creation? who will be responsible for the autonomous decisions of the machines? what if the ai starts creating itself? it may seem premature, but the pace of development is enormous, and sooner or later we will face these dilemmas. and by then, we need to have clear rules and clear legal frameworks in place. the main principle that we have to remember is that technology always serves the human and society and not the other way around. no matter how complex a machine or program is, it is always the product of human thought and work. that's why there's always a human being at the end of the decision. just as humans can always decide whether or not to use ai, humans must have the final say in the decision-making process. only then can we ensure that ai serves us, improves our processes and enhances our services.
mr president, the debate we are having is based on the evidence that technologies have had and are having a dramatic impact on business models and, at the same time, on the position of consumers and users' rights. we cannot renounce what these technologies mean, nor submit to them as passive subjects. we therefore need a secure environment from the legal point of view, from the technological point of view and from the point of view of the protection that consumers expect. and so, first of all, there needs to be real harmonisation in national legislation, clear rules on liability and a clear statement of the principle that what is illegal offline is also illegal online. we must certainly involve the service companies so that they can cooperate and provide conditions of transparency and legality. i mean, it's true that technology creates challenges, but technology itself should also help to respond to them. we therefore need to rely on current and future technology as a tool to address the specific threats and risks of the online world. we understand that we should not prohibit, but should regulate according to the maturity of each of the technologies. and finally, let me say that we need to maintain sectoral legislation to deal with the specific challenges and threats that exist in the digital world. among them is the regulation of terrorist content after terrorism has again attacked an innocent victim of freedom in europe.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, if we were to ask workers, businessmen, the elderly, young people and even the sick at this time of covid, what do they expect from europe, what do they expect from the commission, what do they expect from the european parliament, do they expect concrete action to save lives and to defend jobs? and that is precisely the direction our agriculture can take if it is not penalized by european measures. with european resources we can defend jobs in agriculture and the agricultural chain, with european actions we can defend agri-food products, agri-food value, our agriculture and defend people's lives. and instead europe after the table bugs -- some people thought it was a joke in the past, the table bugs in europe, the table bugs in italy -- now they're thinking about how to make synthetic meat more valuable. so instead of promoting the italian and european agri-food systems, we are thinking about how to deal with the synthetic meat lobby. it is madness, i understand that there is a billion euro business behind synthetic meat, but my invitation is to continue to hold the bar and call steak, sausage, scallop, hamburger what is a derivative of the meat chain, real animal meat and not as someone would like to deceive, with the complicity of europe, deceive consumers. i think that this is the fundamental issue and the direction we should be going in. do not mislead consumers, do not offend the european agri-food system and the italian agri-food system, do not go in the direction of nutri-score, do not go in the direction of using names that are related to meat to promote synthetic meat. no to measures that reward synthetic meat, laboratory meat, that reward the wasteful multinationals, but concrete, economic and direct measures that reward our agriculture.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. in fact, today, the industrialized parts of agriculture are the main part of the climate problem. and agriculture has such great potential to be part of the climate solution. with agroecological methods, with methods of organic farming, we can save co2 and bind it to our soils. even if the social democrats try to gloss over a bad outcome of the negotiations, it remains a missed opportunity to take a decisive stand against climate change, the climate crisis and the biodiversity crisis. instead, we continue to see a reliance on the world market, which leads to farmer deaths and ruinous producer prices in the european union. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the common organization of the market in cereals, which is to be adopted by the council on 1 january, is a very good one. so keep the millions going to the oligarchs, to the financial investors, to the big landowners and to the agricultural industry! this is a blow to farming and a dark day for climate and environmental protection.
mr president, in the last few weeks i have received many e-mails from environmental ngos asking for more support and ambition for the green architecture of the cap. my answer is and will always be the same: if agriculture is not a profitable and competitive activity, environmental protection will be a utopia. we have to be ambitious, but we also have to be very responsible. all of us in this house must be realistic and show that we have common sense. i cannot honestly support the direction of this new cap. i refuse to be complicit, because we are creating a bureaucratic monster, because i see no will to support young farmers with adequate funds and, above all, because implicitly giving up food production for the sake of the environment will leave our farmers and consumers completely unprotected against imports from third countries.
mr president, we often get into debates about acronyms in the european parliament, but we forget what is behind them. the cap is not just an acronym. the cap is supported by millions of farmers and herders throughout europe; it is supported by millions of citizens who are willing to give up their lives in the countryside to support their families and to build a life project. the cap is the result of the efforts of generations, the pride of parents in their achievements over many years and the hope that their children will be able to continue to earn a living in the countryside. that is why this week's vote is not just any vote. this week we are deciding on the future of those millions of farmers and their families and on the quality products at reasonable prices that they work with and that they produce. despite the budget cut the council gave to farmers last july, there are still those who want to impose more burdens and demands on them. the people's party is the party of the spanish countryside and that is why we will never abandon our farmers. we will not tolerate a radical change, as some have proposed, to a model which has been so successful as the cap so far. the common agricultural policy has been the cornerstone of the european construction for decades, and we in the people's party believe that it should remain so. otherwise, a europe without a strong cap will not only destroy the future of millions of farmers, it will simply be much less europe.
mr president, the cap directly affects the lives of millions of farmers in the european union, and indirectly we all benefit from the fruits of the field and its ecosystems. the fight today is the fight for territory and for life. the cap is a cohesion tool and now it must also reinforce the green transformation facing europe. we reject the idea that farmers are subsidized passive subjects. i'm not sure. farmers feed us, they keep rural areas alive, and they are a climate change containment dam. some political groups have come here to defend the old model with a green wrapper. but the cap of this time is fairer and more sustainable. that is why we are calling for the first hectares to be subsidised, because two thirds of farms in the european union have five hectares or less. that is why we want to have a ceiling on aid, because only 2% of the beneficiaries receive more than 60 000 euros. that is why we are advocating real and significant environmental spending for young people and women. that is why we want strong conditionality, not just green but labour, which guarantees the rights of farmers, livestock farmers, workers and temporary workers. we are committed to a united europe in which the way we farm, raise, produce, trade and consume is transformed. today begins the birth of much of who we will be.
in writing. the 2021 work programme is closely linked to the recovery plan for europe, the next generation eu recovery facility and a strengthened eu budget for 2021-2027. as socialists, we stress the need for the recovery plan and the mfp not to be paralysed because they are crucial to economic recovery. the european health union is also an urgent matter which must be properly implemented. we must accelerate the digital and green transitions by ensuring that they are fair, effective and sustainable. we welcome the commission's commitment to present an action plan to ensure the full implementation of the european pillar of social rights, but we need to fight poverty and protect european workers vulnerable to the digital transformation. the european union needs tax fairness across europe and flexibility in tax rules for several years; measures such as the european unemployment reinsurance scheme must be really implemented. we will emerge from this crisis through solidarity: solidarity between people, regions and member states, with migrants who need safe and legal pathways. fighting for our values: for true gender equality and the rule of law.

in the name of the gue/ngl group. madam president, since august belarus has been experiencing a massive social mobilization against election results which many consider to be fraudulent. we have seen a spiral of arrests of protesters, calls for lukashenko's resignation and an increase in tensions at the borders of the european union. we will always defend the right of anyone, anywhere, to protest against abuses, always linked to the demand that each people is sovereign to determine its destiny without external interference. that is why we do not tolerate or subordinate human rights and democracy to geopolitical interests or invoke them selectively for one country and not for another. this week we are celebrating the triumph of the bolivian people, despite a coup d'état that lasted more than a year and multiple impediments. and we look with hope to the plebiscite in chile, which was achieved precisely by popular mobilizations. we need in belarus, as in other countries in conflict, more than ever, dialogue and diplomacy, to prevent further escalation, demanding an immediate stop to arrests, but also to interested interference.
on behalf of the green group, madam president, europe must act in the face of police violence in the states. many of these abuses are racially motivated, such as those perpetrated against the roma people. but they also happen during demonstrations of various kinds. in catalonia, for example, police brutality has been directed especially against the catalan republican movement, leaving physical and psychological wounds that are difficult to heal. in addition, there are reports of links between various european police forces and far-right groups. the police must not, under any circumstances, violate human rights. on the contrary, you must protect them. we therefore propose the creation of an independent system for monitoring police violence within the rule of law mechanism or the revision of the directive on the protection of victims. the union must recognise the need to monitor police conduct, ensure accountability and end police violence in order to protect the rights and freedoms of all citizens.

a third country, with or without an agreement. the position of brussels has always been, throughout these years of negotiations, to punish the british people for their choice by making the break as painful and as complex as possible for london. after trying to tie the uk to the common market with the backstop, the commission is still trying to keep the uk on a short leash by imposing on it, for example, the case law of the european court of human rights or by wanting to keep a watch on its state aid policy. but the truth is, your inflexibility never paid off. boris johnson had demonstrated the fragility of this so-called intransigence at the end of 2019, obtaining, just after his election, the renegotiation of the agreement that had been concluded with theresa may and which you claimed already said that it is in favour of concessions and you yourself, mr barnier, recently suggested that we should, and i quote, 'show some realism on access to british waters'. so it will either be weakness or no deal, no agreement. and i certainly do not welcome the prospect of failure, but contrary to what has been said for the last four years, the consequences of a no deal would probably be far worse for the european union than for the united kingdom. you have taken the problem in reverse, instead of negotiating a global agreement by imposing your ideological dogmas, the general on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, with regard to climate, those were very poor council conclusions indeed. or can i even call them conclusions, because the only thing that leaders could agree on was that they had actually talked about climate. we are in the midst of a rapidly progressing climate crisis and postponement of action is a mistake. this year again looks set to break any sort of records with regard to climate crisis, be it heat waves, droughts, intensity, extreme weather events, arctic shield loss, etc. so, let me be very clear. deciding to talk about climate later is failing to grasp the urgency of this task. we have to act now and we have to act decisively at all levels and also in all policy areas, and the compromise reached by the european parliament on reducing greenhouse gases by 60% by 2030 is a good step forward. some of you have said it’s an ambitious target, but it’s not so ambitious: it’s a more realistic target if we really want to save our planet. member states should sign up to it quickly – to the 60% goal – so we can finally start acting. the climate doesn’t wait and neither should we. and yes, the economic transformation needed for that is no walk in the park, especially for regions with a long-standing tradition of carbon-intensive economies. that’s why the parliament is insisting on having a proper-sized just transition fund, among others, as part of a new eu budget, because you need to put the money where your policy targets is. you need to put the money where you want to have the eu fit for the tasks ahead and that requires adequate funding. any government should know that. we need to have a climate spending target of 30% and a biodiversity target of 10% in order to be up to the tasks ahead. and eu funds also need to advance our union values. that’s why compliance with rule of law must be an essential criteria for allocating the funds and these are critical elements in the parliament’s position on the eu brexit was decided, but now we need to see that we also ensure the union’s togetherness. we are again holding this meeting under very exceptional circumstances. we’re in the midst of a big, big mr. president, ladies and gentlemen, none of us will go down in history, but the coronavirus will. so why do we treat it as if it's a problem that we don't all have or that we'll soon stop having? how are we so irresponsible? the virus is faster, more effective, more consistent than our measures to prevent and control it, and the coming of winter will only increase its advantage over us. but we politicians continue to confuse reality with wishful thinking: as much as we want this nightmare to end, it's not going to end unless we treat it as the health, economic and social emergency that it is. we were wrong when we pretended that summer would be over and we were wrong again by telling the lie that the vaccine will arrive before christmas. we're submitting vaccination plans and we don't have any vaccine. we must prepare for a struggle that will last for years. in spain, for example, the virus has killed 50,000 people in just eight months. there are one million infected; three hundred thousand people are infected every month. what are we doing arguing about other things? mr president, ladies and gentlemen of the council, do you not think it is time to have a common strategy to combat the pandemic? how many more people have to die in europe? the lives and health of millions of people on our continent are at stake, the collapse of health systems, the survival of our small and mediumsized business fabric, the ability of millions of families to afford food, clothing or heating. that is why it is even less understood that, in these circumstances, boris johnson is burning the bridges linking him to the continent, condemning the future of the british economy to the impossible. if donald trump loses the election, boris johnson will have no friends left in the world. let us be responsible, let us stand united behind our negotiator and ask our negotiator to negotiate on behalf of all, for the good of the british people too. in this day and age, collaboration is survival.

ruined the good faith between the negotiators. and here we are in the midst of a second wave of the corona crisis with all its economic consequences, and the uk government, for reasons no one can really grasp, decided to walk away from the negotiation table one minute before midnight, because we do not need a deal in 73 days: we need it within two weeks. only then will this parliament be able to ensure the agreement’s full democratic scrutiny. because, president, let this be very clear to the uk government: since the beginning of the transition period, the european parliament has madam president, fellow members of the house of representatives, the future of the union depends on whether, in the current crisis, we can re-create the economic backdrop of the european model of which we are so proud. as a responsible decision-maker, you must first secure the economic foundations. the european semester is the compass for this work. only then can we achieve our ambitious goals, such as the digital transition or climate action, and only then can we return to growth, preserve jobs and create new ones. this strategy can't exist without rules. even in the current pandemic and crisis, they have provided member states with sufficient flexibility to increase financial freedom. in the course of the negotiations, i saw that the main objective of the left is to break down the essence of economic policy coordination and fiscal rules one by one; the people's party is not a partner in this. the left wants to see the instrument of coordination between member states, the semester for crisis prevention and management, which we established during the hungarian presidency, filled with all kinds of fashionable topics. if the left succeeds in realizing its vision, then we can say goodbye to competitiveness. there's no such thing as a free lunch. if we funded all the left-wing ideas, it would trigger a spending spree that would eventually lead to the elimination of jobs. we cannot allow our economic policy to become a tangle of competing goals. these ideas were voted down by the economic committee, and the left is now trying to smuggle them back with amendments. irresponsible economic policies would jeopardise the union's recovery and put future generations into debt. hungarian citizens have learned what communism is like under the socialist-liberal governments and they don't want it again. and our liberal colleagues are already thinking of a european federation with a common finance minister, which would significantly reduce the powers of the member states. this is contrary to the treaties and would upset the balance between the member states and brussels in political and economic policy coordination. in times of crisis, such thinking will rob you of time and energy to do what is important, not help you to get back on your feet. so i ask my fellow mps not to vote for the amendments, not to be partners in irresponsible economic policy ideas.
madam president, the past financial crisis and the answer the eu has given show how cuts in public services had a negative impact on our economic, environmental and social recovery. no progress in poverty reduction and social inclusion shows that many of our social welfare systems are not robust and resilient enough to absorb social shocks during a crisis and also transition. this is why we socialists and democrats urge member states to stop considering public services as a cost but, on the contrary, as an investment to guarantee a decent life for all of us in the union. and a decent life means wellbeing that includes access to quality education and jobs, as well as social security, healthcare, housing, justice and social services, and also especially for vulnerable groups. to achieve this, it’s important to change the mind-set of many eu leaders and to come forward with a model that puts social and environmental challenges on an equal footing with economic ones. this is the only way to align our eu policies with the three dimensions of the sdgs and to avoid a lost generation. if we want our next generation in europe to be strong and resilient, we must invest in their future and therefore must consider child guarantee and youth guarantee amongst the most precious instruments we have. dear colleagues, child guarantee and youth guarantee can only be successful if we allocate sufficient financial means and if we are able to show creativity when it comes to shaping them and implementing them. let me finish by thanking ms dobrev and mr schuster and the shadows for their excellent reports.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, as long as green investments are seen as mere deficits without future benefits, we will deny european countries a solidary and green future for a more viable and liveable society. of course, i agree that economic policy coordination is necessary, but the current rules do not distinguish between spending to maintain a system that is out of breath and spending to build a future for ourselves and our children. a new approach is therefore essential to enhance green investments. new indicators that serve the real economy and our planet must be taken into account in the calculation of deficits. let us exclude, among other things, the expenditure linked to the ecological transition and, above all, let us stop using indicators dating from the 1930s, whose obsolescence has been demonstrated by the health crisis. the public services, the ones that serve our planet, are not deficits, they are the real economy.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is unacceptable in the european union to have 100 million citizens at risk of poverty and 700 000 people without a home. we cannot remain indifferent, we must act and each of us must do our part. the european union provides many resources, such as the european social fund, the youth guarantee, followed by the child guarantee. they must be put to good use and made available, in particular to social solidarity institutions such as ipss and misericordie. but then we have to go to the causes, promote individual autonomy, help social ascension. that is how we give back dignity to our citizens, and for that we have to bet on the economy, which has to be resilient, has to be competitive, has to be sustainable. we can't be afraid of the words productivity and sustainability, and the entrepreneurship that's required. we must also help businesses, especially small and mediumsized enterprises. and that is why, commissioner, it is unacceptable that the council has cut a solvency instrument precisely to support companies in difficulty. it is unacceptable that the council has cut investeu, which promotes investment. everyone has to do their part. the european commission has done its part, and parliament has done its part. member states not all. the council has not done its job to fight poverty, to promote inclusion, to help small and mediumsized enterprises, and i hope that an instrument like sure will be transformed from temporary to permanent and that it will also be an instrument in which the european parliament is also involved. we are, of course, in favour of the community method.
madam president, covid-19 is striking us with a dramatic balance of loss of life and millions of jobs. the european union has assumed its responsibility and is responding to the enormous and constant needs. it is now time for the member states to make a particular effort in each country to strengthen our national health systems and to promote economic recovery and employment. employment is the best social policy to combat poverty. but we must also be vigilant that, in the chaos of the crisis, partisan agendas do not take precedence over the common and collective good. the pillars of the union must be kept intact by a firm defence of the separation of powers. and the european funds are there to strengthen us. that is the way to go and not undermine the rule of law. to ensure that european aid is used properly, it is therefore essential that all levels of government, national, regional and local, are involved responsibly, together with the social partners, under the effective coordination of the european union. in spain, we must act immediately, using the funds to support sectors that have been brutally punished, such as tourism, hospitality and services. the spanish government should focus on implementing active policies to alleviate the 41% youth unemployment and apply tax cuts, abandoning restrictive policies aimed at maintaining the huge non-productive structural cost. the spanish people and the people of europe are looking at us with greater expectations than ever before, expecting us to come up with responsible solutions for the future, and to do so immediately.
i am speaking on behalf of the gue/ngl group. madam president, for years, those of you who governed the eu have told us that europe had a strong economic and social model that could do anything. however, it has taken 15 days of the pandemic to show our weakness. fortunately, we have been able to push through an agreement on this occasion for the essential recovery fund, which can be distributed fairly or can benefit them again. why do we say this? because europe is a place of enormous asymmetries and usually only a few benefit from public resources. this is particularly serious in sectors such as industry, where we often see relocations that have taken advantage of subsidies, condemned thousands of workers to unemployment and left us without the capacity to produce what we need. an example of this is here in spain: alcoa. however, we believe that the crisis can be an opportunity to change things. the pandemic has shown that only public control can guarantee the well-being of all. so we need an industry that is subject to public control and planning and, of course, that responds to the challenges of the climate emergency and creates quality jobs. that is why, mr gentiloni, i take this opportunity to ask you, in these days when the future of airbus' workforce is being negotiated, to focus on what is important and to make an effort to ensure that the way out of this crisis is to defend strategic sectors such as aerospace. let us unwaveringly support the working women who are the real creators of wealth in europe. let us make an effort to demand that european funds serve to secure quality employment and the future of our people.
madam speaker, just as there is a consensus among virologists to restrict our individual freedoms and try to limit the progress of the pandemic, there is also a consensus among economists that governments need to increase their leeway and spend a lot of money now, on a timely basis, to prevent the economy from collapsing during a time as complicated as the current one. that is why, from europe, we have agreed on this recovery fund of 750 billion euros, which will enable all countries to have a cushion and help in getting their economies to recover and rebuild in the best possible way. this means that governments have much more room for manoeuvre than without it, but this very opportunity also has some obvious risks. we must prevent governments from using this european money to build new elites of their own, to build client networks that together manage to perpetuate themselves in power. this money - european funds - should in no way be money to finance a long election campaign for any government. that's why it's so important that now that we're negotiating the recovery and resilience facility regulation with the governments and the groups in the european parliament, that we put in place clear and demanding rules to ensure that the money is used for the good of the europeans, that we put in place those controls to ensure that it's not used to build those client networks or to run that long election campaign. the prestige and future of the european union also depend on this money being spent well. if we do not, in five or six years' time people will see this as a failed experiment and we will never see this solidarity between europeans again.
madam president, my message is this: we will not achieve a genuine recovery and a socially sustainable europe without measures adapted to the tragic situation we are living in, which deal with its consequences not only economically but also socially. we therefore need to invest in measures and funds for permanent recovery, governed by indicators, within the european semester, which guarantee investment in welfare and social protection systems, putting the principles of the social pillar into action. only with a multiannual financial framework and an ambitious recovery fund focused equally on economic growth aspects and social objectives can we move towards a truly sustainable europe that invests in what really matters: people. the covid-19 crisis is affecting our health, living conditions and, above all, employment. we therefore also need a commitment from all the social partners and businesses to commit themselves to maintaining economic activity and employment in return for the aid they receive, avoiding business relocation processes that lead to mass redundancies. the idea is very simple: a socially sustainable europe means investing in public welfare systems. the welfare state, like the rule of law, is a hallmark of our european union, and we must continue to strive for it.
on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, global deforestation is a problem that unites us, and yet reports like the one we are discussing divide us. we cannot use the fight against deforestation to attack data-free biotechnology or start the transition to a vegan paradise. we cannot stigmatize a crop like soybeans, which is essential for feeding our livestock and which comes mainly from the united states of america, a country where there is no deforestation. i think it is counterproductive to ridicule the efforts of the sector through certification or voluntary agreements. only through realistic targets and loyal cooperation with the sector will we achieve the objective of reducing deforestation outside the european union. i'd like to be very clear. we must ban all imports of products from deforested areas into the european union, but let us not focus the debate exclusively on latin america: deforestation is much greater in parts of asia and africa, which we are not talking about.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, today's debate on this report is very important in the fight against global deforestation and i want to congratulate delara burkhardt because i think she has done an excellent job. we have adopted a report on the climate emergency in this parliament, we have also adopted the climate law to achieve neutrality and i believe that today we are taking another step towards making the european parliament the most environmentally friendly institution in the world, a house which is at the forefront of the fight against the effects of climate change. ladies and gentlemen, we are talking about them this afternoon, but what do forests mean? forests are unique ecosystems that are home to 60,000 different tree species, 80% of amphibian species, 75% of bird species and 68% of mammal species on earth. they are natural carbon sinks of incalculable value in basic natural processes such as air and water purification, carbon absorption or temperature regulation. this is what the woods mean. how do we protect ourselves? commissioner, under article 225 of the treaty on the functioning of the european union, it is clear that there will be a legislative proposal. i hope he'll introduce us to her soon. you can tell that to the president, ursula von der leyen. we must protect forest ecosystems, and i do not want to forget natural ecosystems, wetlands. we need to include binding sustainable development chapters in free trade agreements such as mercosur, thus fulfilling the paris agreement and the 2030 agenda for sustainable development. mrs burkhard, i congratulate you on paragraph 46 of the report. so, ladies and gentlemen, we are in the sixth mass extinction of species. the holocene can end at any moment if we continue like this, so let us use this momentum, the momentum of this afternoon and of this report to protect and preserve ecosystems, biodiversity and forests.
mr. president, yesterday they were forests and jungles of rugged thickness, where the mist floated and the fountain gushed out, today they are arid hills that have deformed and black deep fissures. this is what rosalía de castro, a galician poet, said about the destruction of forests. and it's true, every forest has a story, stories of protecting the human being. by dispossessing the forests, we are protecting ourselves. that is what this report is about and i would also like to congratulate mrs delara burkhardt on that. because this report helps us fight deforestation everywhere on the planet by setting the rules of the game for our consumption patterns. we need better regulation. we also need to protect the primeval forests, the primitive, the indigenous. not just outside the european union. here too we are suffering from deforestation and the replacement by other agro-industrial production models. that's why we have to defend ourselves here, too. and to remember that cutting down a forest, dispossessing it, is fuelling climate change and is also causing the flight of its inhabitants, not only of those biodiversities but also of humans. the european union will therefore be well placed to call for regulations that protect forests at all levels.
the importance of this report on gender equality in the european union's foreign and security policy, for which i congratulate ernest urtasun, is mainly due to three issues. firstly, women are still underrepresented in the representation and management of the community's external policy. second, differentiated socialization, the historical linking of the feminist movement with the pacifist movement has always meant that women's participation in conflict resolution has been more linked to life, more linked to peace. this differentiated approach is therefore necessary. and third, because these conflicts affect us women unequally, and we need to have our voices heard. this day coincides with the 20th anniversary of the united nations resolution 1325 on women, peace and security. and 20 years later, if we look at what is happening in belarus, we see that women are still approaching conflict resolution in a different way, much more peaceful, embodying the values of democracy and harmony that we want to remain the values that represent the european union. it is therefore very necessary that we make progress precisely in the inclusion of gender equality in our foreign policy.

mr. president, i have to ask you... today we're talking about women, their indispensable role in conflict resolution and peacebuilding. the debate is very important to strengthen gender equality in foreign policy, including in the response to the coronavirus epidemic. women and girls are the most vulnerable to violence worldwide, and the european union must do much more. we call on the high representative of the union to invest more funds to promote the training of women to become more involved in humanitarian situations. sex should not be an obstacle. in fact, women and girls in crisis areas are often subjected to sexual violence, which is also used as a military tactic. women can no longer be victims, peace lasts much longer when women are included in peace negotiations. today, less than 10% of them are. we must ensure parity, we must guarantee them conditions if we are to be true champions of women's rights in europe. we want binding action, and this year marks the anniversary of the beijing conference and the un resolution on women's rights.
mr president, women remain largely unrepresented and undervalued in politics and the decision—making process in the eu and beyond. nonetheless, the european union has committed to the united nations sustainable development goal to promote and achieve gender equality. this also counts for our foreign and security policy. aside from the aspect of equal representation, women’s participation also has an added value. studies show that our contribution to peace negotiations has led to long—lasting peace agreements and improved human security. we can see some progress as almost all eu delegations have carried out a detailed gender analysis and the number of women deployed to security and defence missions and operations has increased, but we are still far from reaching a real balance. as the ppe shadow rapporteur for the committee on foreign affairs (afet) opinion to this report, i welcome the guidelines on gender mainstreaming for eu civilian missions and call to extend this concept to all other relevant missions, departments and delegations related to our foreign policy. only in this way can we fully promote recognition of the significant role women play at the local, national and international levels. to conclude, i would like to call on the high representative and the commission to intensify their actions to reach our ambition of a gender balance by the end of the mandate. gender mainstreaming requires not only high—level policy statements but also a real political commitment.
member of the commission. – mr president, i want to thank members for this very, very good and interesting discussion. women’s meaningful participation in all matters related to foreign and security policy has a wider relevance to the central role of the european union in contributing to efforts to achieve peace, security and sustainable development. the action plan will enable us to walk that talk by putting resources where our priorities are, including by reiterating our commitment that 85% of all new eu actions promote gender equality as a significant or principal objective. i would also like to re-confirm that the commission and the external action service attach great importance to ensuring equal opportunities and diversity of staff in management positions and we are already taking concrete steps to this end. within the commission, the gender equality strategy adopted earlier this year gives concrete steps to reach 50-50 equal gender balance in management positions at the commission. at the external action service, the work is jointly done with the member states as, by nominating one third of the staff, they have a direct contribution to our objective. today, men hold 66% of middle management positions and 74% of senior management posts, while almost 27% of the heads of delegations are women. although this is an improvement, i fully agree with the members on the need to further promote gender balance in the eu’s external representation and reach full equality. this will be addressed as part of the gender action plan (gap) iii, and work is already ongoing to work towards a similar target as for the commission. mr president, putting women and young people in the driving seat of the recovery: i strongly believe this will be the most effective way to collectively respond to the global challenges that we are facing worldwide at this very critical moment of our human history. rapporteur. – mr president, i would like to start by thanking everybody who contributed to our debate. i think that the debate showed that there is a broad majority in parliament behind what we are going to adopt tomorrow, and this is extremely good news. i would also like to thank the commissioner for her words and for being with us. hannah neumann and i liked a lot her mention that she wants to build a gender—responsive and geopolitical commission. we liked that approach a lot. i think this is one of the good conclusions of our debates. but i think in general that we are all worried about the backlash on women’s rights that is happening all around the world, and that is really something that needs to be an additional motivation for us, to push for this feminist foreign policy that we all want. three things before i come to an end. firstly, as you mentioned, madam commissioner, i think that the neighbourhood, development and international cooperation instrument (ndici) regulation is going to be an important tool to achieve gender equality and an important instrument for us. i hope that in the negotiations the targets on gender equality will remain as high as proposed. this is one of the key issues in the coming months. secondly, i think it’s important to say that the commitment to improve the presence of women both in the eeas, in the commission, and in our delegations is an important target. i would like to remind the house that the high representative has committed to the 40% target of eu heads of delegation by the end of the term. i think that it should be a joint goal to achieve that, and so i’m very much looking for the actions that that you will be undertaking and, of course, we will be very attentive to the presentation of gap iii in november, which is going to be the most important tool that we will have to achieve all of these goals. so, all in all, i just want to conclude by thanking everybody, thanking the commissioner. let’s make a gender—responsive commission and gender—responsive eu foreign and security policy a reality.
the debate is closed. the vote on the amendments will take place today, 22 october 2020, and the final vote will take place tomorrow, 23 october 2020.
the next item on the agenda is the statement by the commission on serious security threats through the sale of eu passports and visas to criminals (2020/2824 ((rsp)). i would like to inform you that there will be no catch-the-eye procedure for all the debates in this part-session and no blue card questions and observations on the application of the rules of procedure will be accepted. it also provides for possible remote assistance from parliament's liaison offices in the member states.
member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all, i would like to reiterate what commission president ursula von der leyen said forcefully last month in her state of the union address to this parliament: european values are not for sale. unfortunately, this statement does not seem as clear as it should be everywhere in the union. some member states have in fact set up systems allowing wealthy investors and their family members to obtain a passport in exchange for the payment of a large sum of money or a substantial investment, without having a real link with the member state in question. such programmes have clear implications for all other member states and for the union as a whole. becoming a national of a member state automatically gives you citizenship of the union and all the rights attached to it. a foreign investor who acquires the nationality of a member state of the union can, on this basis, immediately go to any other member state, engage in economic activity there and benefit from the principle of non-discrimination between european citizens in a number of areas. they may even participate in the democratic process, in particular by exercising their right to vote and stand as candidates in european or municipal elections. for several years now, these programmes have been a source of great concern, notably for this house and the commission. the commission has worked hard on this matter, as it is also a european issue with european values at stake. in january 2019, the commission published its first-ever report on investor citizenship schemes and investor residence schemes operated in the eu. the report clearly spelled out the inherent risks of such schemes, in particular as regards security, money laundering, tax evasion and corruption. since the beginning of this commission's mandate, we have continuously raised this concern with the member states concerned, urging them to reconsider their decision to operate such schemes. since the beginning of my mandate, i have had a constant dialogue with the relevant member states where i repeatedly highlighted the commission's concerns and urged them to reconsider their decision to operate such schemes. before the pandemic, i met personally with the ministers of the relevant member states to discuss investor citizenship schemes. in april, i followed up with letters asking to reconsider the schemes. i also had several calls with the competent ministers to follow up on their commitments to provide more information on their government's plan. while both malta and cyprus may have the intention to apply some changes to their investor citizenship schemes, they appear to be determined to continue their schemes in some form. we need to convince them not to do that. ladies and gentlemen, a series of recent scandals in cyprus and malta only confirm the serious concerns we have already expressed. in its report, the commission undertook to monitor the compliance of the existing schemes with union law and, if necessary, to take the necessary measures. today i am here to tell you that the commission is delivering on that commitment. the commission decided this week to open infringement proceedings against the citizenship by investment programmes set up by cyprus and malta. eu citizenship is a collection of shared rights that all member states provide in solidarity. together, the member states create the conditions for the enjoyment of eu citizenship. the european court of justice has been clear: it is for each member state to lay down the conditions for the acquisition and loss of nationality. but, as the court has also ruled, this power must be exercised having due regard for eu law. eu law requires member states to recognise the nationality of another member state without imposing any other conditions. as a result, the decision to naturalize a person by a member state is not limited to its own jurisdiction and is not neutral with respect to other member states. member states should therefore use their prerogatives to grant nationality with due regard for eu law and, in particular, the principle of sincere cooperation which is laid down in the treaties. a member state fails to fulfil that obligation when it establishes a scheme which systematically grants naturalisation in the absence of any real link with the state and on the sole basis of a financial investment or, in other words, where passports are for sale. over the span of only a couple of years, cyprus and malta have naturalised several thousand persons based on their schemes. the price for a european passport in those member states ranges between eur 1 million and eur 2 million. these investor citizenship schemes undermine the mutual trust of member states in each other's naturalisation procedures. this trust is a precondition for the effective enjoyment of eu citizenship rights. by undermining this trust, these schemes threaten the integrity of eu citizenship and its proper functioning and violate the principle of sincere cooperation. ladies and gentlemen, malta seems to have committed itself to replacing the current regime with a new programme, on largely unchanged a priori bases. in cyprus, while the government has announced the end of the current regime in the near future, the establishment of a new programme is already being discussed. but the issue is too important to wait for the process to be completed. we are now taking action to show our determination and commitment not to accept that citizenship of the union is marketed as a mere convenience. i think we can all agree on one important point: the principles and values of the union are weakened if membership of the european project, the rights deriving from it and participation in its democratic life are merely an economic transaction. let me also add a couple of words on bulgaria, which also operates an investor citizenship scheme. this week, the commission sent a letter to the bulgarian authorities strongly urging them to phase out their scheme in the context of an ongoing national legislative procedure to change the bulgarian citizenship law. we have also asked for additional information on the scheme. the commission will not hesitate to consider an infringement procedure against bulgaria if necessary. finally, the commission has not forgotten about schemes that give foreign investors access to residence permits, also called golden visas. while golden visa schemes do not raise the same legal issues as schemes that result in the acquisition of eu citizenship, there is often a close interaction between the two types of scheme, as residence can be a prerequisite for the acquisition of nationality. most of the inherent risks of such schemes, for example regarding security, money laundering and corruption, are the same. third-country nationals with a valid eu residence permit can travel freely, in particular in the schengen area. this means that if one member state does not apply the necessary security checks, other member states can be negatively affected. member states should therefore ensure that all necessary steps are taken to prevent security, corruption, tax evasion and money laundering risks. they must also apply rigorous and transparent criteria to identify and combat crime and corruption. the commission has expressed its concern about the risks inherent in residence schemes and about the fact that these risks are not always sufficiently mitigated by the measures taken by member states. in this context, the commission continues to closely monitor these schemes and their application, to ensure compliance with eu law, including the need to carry out all mandatory border and security checks, in line with the eu acquis prior to the issuance of any permit. the same applies to citizenship schemes operated by third countries, including some candidate countries, which enjoy visa-free access to the union. such access should not be used as a marketing tool to attract money in exchange for citizenship. following the adoption of its january 2019 report, the commission requested information and raised concerns with all visa-exempt third countries that are implementing or planning citizenship by investment programmes, in close cooperation with the european external action service. as a result, some countries have already abolished their schemes and others have ended their plans. we will continue to monitor the matter closely to assess any potential security risks associated with these schemes. i am glad that i was able to explain the commission's policy to your parliament this morning and i would be very happy to take note of your comments and answer your questions.
on behalf of the renew group. – mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner for coming here and for starting the infringement procedures. i think that is most welcome and we knew that it had been in the pipeline for quite some time. however, this house has been debating the problems with golden passport since 2014. why did it take so long? why did the authorities wait so long to intervene? why did we have to wait for brave journalists to uncover the scandals that we all suspected for a long time? and some of these journalists have paid a very high price – the highest price, as we know. but journalists are not responsible for law enforcement. the authorities are, and the authorities are failing. those who have a legitimate interest in investing in the european economy can do so without problem, and no golden passport nor golden visa scheme can ever really be clean and legitimate – because due diligence that relies on cooperation with the russian and the chinese and the saudi authorities, frankly, can never be watertight – and i’m being polite here. despite the fancy names of the schemes – you know ‘residency by investment’ sounds nice, evokes these images of villas and yachts, and what have you – in reality, they are just a golden backdoor into the european union for criminals, criminals. it’s not investment; it is criminal money. now, we need a full ban, and we’re calling on the european commission, not for the first time, to propose legislation, and i’m proud to say that at the initiative of my group renew europe, the committee for civil liberties, justice and home affairs (libe) committee is going to draft a legislative initiative calling for a ban on golden passports. but of course this is a national competence. you know, national governments cannot sell what is not theirs to sell, and that is eu citizenship. it isn’t theirs, they cannot sell it. so are governments, is the council finally also going to take responsibility, or will they continue to knowingly facilitate criminals? the final question, commissioner: will you also address the ‘legacy’ of those who have already received a cypriot or maltese or other passport? will there be a second screening to see what kind of people we have actually elevated to the status of eu citizen?
on behalf of the id group. mr president, first of all, i should like to say that it is always a pleasure for me to speak from the strasbourg parliament, where i hope we will soon be allowed to return regularly to hold our plenary sessions. is it any wonder that the cyprus golden visa scandal has been triggered, since the costly acquisition of citizenship and residence permits could only serve fraudulent purposes? the fact that montenegro, once again the provider of such facilities, continues to be in the lead for the next european enlargement demonstrates the commission's indifference to a tangible security threat. the eu has also been active in the fight against fraud, and has been involved in the fight against money laundering and the financing of terrorism. the union therefore regrets the effects of the causes which it cherishes. firstly, its support for the interventionist states and, in this case, for the avowed supporters of radical islam. however, these visas are only part of a lucrative business that fuels the surge in migration and terrorism. the possible sale of bosnian visas to pakistani migrants, when two bosnian nationals were suspected of terrorism, or the profile of the fake minor who carried out the paris attacks, are examples of this.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the period of validity of the directive to the period of 1 january 1999. the commission is not the european passport is one of the most tangible results of european cooperation. it gives rights to all who have it, and therefore this right and this passport cannot be sold. eu passports decide who has access to our citizenship and who does not. and if that decision is made on the basis of the purse and not on the basis of who is integrated into our societies, who works here, who lives here, then the principle of european law is not in force, but the principle of the mafia. and that is why it is not acceptable that civil rights are put up for sale. it is not possible for one country to decide who gets access to it, but then to open up the rights in all eu countries. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. and that is why it is unacceptable that the investments worth over five billion euros, and often this leads to citizenship. and that is why we must not close our eyes. i would therefore ask you, commissioner, not to adopt a double standard. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, for several years now the european passport and golden visa business has been a well-known and growing phenomenon, and the cyprus scandal is only the latest in a long series. we have long allowed various member states to profit from citizenship and residence rights, allowing them to put a price on them, to exchange them for investments by wealthy foreign nationals. this market, which moves billions of euros and in which there are no common standard rules, is also exploited by criminals and corrupt actors who want to launder enormous amounts of money, often through illicit activities, by taking advantage of privileged tax regimes. the european institutions have for years expressed concerns about national residence and citizenship programmes for investors in the european union, but they only react decisively when scandals are the subject of investigative journalism, as was the case recently with the news of the launch of infringement proceedings against cyprus and malta. the european union cannot tolerate the selling of rights and values, which allows criminals from third countries to obtain passports or visas to move freely within the european territory, with serious consequences for internal security and leading to serious shortcomings in the fight against organised crime, money laundering, corruption, tax evasion and avoidance. the european union has a duty to put an end to the serious shortcomings of these programmes in terms of transparency and strict controls, safeguarding trust and the principle of loyal cooperation between member states and protecting europe's borders from criminals with financial resources.
president, golden passport, it almost sounds sympathetic, a bit like the golden wrappers from shakie and the chocolate factory. but in that book, roald dahl warns us that undesirable people with a lot of money can simply appropriate such a ticket, with all the consequences that follow. that's annoying for a chocolate factory. it is simply dangerous for a european union. so let us stop with the euphemism, golden passport, and let us just call the beast by its name. not a golden passport but a fake one! a rich russian or chinese who spends his dubious money and registers at a petrol station in malta is suddenly an eu citizen, with all the privileges and benefits that go with it. benefits and privileges for rich chinese and russians, for rich criminals, but the risks are for all of us: security risks, money laundering, tax evasion, corruption, circumvention of eu rules, risks that we have been warning about in this house for years. it is therefore good that the european commission is finally taking action. better late than never, so to speak. but infringement proceedings against two small member states are not enough! we really need strict and clear rules for the future to ensure that this kind of fraudulent passports are also effectively made impossible throughout the eu. the eu passport, that is the main conclusion, is a privilege and not a commodity. it's time we all got down to business.
mr president, over the years, millions risked their lives to gain an eu passport. if they only had 20 million! in fact, for that money, cyprus would have welcomed them with open arms. in fact, cyprus welcomed some of the very same people responsible for others fleeing their home. they welcomed the cousin of assad, they welcomed the associates of hun sen, the dictator of cambodia, and they welcomed known criminals and gave them safe access to the entire eu, so the eu is fully justified in taking action, and i applaud the commission’s infringement procedure. better late than never. but these alone won’t do the trick. criminals looking for a safe haven in the eu will find our weakest spots: today, cyprus and malta, tomorrow, montenegro and austria. this is why we need clear minimum requirements for citizenships and for residency. this means an end to fast—track procedures. this means – at least – strong anti—money laundering safeguards to ensure funds were legally obtained and this means clear requirements for the kind of investments allowed, investments that indeed require you to actually be here. most of all, instead of handing passports to criminals, i’d say we should save our passports for their victims.
mr president, they say that good news is not news, but when it comes to a decision that makes things more difficult for the big evasions and criminals, to me, of course, it seems like very good news. and i want it known in my country, spain, that the european commission is finally going to open infringement proceedings against cyprus and malta for selling passports to non-community nationals. for those of us in the house who were part of the previous legislature of the panama papers commission of inquiry, this is not only good news but necessary. it is not just a game of advantage to get money by selling the residence to the highest bidder or opening the door to citizenship of a country that combines european citizenship and freedom of movement and business. it is wrong, it is disloyal, it is a haven for friends of dictators and criminals, it poisons our democracies and facilitates networks of corruption. the so-called cyprus papers refer to convicted fraudsters and money launderers, public figures and politicians accused of corruption. no excuses: we need data to be checked. and of course it is essential to launch a review of suspicious awards, to review national systems of accelerated investment awards, especially those where there is no requirement of physical residence. and if bulgaria has to be apprehended and if it has to be sanctioned, so be it; and if my country has to be sanctioned, so be it. commissioner, to clear the playing field, you have our support.
mr president, welcome to this debate to expose the incoherence of the commission. because on the one hand, they are opening cases against hungary, poland and the czech republic for not accepting false refugees, who then behead professors in france, and yet they are opening cases against cyprus and malta for granting visas to investors who have no connection with the european union. i welcome the genuine links with europe, but apply it to all immigration policy. because what genuine links do the thousands of immigrants who are crammed onto the shores of algeria, libya or morocco have with europe? 2,600 illegals have been smuggled by the mafia in the last two weeks to the canary islands, more than in the whole of last year. stop looking at everything in economic and financial terms. stop saying that europeans need thousands of immigrants to pay for pensions, because that's not true. nations are not money. nations are spiritual, family and cultural bonds of historical communities and must be respected. we don't want visas for speculators, drug dealers and mobsters. but we also do not want visas for those young soldiers who come to europe to commit crimes and fight us in the streets.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, commissioner, the energy union is crucial and important for our economy and our transition to climate neutrality and, to make it a reality, europe must commit to resolving the bottleneck it has. there is no union without interconnection and, for example, the iberian peninsula is isolated in this respect. we see that the emission reduction targets for 2020 are going to be met. let us hope that this is not a mirage also derived from the pandemic and let us remember that we still have 34 million europeans who suffer from energy poverty and cannot adequately heat their homes. the report also shows that we are losing ground in research and development in europe compared to other blocs; we must promote them, we are reducing the number of patents. commissioner, parliament has just set its commitment to a 60% reduction, we have a strong will. i believe that the commission's work programme is ambitious in order to make progress on the green deal. we have all things pretty much in place; let's push them a little bit further and not be overcome by skepticism or the economic damage that this pandemic is doing.
in writing. the state of the energy union is a key instrument for measuring our progress towards cleaner energy, enabling us to meet the decarbonisation commitments agreed in the european climate law for 2030 and 2050. the state of the energy union allows us to analyse the weaknesses of our energy system and, above all, to show where we need to invest more. the new recovery and cohesion funds should be a key tool to ensure better energy efficiency in buildings, to invest in sustainable mobility plans and to reduce the use of fossil fuels by individuals and industry, through progressive electrification and investment in alternative sources such as green hydrogen and renewable energy. it is time to engage civil society, foster innovation and public-private partnerships to achieve greater energy independence and a greener and more digital europe. recovery funds, together with programmes such as the cef or the just transition fund, offer a unique opportunity to address the challenges of the future without leaving anyone behind.
mr president, we are approaching the final stretch of this budget, of this political instrument which will determine our lives for years to come. and while there are positive aspects to it, such as the reversal of cuts in education or research programmes, we are also concerned that it continues to allocate an immense amount of money to the militarisation of the eu, too much money to frontex. it is obvious that this is not our ideal proposal, but perhaps it is time to have a debate on how to manage it. in my opinion, it is clear: we must do everything possible to ensure democratic control; to ensure that public services and employment are strengthened so that no one is vulnerable either to a pandemic or to a lack of work; to make resources serve to recover productive sovereignty with public control. if the pandemic has shown us anything, it is our weakness: not a single paracetamol is produced in the eu. but above all, let us not deceive ourselves. if we allow the austerity rules to creep in, as the right wishes, and if we do not provide social conditionality and fair distribution, those who are spitting out their hateful project for europe from their seats will win.
in writing. the agreement reached this week with the council for the next multiannual financial framework (mff) 2021-27 after months of negotiations is a historic success: for the first time since the mff came into being, the european parliament has managed to increase the amount of money initially agreed by the council. we have achieved an ambitious, progressive and people-centred multiannual budget. we have defended the 15 core eu programmes and ensured that none of their beneficiaries are left out of the new financial framework. we have also secured funding for the green deal and for a just ecological transition; we have defended the people and put the socialist stamp on this long-term budget, which is now greener, fairer and more social. the agreement reached also lays down a road map for the introduction of new own resources for the european union, which will allow us to finance our programmes on the one hand, and on the other to have a fairer tax system, which will oblige the big technology companies and the big multinationals, the most polluting companies and those who make financial transactions for speculation purposes only, to pay taxes in europe.
