in writing. i voted in favour of the agreement reached this week with the council for the next multiannual financial framework (mff) 2021-27 because it is a historic success: for the first time the european parliament has managed to increase the amount of investments initially agreed by the council, and it has been more than eur 15 billion. the agreement is an achievement of the s&d group in preserving the eu's ability to invest beyond the recovery plan. we have increased funding for strategic programmes (horizon, eu4health, erasmus+, creative europe, rights and values, humanitarian aid...) and secured funding for a just ecological transition. in addition, this agreement sets out a roadmap for the introduction of new eu own resources (non-recycled plastic and border carbon tax in 2021, digital tax in 2023, ttf and harmonised corporate tax in 2026) which, together with the issuance of federal public debt to finance the recovery plan, make possible the temporary financial union to become a permanent union in the future, being one of the keys to making full political union possible.
in writing. the agreement reached this week with the council for the next multiannual financial framework (mff) 2021-27 after months of negotiations is a historic success: for the first time since the mff came into being, the european parliament has managed to increase the amount of money initially agreed by the council. we have achieved an ambitious, progressive and people-centred multiannual budget. we have defended the 15 core eu programmes and ensured that none of their beneficiaries are left out of the new financial framework. we have also secured funding for the green deal and for a just ecological transition; we have defended the people and put the socialist stamp on this long-term budget, which is now greener, fairer and more social. the agreement reached also lays down a road map for the introduction of new own resources for the european union, which will allow us to finance our programmes on the one hand, and on the other to have a fairer tax system, which will oblige the big technology companies and the big multinationals, the most polluting companies and those who make financial transactions for speculation purposes only, to pay taxes in europe.
mr president, we are more confident today, after congratulating parliament's negotiators, especially eider gardiazabal and margarida marques, on reaching an agreement in which parliament has for the first time succeeded in increasing the figures proposed by the council and which also allows the new own resources to finance the flagship programmes in the budget. we are indeed pleased that this has been achieved, from development cooperation, from humanitarian aid, as the alternative rapporteur for the deve committee's opinion, but we cannot stop here. we need a commitment also for the 2021 budget. we therefore wish the rapporteur, mr pierre larrouturou, good luck in the conciliation period and hope that he will also reach a good agreement with the council. we have already been tricked once at the july council, when we lost the 5 billion euro for humanitarian aid. in the face of the tremendous crises that are coming, crises that are becoming more numerous, crises that are becoming more intense, we need to strengthen development cooperation, to strengthen humanitarian aid. the funds must therefore respond in a way that is proportionate to the global crises we are facing, and we must demand that if the european union wants to be a global player, if it wants to guarantee security in europe, it must step up its external action and its humanitarian aid. this is a clear message to face the negotiation with the council of the 2021 budget.
author. madam president, commissioner reynders, you know that i had the opportunity just yesterday to remind you that this institution - this european parliament - is the only directly elected institution in the union: it represents the citizens and therefore also expresses their concerns and fears in the face of what is proving to be the worst crisis in the history of the union. unexpectedly, when the citizens responded to the electoral call with an increase, at last, in participation, they were unaware that we were plunging into a crisis that has impacted on all our vital constants. but the european parliament, representing the citizens, has responded from the very beginning. we adopted a resolution in april, another in may and another in june on the impact that the emergency measures taken by member states in the face of the covid-19 pandemic and its ravages were having on what i believe is the most precious asset of the european experience: the free movement of people without restrictions at the union's internal borders. and in line with this concern, we are raising an oral question, which is equivalent to what in parliamentary law in many member states is considered to be an interpellation followed by a resolution. and here is the resolution: 23 recitals and 23 other paragraphs in which we demand that the member states and the commission live up to it, with a clear message: the rule of law, democracy and fundamental rights matter; law matters, law matters. it's not only important all the time, but it's particularly important in times of crisis, because a crisis challenges us to be who we say we are; it can bring out the best in us, but it can also bring out the worst in us. and it's very important that at this point, when the whole of 2020 has been consumed by the response to the pandemic, that we start to draw some lessons, draw some conclusions. and the first is that the law is here to be taken seriously, and this conveys a message that has to do with the principles of necessity and proportionality but, above all, of temporary limitation, time frame, temporary limitation when measures are taken that may have an impact on the rule of law, democracy, the quality of democracy that we are building in the european union, its member states and which is a constitutive principle, article 2 of the european experience, and the fundamental rights of citizens. and this resolution fully reviews the impact that these emergency measures have had on the fundamental rights of citizens, first of all those enshrined in the charter. first, the free movement of persons. there is a schengen borders code; there is a directive on free movement, which has been ignored, if not scrapped, in the rush to adopt emergency measures. and the commission, as guardian of the treaties, is responsible for ensuring that member states respect the principles of proportionality and necessity in the restrictions imposed on free movement and for their temporary limitation, so that, as soon as possible, those restrictions are lifted. we also deal with the fundamental rights enshrined in the charter: freedom of expression - including the expression of dissent against some of the measures adopted by the member states - protest, assembly - right of assembly - right to demonstrate. there are certainly rights that are contingent on emergency measures, that are contingent on the need to safeguard public health, but they have not been extinguished or disappeared from the charter of fundamental rights. therefore, measures that impact on those rights must also be proportionate and limited in time. we are particularly concerned about the free flow of ideas, because if we deal in this european parliament with disinformation, we must deal even more when that toxic disinformation causes harm to people's health; disinformation aimed at confusing or questioning scientific knowledge and generating a state of fear and anxiety that is easily exploited by demagogues and populists, who fish in the river of the citizens' fear of the impact of the pandemic. it is therefore very important that the european union adopts the necessary strategies to defend itself against cyberattacks, against cybercrime, but also against disinformation. we are particularly concerned about the impact of the emergency measures on criminal law and prison law: disproportionate, repressive, disproportionate penalties, not based on the principles of penal law; and we are equally concerned about the impact on democracy and parliamentary and judicial control. those are the recommendations of the venice commission, those are the recommendations of the european court of human rights and the court of justice, which cannot be ignored or overruled, even in times of pandemics. we therefore call on the member states to draw lessons from this, to ensure that where emergency measures have been taken, they are consistent with their constitutional arrangements, and that where there is no prior provision, specific legislation is now adopted to enable us to respond; and on the commission to monitor and ensure continuously that the member states' responses are in line with the rule of law, democracy and fundamental rights.
member of the commission. madam president, i would like to thank ladies and gentlemen, i would like to begin by thanking you for your commitment to the impact of the emergency measures taken in the context of the fight against covid-19 on democracy, fundamental rights and the rule of law. beyond its health and economic impact, the crisis that has hit us has created a whole series of challenges for our societies, including, as has just been recalled, for the fundamental values of the union. all member states have taken exceptional measures to protect public health, which have resulted in restrictions on certain fundamental rights and freedoms. most member states have declared a form of state of emergency or granted special emergency powers. this has been done either under constitutional provisions or under legislation on the protection of public health. changing or suspending the normal balance of power at national level can indeed pose particular problems for the rule of law. the crisis has proved to be a stress test for assessing the resilience of national systems in times of crisis. the commission has insisted from the outset that measures taken in response to the crisis must respect our fundamental values, as enshrined in the treaties. in particular, emergency measures must be limited to what is necessary, strictly proportionate, clearly limited in time and in accordance with constitutional guarantees and european and international standards. in addition, governments must ensure that measures are subject to regular parliamentary scrutiny, in full respect of democratic balances. these key requirements were reflected following the commission's proposal in the country-specific recommendations adopted by the council in the context of the european semester this year. the commission has been proactively monitoring the emergency measures taken in all the member states. the first annual report on the rule of law highlighted the need to maintain established checks and balances, including the role of parliaments in particular, when the measures taken affect fundamental rights. the resilience of the justice systems was also put to the test. a number of member states have taken measures to reduce the impact of the pandemic and were able to restart hearings applying distancing rules or videoconferencing techniques. the covid-19 pandemic has shown the importance of accelerating reforms to digitise the handling of cases by the judicial institutions, the exchange of information and documents with parties and lawyers, and continued and easy access to justice for all. we will continue to discuss with the member states about a real investment in the digitisation of the justice systems in all the member states. on data protection, the commission has already provided guidance and recommendations to member states, in particular on apps supporting the fight against the covid-19 pandemic. the european interoperability gateway for contact-tracing apps is now operational. it allows people to receive alerts and declare infections, also when abroad, using their home country's app. it ensures a high level of personal data protection and security. the commission has continued to monitor the situation and tried to receive a positive response from all member states to take part in such an interoperability gateway. it is not already the case with all the member states, but we are making progress. however, national data protection supervisory authorities and national courts and tribunals are at the forefront of this area, as they have the primary responsibility for ensuring compliance with the rules. these authorities are responsible for advising national legislators and for ensuring that all operators, public or private, comply with the law. they coordinate within the european data protection board and the commission has regular exchanges with the european data protection board. the crisis has also had an impact on democracy, electoral processes and freedom of expression. the covid-19 pandemic has indeed been accompanied by an unprecedented flood of false or inaccurate information that can create confusion and distrust, undermine an effective public health response and have a negative impact on democratic institutions. in june this year, the commission adopted a communication on disinformation during the pandemic and getting the facts right. the upcoming democracy action plan will address further challenges along three main pillars: electoral integrity, a free and plural media and combating disinformation. closely linked to this action plan, in december the commission will also issue its eu citizenship report which will present initiatives to promote the enjoyment by eu citizens of their civic rights under eu law. by the end of the year, the commission will also publish a new strategy on the implementation of the eu charter of fundamental rights and we will come with the first annual report on the new strategy next year before parliament. with regard to the right to free movement, a right cherished by many eu citizens, the principles of proportionality and non-discrimination must always be respected when restrictions are being considered. since march 2020, the commission has adopted a number of guidelines and communications, and following a commission proposal the council adopted on 13 october a recommendation on a coordinated approach to the restrictions on free movement in response to the covid-19 pandemic you know the common map that we now have published by the ecdc (european centre for disease prevention and control) every week and we also try to fight against all possible forms of discrimination also with a dialogue between the commission and the member states. just to give an example, in the past months we have seen since the end of august a possible discrimination in hungary with controls at the border except for travellers coming from the other three visegrad countries from the czech republic, slovakia and poland. i have had many contacts with the hungarian authorities and at the end of october they extended the measures on border control but without any exceptions now for the three visegrad countries. so the dialogue between the commission and the member states is also very important to try to avoid some kind of discrimination. thank you again for this opportunity to explain what we have been doing since the beginning of the crisis, since march, in the different fields and, of course, we will continue to do that. oversight by the judicial systems and oversight by the parliaments, and also by the european parliament is very important in the crisis and i look forward to this debate and to hearing your views on this important matter.
on behalf of the ppe group madam president, governments have put in place extraordinary measures to contain the pandemic. in general, these measures have entailed restrictions on fundamental rights. the problem is not whether confinement or curfew is necessary - that is for scientists - but whether such limitations on individual freedom are adopted in accordance with the guarantees of the rule of law. but i wonder today whether this parliament, closed and remote, is in a position to remind other open parliaments of the importance of defending the democratic principle in the face of quarantines. yes, i am concerned that, under the umbrella of the health emergency, we ourselves are resigning from the democratic emergency. i am concerned that hundreds of our colleagues are not able to speak in this house because they do not live in the capital of their country and are not allowed to travel; that we have elite members who can speak here and second-class members; that we do not have interpretation in all the languages. i am concerned that our agendas are becoming more and more insufficient, our speaking times are getting shorter and that we are voting on the financial framework, the recovery plan or brexit without amendments or any real debate. i am concerned that all parliaments, including belgium's, are open and that the european parliament, instead of being the most transparent and guaranteeing its members safe working conditions, is the only one that chooses to close its doors and point the finger at its members as if they were to blame for the contagion. if we remain closed and distant when we are most needed, it is no wonder that the public will soon be asking what the european parliament is for.

mr president, commissioner, i am pleased that we have a debate in this house this afternoon on such an important subject as the sustainability of the single market, a subject which certainly needs to be topical in a situation where the pandemic has shown that we have to change our consumption habits and our ways of relating to each other. so today it is fundamental where we produce and where and how we consume. so from the point of view of industries, it is important that the european union takes action on where we are going to produce and how we are going to be able to increase sustainability throughout the value chain, from the design to the distribution of products. and, in relation to consumers, i think it is important to give a figure, and that is that in the last eurobarometer, in a survey that has been carried out, more than 77 per cent of europeans are willing to reuse their products and try to fix them and usually do so. this should make us think that european culture, european citizens are demanding a better way of consuming, a more sustainable way of consuming. and as the european parliament, together with the various institutions, we must be able to give an answer to these citizens. the socialist group therefore believes that it is important to ban everything that has to do with the planned obsolescence of products. it is important that consumers are not misled. it is also important that there be a labelling that informs about the durability and how to resolve a defect of any type of product. and finally, also - and i don't want to miss this - it is important that the public administration takes note, that public procurement is much more social and more sustainable.
the european commission published the new consumer agenda on 13 november, which aims to put consumers at the centre of the digital and ecological transitions as the driving force behind the recovery from this crisis. the covid-19 crisis has brought to light the vulnerability of consumers to counterfeit products, cancellation of trips, online scams, etc. this agenda takes note of the situations that have occurred during this pandemic and sets out the roadmap to be followed during the next five years. consumption accounts for 50% of europe's gdp, so it is essential to safeguard consumers and to try to give them a high level of protection. the new consumer agenda covers five key areas. these are: green transition, digital transformation, respect for consumer rights, protection of the needs of vulnerable consumers and international cooperation. with the rise of electronic commerce, we must protect citizens who shop online. the commission therefore plans to develop an action plan with china in 2021 to improve the safety of products sold online.

in writing. in the midst of the covid-19 crisis, europe is once again looking to industry as a driver of change, competitiveness and quality employment. europe is facing the transformation towards industry 4.0 at a time of change and must demonstrate that it is capable of leading not only the green and digital transitions, but also to strive for technological leadership and open strategic autonomy. only through a new industrial strategy, which respects the importance of smes, innovation centres and universities, can we build a leading european economy that is capable of strengthening supply chains, relocating value chains to europe and increasing security of supply of strategic raw materials. only through public-private partnerships will we be able to create innovation ecosystems that are anchored in the competitive advantages of each of europe's regions. we need to move towards a circular economy, we need to invest in alternative energy sources - such as hydrogen - to achieve the climate targets we have set for ourselves, and we also need to invest in skills and education to ensure that all europeans are prepared to face the major change that this new industrial strategy will bring.

madam president, on the occasion of the recent international day to end impunity for crimes against journalists, it is necessary to remember the 1 628 media workers and journalists who have been killed over the last twenty years simply for exercising their right to freedom of the press. according to the un, in nine out of ten cases the guilty are not prosecuted, thus undermining their societies by the impunity that covers up serious human rights abuses, corruption and crimes. independent journalism that keeps citizens informed and governors accountable is as crucial to a strong democracy as free and fair elections themselves. freedom of opinion is one of the inalienable values on which the european union is founded, enshrined in article 11 of its charter of fundamental rights. in addition, the protection of freedom of expression and media pluralism are among the priorities of the union and its member states in external relations. however, it is imperative to remain vigilant, both outside and within our borders, where these crimes also go unpunished. the pandemic presents challenges to fundamental rights around the world. it is clear that we must not justify limits on democracy with orwellian measures such as truth ministries, anti-hate laws or the promotion of censorship within our own continent or anywhere else. what will the commission do to prevent this?
madam president, commissioner, dear didier, i would like to propose a round-the-world trip on the subject of the lack of freedom of the press. in latin america, there has been a terrible increase in the repression of journalists who criticize both government corruption and inequitable social and environmental policies. the asia pacific region is the most dangerous region for journalists and bloggers. we had the opportunity, in the committee on human rights, to welcome mrs ressa, the founder of the rappler news site in the philippines, who was accused of cyber-defamation, just for having disseminated information. in vietnam, the situation is also not very clear. blogger nguyen trung and freelance journalist pham dung were arrested just as we ratified the eu-vietnam trade agreement. in sub-saharan africa, freedom of expression is under attack again, as in nigeria, where there have been 115 cases of abuse against journalists this year. the situation in the middle east, with the murder of khashoggi, or in north africa, with the arrest of khaled drareni in algeria, is not very clear, nor is it very encouraging. in bangladesh, several journalists and bloggers, such as shahidul alam, have been jailed and tortured under the draconian digital security act of 2018. i could continue this express world tour by stopping in europe, for example, where, as has already been said, the situation is not very good either. so, commissioner, violence against journalists is not only an attack on the physical and moral integrity of those who inform us, it is also an attack on the very foundations of our democracy and the rule of law. and so i'm not asking what we're doing to protect journalists, but how we're using our tools to make sure that these situations can no longer continue in countries with which we have ongoing dialogues.
madam president, journalists are seekers of truth and guardians of our most valued freedom of expression. their work worldwide is a major disturbance and irritation to those rejecting democratic principles of openness and transparency. freedom of opinion, expression and access to information constitute an essential part of the universal human rights index, which determines the quality of democracy worldwide. the extent and tendencies of crimes to silence journalists and to limit media freedom around the world is alarming and increasing. examples of crimes committed against journalists are disturbing – intimidation and harassment, as constantly faced by journalists in venezuela and russia; online harassment, as fought by pakistani women journalists; threats to family as incurred by iranian journalists working abroad; exile and expulsion as experienced by a congolese journalist, gaël mpoyo; abduction, as of dozens of journalists currently held hostage in syria; detention and imprisonment, like in china, which is the world’s biggest captor of journalists; physical attacks and torture like that suffered by a bolivian journalist, rene guarachi; and barbarian killing as with jamal khashoggi, a critic of the saudi arabian government. we need to ensure the safety of journalists by deterring and holding the perpetrators to account. we need to establish an effective framework by adopting the eu magnitsky act.

in writing. eu leaders met to discuss the first strategy for the equality of lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, non-binary, intersex and queer (lgbtiq) people, presented by the commission on 12 november. the strategy sets out a series of specific actions around the following four priorities: 1) fighting discrimination; 2) ensuring security, by extending the eu's list of 'crimes' to include hate crimes, and incitement to hatred against lgbtiq persons; 3) protecting the rights of rainbow families through a legislative initiative to support the mutual recognition of same-sex unions within member states; 4) promoting lgbtiq equality under the neighbourhood, development and international cooperation instrument (ndci), the instrument for pre-accession assistance (ipa) and the asylum and migration fund. i support this strategy with the aim of member states introducing and developing their own lgbtiq equality action plans, and ensuring better protection of lgbtiq people against discrimination.

in writing. eu leaders met to discuss the first strategy for the equality of lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, non-binary, intersex and queer (lgbtiq) people, presented by the commission on 12 november. the strategy sets out a series of specific actions around the following four priorities: 1) fighting discrimination; 2) ensuring security, by extending the eu's list of 'crimes' to include hate crimes, and incitement to hatred against lgbtiq persons; 3) protecting the rights of rainbow families through a legislative initiative to support the mutual recognition of same-sex unions within member states; 4) promoting lgbtiq equality under the neighbourhood, development and international cooperation instrument (ndci), the instrument for pre-accession assistance (ipa) and the asylum and migration fund. i support this strategy with the aim of member states introducing and developing their own lgbtiq equality action plans, and ensuring better protection of lgbtiq people against discrimination.
in writing. the last week of november marks a breakthrough in the work of the european parliament on human rights. for the first time, the commission has put forward a comprehensive strategy for equality for lgbt+ people. there is no doubt that these aspirations are directly derived from the eu's values - that all people must be equal, enjoy the right to live in harmony with themselves and cannot be discriminated against on the grounds of gender, sexual orientation, religion, origin or disability. it should therefore not be surprising that parliament is reacting at a time when lgbt+ free zones are being established in some member states and non-heterosexual teenagers are taking their own lives due to the intolerance of politicians and state media, which directly affects public attitudes. the objectives of the strategy are to combat discrimination, ensure safety and build an inclusive lgbt+ society, and promote lgbt+ equality around the world. we have a lot of work to do. in poland, for example, there is no category of hate crime based on sexual orientation, and education does not provide for the promotion of non-discrimination. ilga research shows that 70% of lgbt+ teens suffer from depression and suicidal thoughts. i believe that the strategy will be the beginning of building a common policy guaranteeing fundamental rights for all across the european union, regardless of their sexual orientation or gender identity.
in writing. it is shocking that the discrimination experienced by lgbtq people has increased in recent years in europe. we must do everything we can to ensure that no european is faced with fear and discrimination in their daily lives, whether in the workplace, on the streets or online. i would like to thank the commission for its new strategy on lgbtq. it is time that we safeguarded the right of all people to be free from discrimination. the inclusion of all hate speech and all hate crimes, including rainbow people, in the list of crimes established by the eu is a good thing. hate crimes and hate speech are on the rise and we need to put a stop to it. we must ensure that lgbtq people receive good, reliable, expert and confidential health care in every member state. we must also ensure that rights established in another country, such as custody and marriage, are recognised in each member state in cross-border situations, otherwise this is an obstacle to free movement.
in writing. the strategy raises a number of concerns. issues relating to social policy, including action to combat inequalities, social exclusion and discrimination, fall within the competences shared between the european union and the member states (articles 4 and 7 tfeu) and, therefore, the union is obliged to respect the principle of subsidiarity in its action in this area. issues of morals, sexual morality, family structure, parent-child relationships, and the extent of government interference in the intimate lives of citizens are deeply rooted in the cultures of the eu member states and vary widely. the strategy presented here aims directly at the uniformity of member states' cultures in terms of understanding of basic concepts such as gender or social institutions such as the family. in this respect, it infringes article 167 tfeu. there is no possibility, in accordance with the treaties, for the commission or parliament to arbitrarily interfere with the content of teaching, including the use of the term 'family' in the context of same-sex relationships. in accordance with article 167 (1) tfeu, the union is to respect the diversity of the cultures of the member states, while at the same time emphasising their common cultural heritage of judeo-christian origin. in accordance with article 167 (4) tfeu, the european union is under an obligation to promote the diversity of the cultures represented in it in all its activities under other provisions of the treaties.
the next item is the debate on the commission statement on the eu pharmaceutical strategy (2020/2878 ((rsp)). i would like to point out to all members that today, as in all the debates in this assembly, the agreed procedure applies, that we do not accept spontaneous speeches - i know that this is very difficult for some people - and that there are no blue cards, so as not to discriminate between the colleagues in the chamber and those who are connected by the eplo. the commission will also be able to make use of the european parliament's liaison offices in the member states.

member of the commission. – mr president, we are nearing the end of one of the most challenging and most consequential years in recent memory. first of all, as i look back on 2020, the feeling i have is of being heartbroken for so many lives that have been lost. but i also feel pride and i feel gratitude when i think of all the frontline workers and all the doctors and nurses who have sacrificed, how neighbours have rallied around each other, and how the european union came together to support citizens of member states. i’m also reminded of an old saying that those who do not learn from history are condemned to repeat it, and covid-19 has taught us some harsh lessons, lessons that we are still learning. it has reminded us of the foundational role that health plays in our societies and our economies. it has highlighted the vital importance of solidarity, and this has amplified structural vulnerabilities in our health system. the commission is determined to heed these lessons and take decisive action in partnership with the european parliament and member states. yesterday, we adopted an ambitious pharmaceutical strategy for europe. it is an important building block in a genuine european health union and i am truly delighted to have the opportunity to discuss it with you. this strategy addresses both the short-term challenges linked to covid-19 and the long-term challenges connected with unmet medical needs, europe’s strategic autonomy and sustainable health systems. i will share with you that this was a strategy that was in my mandate letter from president von der leyen one year ago. when i saw it there, i knew that we were faced with challenges with the pharmaceutical situation in europe, but i never imagined that we would be presenting it in the covid—19 crisis. it has changed a lot and we have adjusted many things in this strategy from what we have learned in this pandemic. it focuses on four key areas. first and foremost, improving patients’ access to affordable medicines. secondly, ensuring innovation to boost the potential of our pharmaceutical industry and increasing support for unmet medical needs of patients. this includes harnessing the benefits of emerging science and technology and reducing the environmental footprint. it is no good having innovation if you cannot have this reach patients. third, building strategic autonomy, developing resilient and diverse types of supply chains, and addressing the root causes of shortages of medicines. this includes the european health emergency response authority (hera), which will enable the eu and its member states to rapidly deploy the most advanced medical and other measures in the event of a health emergency. and finally, fourth, to promote eu influence and competitiveness at global level and ensure a level playing field for eu operators. so the pharmaceutical strategy aims to cover the whole life cycle of medicine, from research to clinical trials and authorisation, from consumption to safe disposal. honourable members, the current pharmaceutical legislation has served us well. however, we are now living in a vastly different reality. we have to take a holistic view of the pharmaceutical legislation and make the necessary improvements to ensure it is better adapted for today’s world. over the next two years, we will work hard on the reform of the pharmaceutical legislation. this reform will address all the angles i mentioned, but always putting the patient at the centre and always ensuring that our pharmaceutical industry maintains its global leadership. this applies to the framework for medicines, for children and rare diseases. however, this strategy is not just about changes to legislation. we will also simplify the regulatory procedure allowing quicker access to innovative and affordable medicines for patients, such as gene-editing technologies and personalised treatments; to facilitate the development of novel antibiotics and to combat antimicrobial resistance; to increase the uptake of generics and biosimilars. we will also create a health data space where regulators and the industry can exchange data for the benefit of patients and provide for simple solutions such as electronic product information. at the same time, we will revise our system of initiatives for research and development to ensure those incentives are fair and balanced while allowing the eu industry to remain innovative and competitive worldwide. we will also take action to make medicines more environmentally friendly. last, but definitely not least, we aim to address shortages of medicines by reinforcing the current obligations for supply, and require an early notification of interruption. in parallel, we will strengthen coordination with key stakeholders and industry and patient associations by establishing a structural dialogue to better understand vulnerabilities in the supply chain. i just want to end by saying a few words. there are thousands of european union citizens who are today suffering from rare diseases, and there are thousands of families who have children with cancer, and they do not have access to the medicines that we would want them to have that would give them a better quality of life and hope. the way forward with this is to encourage innovation and to make sure that no matter where you live, no matter which member state you belong to, you have equal access to safe and affordable and innovative medicines. the road is not going to be easy, and this is a strategy that is a vision, and it is a vision with actions that will be developing over the next four years. we already have a very buoyant, and i would say a front-runner, in the pharmaceutical industry in europe, but we can do better and we now have the opportunity, so it’s up to all of us – the european parliament, the council and definitely the commission. if we work together, we can bring together new legislation, but also other actions that are really going to change the realities for access to medicines for citizens across the member states, and this is a responsibility that we will definitely do our best to stand up to.
on behalf of the ppe group mr president, commissioner, one of the lessons of the covid-19 pandemic is that we have accelerated the construction of a european health union, and this new pharmaceutical strategy is an opportunity to cement one of the pillars of this new europe for health. with this strategy we must be able to tackle any pandemic or health threat, put the patient at the centre of all decisions, address unmet medical needs, ensure access to medicines for all, strengthen the competitiveness of european industry and make europe a world leader in research and healthcare. we must invest more in the fight against antimicrobial resistance and its rapid spread, which threatens europe with more than 33 000 deaths a year. today, there are more than 7,000 rare diseases, 95 percent of which are untreated. we must respond to patients and their families in the face of this great despair. we have a huge gap in pediatric drug research and development, and our kids come first. and we cannot allow a european cancer patient to have a difference of between 7% and 90% chance of accessing approved medicines. or that you have to wait 90 to 1,000 days for a drug to go on the market after it's been approved. the strategy should also strengthen the competitiveness of the pharmaceutical industry by fostering a stable and robust regulatory system, improving public procurement, streamlining approval procedures without sacrificing safety and facilitating the availability of quality medicines. the technological and digital transformation must also be a reality, using artificial intelligence to improve early detection and advance innovative and personalised treatments. and we must promote production in europe and our industrial autonomy so as not to be dependent on third countries. there are many challenges ahead of us and we must respond to each one. and to do that, we need the public and private sectors to work together. only then will we achieve the balance between innovation, accessibility and sustainability of national health systems. only then will we protect one of the great fundamental rights, the health of all, and hopefully for all.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, this covid-19 crisis has brought to light the main problems we have. these are problems of price, of relocation and of shortages of medicines and health products. the first thing i ask of you, commissioner, is to create a structure. in the strategy you talk about dialogue, but i think that we need a task force, a structure, so that there can be this work between patient associations, between administrations and also between the pharmaceutical industry. and i ask you to bet on genuine innovation, not incremental innovation, which is what we've been doing up until now and it doesn't solve our problems. we also need to assess the added therapeutic value of new innovative medicines and be a leader in r&d. i remind you that we have not had new antibiotics for over 30 years, commissioner. and finally, to point out that access to advanced therapies cannot be discriminatory. let's use this crisis to make a good strategy.
in writing. the adoption of the pharmaceutical strategy for europe is certainly a step forward in the construction of a european health union. the s&d group has long called for greater action to ensure access to safe, effective and affordable medicines and is pleased to see that the commission's proposal suggests concrete actions to achieve these objectives. the spanish socialists hope that the commission will create a structure in which there can be joint work between patient associations, administrations and the pharmaceutical industry. we also call for more investment in genuine, not incremental, research to assess the added therapeutic value of new innovative medicines and to make the eu a therapeutic r & d benchmark. the covid-19 pandemic should serve as a lesson for us to create a good pharmaceutical strategy. future measures should focus on patients, ensuring equal access to medicines and therapies for all european citizens. the ultimate aim is to ensure the health of all and to build a europe more resilient to future health crises.
madam president, commissioner, this 'mini-agreement' is a small step on a commercial level, but it is a big step on a political level to make progress in the normalisation of trade relations with the united states. it is a very important point, and i hope that the commission will take it up with us. the lack is obvious: lack of coherence. why would you do that? because while this commission is holding back and paralysing the agreement with mercosur, because it is questioning the environmental commitments of countries that signed the paris agreements, it is negotiating a trade agreement with a country that has directly abandoned them, as is the case with the united states. so respect for the paris agreement is a sine qua non condition when it comes to mercosur, but not when it comes to the united states? it's not consistent. the shortcoming: this agreement is a missed opportunity to correct an unfair situation for european products that suffer unilateral tariffs from the united states, as is the case for spanish table olives since 2017. commissioner, europe needs trade, trade based on rules, committed to the protection of the environment, sustainable development and human rights. but these values must be applicable and enforceable on all our partners equally. it also needs the commission to defend all european products equally, not just aluminium and steel but also olives. because sending conflicting messages with asymmetrical demands depending on who you are negotiating with is detrimental to and a serious threat to the international credibility of the european union. the same way that failure to defend all european products equally diminishes and threatens the citizens' life in their institutions. finally, the desire for a geopolitical europe, especially in the commercial field, requires momentum, leadership and commitment, but above all equity and consistency.

i am speaking on behalf of the gue/ngl group. madam president, only fools confuse value with price, said antonio machado. last week we broke the news that water is starting to trade on the wall street futures markets. the price has already been set: the price for speculating on a fundamental right, as pedro arrojo, the united nations rapporteur, denounced. today we celebrate the progress of this directive and its work on water quality, which will improve the lives of many europeans. but we fell short of protecting its value as a common good, far from the citizens' initiative, brought to this parliament and supported by over 1 800 000 people, which guaranteed access to this human right, recognised as such by the united nations. water belongs to everyone and is a public good, closely linked to all our lives, environments and livelihoods, and an essential component of public health. given the current climate change outlook, the available flows will continue to decrease. in the face of corruption problems and privatisation pressures, our water policy must be implemented from a strict commitment to public, integrated, participatory and sustainable management of rivers, lakes, wetlands and aquifers as well as water and sanitation services.
madam president, we finally have a revised water quality regulation for human consumption after 20 years, updating water quality standards with the latest scientific data, improving consumer information and promoting the consumption of tap water. but let's not kid ourselves: he has two big faults, two big problems. first, we have lost the opportunity to recognize water as a universal right again. we've been asked to do this by two million people, citizens, who signed the right to water initiative, who asked us to legislate to recognize universal and equitable access to safe and affordable water for all. but it hasn't. it could not be done in this parliament by the conservative groups, and it could not be done in the council either. but i say to them that access to water should not be promoted, it should not be improved; access to water, as a universal right, should be secured, guaranteed, recognized. on the other hand, it is true that the initiative affects the accessibility of water, its safety, its acceptability, but not its affordability. and there's also water poverty, not just energy poverty. because the resources may come, but if there's no money to meet the bills, we have the same problem. as regards the resolution on the application of water legislation, i am pleased to see that the gaps which still existed have disappeared and that it is therefore fit for purpose, making it clear that it does not need to be revised. i'm glad to hear that. now, water is a public good, you can't industrialize this sector any more. we cannot leave the field open to speculators with water use and exploitation rights.
mr president, this transitional regulation is an important achievement because, as we are immersed in this pandemic, we have been able to give legal certainty to farmers so that they can continue to receive cap aid during the years 2021 and 2022, until the entry into force of the new cap in january 2023. in the second part of the trilogues we have also achieved something very important, and that was to incorporate the 8 000 million from the recovery fund into the transitional regulation. finally, the transition will be for two years. it is a pity that the european commission has insisted throughout the negotiation process that this transitional period should be one year only, when it was obvious that the desirable minimum was two years. this may sound anecdotal, but it is a very partial view of agricultural reality. finally, i would like to highlight the achievements for the wine, fruit and vegetable sectors, which will enjoy greater flexibility under the single cap, and - and this is very important - the maintenance of the funds for the outermost regions. i should like to end by thanking the rapporteur, mrs katainen, her whole team and all those who have made it possible to bring this transitional regulation to fruition.
mr president, mr commissioner, the agri-food sector has been living since march in uncertainty because of the coronavirus - transport problems, temporary workers who could not cross the border, forced closure of hotels, national aid that did not arrive, etc. - all this added to the big questions faced on the sidelines of the pandemic, such as the impact of brexit, trump's tariffs or an increasingly restrictive environmental legislation. that is why today, with the adoption of the transitional regulation, parliament is seeking to provide certainty and confidence for the future: by 2023, european farmers and livestock farmers will be covered by the same rules as before for their cap funds. we also accelerated the allocation of the additional eur 7.5 billion from the recovery fund for rural areas, because europe cannot afford to leave anyone behind. we also include a fundamental change in article 167a, which will allow the regulation of the supply of olive oil in crisis situations - a historical demand of the sector in spain, and in andalusia in particular - and provides certainty in planting authorizations for the wine sector. and finally, we have managed to avoid the serious cut that some people wanted to make in the financial allocation for the posei programme, a measure of justice to protect agriculture in the outermost regions such as the canary islands. in short, this regulation has brought some calm in the midst of the storm, but we will continue to work to improve its future in the years to come.

i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. i wish we weren't having this debate, because that would mean we wouldn't be living the nightmare that we're suffering from covid. what we are experiencing is a real human tragedy in the form of a virus which has already killed over 476 000 europeans; a pandemic which has caused the death of over one and a half million people worldwide and has infected over 72 million people. and so i want to begin my speech by expressing my deepest sympathy for the dead, sending my warmest regards to all the relatives and friends and wishing a speedy recovery to all those infected. i also want to acknowledge the immense work of so many people who over these months have been and are on the front lines of the fight against the pandemic: to all of them, a heartfelt thank you for their immense dedication, sacrifice and professionalism. and it is precisely thanks to the immense work and talent of scientists and the investment of multinational pharmaceutical companies, supported by public institutions, that we can now say that there is hope because we have a vaccine. we'll soon be past this terrible pandemic, but what follows is almost as tragic. without a doubt, we are facing the greatest challenge we have faced since world war ii. the humanitarian and health tragedy is compounded by the terrible social and economic consequences we are already beginning to suffer: the collapse of our gross domestic product, the increase in debt and deficit, the closure of businesses, the destruction of jobs, the fall in consumption, uncertainty, since this nightmare is not yet over, but we are still immersed in it; macroeconomic data that are already translated into the needs and social emergencies of many of our compatriots and their families. in the face of all this tragedy, we should be proud of the european union's reaction. i believe that the european union and its institutions have risen to the challenge and have been able to give a rapid, effective, forceful and unanimous response since march and april, when it was responded to with rapid initiatives to speed up the member states' access to the funds they needed to be able to meet the needs of masks and health expenditure. it is in this context that the next generation eu programme is born, an unprecedented economic recovery plan - we are talking about 750 billion euros - and it is within this programme, the next generation eu programme, that we find the react-eu programme - which we are discussing today - a programme of 47 500 million euros. just as it was necessary to remember why this plan arose, it is equally essential to underline for what. the recovery plans and the injection of public money are not in themselves good news. let us not forget that public money does not fall from the sky, it comes from debt, which we or future generations will have to pay, or it comes from taxes, which we will undoubtedly have to pay. these are plans that must always be born in exceptional situations such as the one we are living in and with a series of concrete, measurable and evaluated objectives. and in my opinion, the plan we have presented today is a very good agreement. we have reached a good agreement - as i said - which clearly identifies the areas of investment, the objectives to be achieved, the sectors to be financed and the conditions to be met. without a doubt, a good road map to achieve a real recovery of our economy. what is react-eu? grants and loans to smes in crisis to support those who are the real protagonists of recovery: the self-employed and entrepreneurs; the entrepreneurs, the only ones capable of creating jobs, those jobs so needed; the small businesses that create community, which maintain much of the employment, often supported, at this moment, by family support. the state will not be the one to revive the economy or create jobs. the state has the opportunity to help those who can, entrepreneurs. we must not make the mistake of repeating the outdated interventionism of the past, and it is good that react-eu has approved aid for entrepreneurs and the selfemployed. in addition, this programme includes a clear commitment to something fundamental: training, digitisation, the energy transition and support for our severely punished health systems, all of which are pillars of a recovery not only in the short term, but a recovery understood as the construction of a path for the european economic and social future. this good agreement also includes the much-needed retroactivity from 1 february 2020 so that member states can use the funding from the moment the pandemic began. having one more year to invest thanks to the increase in investment eligibility until the end of 2023 will allow the rationality of the investment. there's no rush to spend the money. first, because we have not seen the end of the crisis and we have yet to see many companies sadly fail and restructure. so we have to keep in mind that we are looking at the medium term. there's no rush to spend money; it has to be spent well. and secondly, because it will be deliberate and will go where it can have a leverage effect, one of the characteristics of cohesion funding. the speed with which the european commission approved the investment within two weeks is very good news. we do not need the money to be in brussels, but on the ground, in the regions, in the municipalities, wherever it is needed. we therefore acknowledge the efforts of the institutions to shorten the approval periods throughout the process. implementation will be done through the existing cohesion policy rules and structures; there will be no additional red tape. and 100 per cent of the funds will be european funds; there will be no national funds. finally, i would like to thank all the political groups in the committee on regional development for the immense work they have done, for the effort we have made to get react-eu approved. it is a very good agreement for the citizens, it is a good agreement for entrepreneurs and businessmen and it will certainly help to create jobs.
on behalf of the renew group. madam president, today we can be very proud to be europeans. in the midst of the second wave of covid, with heartbreaking death and infection figures across europe and economic consequences still unpredictable, today we approve the first fund of the recovery package: the react-eu fund, with €47.5 billion for the countries that need it most. and if we often talk about the inefficiency of the european union, the rigidity of its procedures and how far it is from the citizens, today we are demonstrating the opposite: that the european union has acted quickly and that it has certainly lived up to the challenge, and that this is an example of european solidarity. because this is not going to be a fund to use. it will have unprecedented flexibility, no usual restrictions will be imposed and it will have a co-financing rate of one hundred per cent, so that it is the regional and local authorities who decide what this money can be used for until 2023. thanks to react-eu, small entrepreneurs, the hospitality industry, small businesses will be able to receive liquidity to cope with the serious losses they have had since the pandemic began, since february 2020. but you can also finance healthcare costs - beds, ppe, respirators - and of course support all the unemployed who need it. it's a breath of fresh air for the hardest-hit sectors. in countries like mine, like spain, where forecasts indicate that one in ten businesses will have to close, that gdp will plummet by 12%, and that there will be a million more people at risk of poverty, citizens, who are having such a bad time, need good news. and this is good news. europe has now done its homework and it is up to us to ensure that these funds are used correctly, that they reach the citizens as soon as possible and that not a single euro is wasted. i can only thank you, commissioner, for your involvement, and also the chairman of the committee on regional development and all the rapporteurs, who have worked so closely together, in this spirit of commitment, to ensure that these funds reach the citizens as soon as possible.

it will give a boost to business; it will help to recover and maintain jobs for hundreds of thousands of european citizens and it will help sectors as damaged as culture or tourism in these times of pandemic. this is certainly a great instrument which will help the member states to cope with this crisis. let us help them to make it a genuine solidarity and hope-filled opportunity in the face of this crisis. it is also a question of making it easier for public administrations to have the flexibility we have always been asking for: flexibility which is not at odds with efficiency in the use of resources; member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, i think we really can say that the european parliament has profoundly influenced the revision of this olaf regulation. you have supported all the commission proposals to strengthen the effectiveness of investigations, as well as our objective to base the relationship between olaf and the eppo on the strongest complementarity. in addition, you have pushed to ensure that olaf’s strong investigative tools co—exist with stronger procedural guarantees. in fact, the revised legislation establishes a controller to handle complaints by persons concerned. it allows the person under investigation to assess the final report under certain conditions and provides for respect of higher procedural safeguards when olaf supports the eppo. these amendments should increase the protection of persons subject to investigation and also increase the reliability of olaf’s work even though the rules on admissibility of olaf—collected evidence in subsequent criminal procedures will go unchanged. we should soon start implementing a new financial framework accompanied by an ambitious recovery plan. it’s crucial that the union will be ready to protect european union citizens’ money, ensuring that money is spent properly and effectively. having said this, also in response to mr freund and others, i think we have now provided the necessary financial means to the different organisations. in particular, we constantly hear that the eppo should get more money. i think even before they start working they have the same staff numbers as olaf, because you have to include the decentralised prosecutors and then you certainly arrive at the same number. and may i say that the original concept of the eppo was to focus very much on the work of the decentralised prosecutors, but now we see a very strong headquarters. again, i think we should see how it works, how it comes into operational daily work and then, of course, we have to adapt to the needs and see what are the future needs. i was very clear in the discussions. there are opportunities like amending budgets, etc., where we can, so to say, react in case there are further needs. but first, i think, we should now really pay attention so that the eppo can finally start – as soon as possible, hopefully in spring – its work, and then i think we can have a further assessment about the future needs. please be assured that we have every interest not only to protect our money, but also to get it back in the case of fraud and criminal cases. so indeed – and i think this was said by ms cseh and others – this is something which should really pay off in the way that we get the money back. both these institutions, but also others dealing with such issues, are indeed properly equipped, because they need it. we will certainly take care that at the end of the day we get the money back – and certainly more than we have to invest into the staffing and the financing of these institutions. but on the other hand, we have to see that everything is in balance and we really lead also by example. finally, we are talking today primarily about olaf and that’s why i would again like to highlight that the revised olaf regulation is a key element of the new eu anti—fraud architecture that we are building to achieve that objective. this is not only about the cooperation between olaf and eppo. here, of course, it’s about the investigative and prosecutorial powers of eppo which are now there, but we should not forget there is also europol, and its analytical capacity has to be used. the same applies to eurojust, which definitely has a coordinating role. so the reformed olaf is a centrepiece, if you like, of this architecture to carry out even more effective investigations. what we have to achieve is proper coordination, cooperation amongst all these institutions. it’s not only about the two, and we should not forget europol and eurojust and cooperation with these organisations so as to have complementarity and avoid overlapping. this also would contribute to more effectiveness of these european institutions and instruments.

in writing. as reported by europol, organised crime is already at work trying to exploit the many opportunities offered by the recovery fund and the various european structural funds provided to support member states in coping with the pandemic crisis. in this alarming context, the european union must equip itself with effective law enforcement bodies, and the establishment of the european public prosecutor's office (eppo). in the fight against fraud, which is detrimental to the union's budget, it is certainly a key stage in the european anti-fraud architecture. it was therefore equally essential to update regulation (eu) no 883/2013 governing the european anti-fraud office (olaf), whose task is to investigate illegal behaviour such as fraud and corruption, which often represent the first step of more complex criminal schemes to the detriment of the union budget. thanks to the commitment shown in the complex negotiations, which i had the honour of following in person, i can say with satisfaction that the new regulatory framework, which extends olaf's legal base and increases its investigative powers, will ensure close cooperation, exchange of information and complementarity of investigations with the european public prosecutor's office, but will also represent a significant step in further strengthening the rule of law in the european area of freedom, security and justice.
the next item is the report of mr kosma złotowski, drawn up on behalf of the committee on petitions, on the 2019 agenda of the committee on petitions (2020/2044(ini)) (a9-0263/2020). members are informed that no requested speeches or blue card questions will be allowed in the debate during this part-session. i would also point out that the speakers can be reached remotely from the liaison offices of the member states.
the rapporteur. ladies and gentlemen! the committee on petitions plays a special role in the work of our parliament. it is the only forum where eu citizens can not only present their problems and the challenges they face, but, more importantly, have a personal say in committee meetings. it is always invaluable for democracy when the victims can speak for themselves about their plight. we regret that this difficult year has deprived us of the opportunity to meet the petitioners in person. peti is also the most interdisciplinary of all committees. the programme is drawn up by the petitioners themselves who come to us. among the top topics in 2019 were the environment, transport, children's rights, the plight of the disabled and the many concerns on both sides of the brexit channel. the number of petitions filed increased by more than 11% compared to 2018. more petitions were also found admissible. unfortunately, still more than 30% of petitions did not directly relate to the competence or area of activity of the european union, which means that they could not be considered. the lack of awareness among citizens of the division of competences between brussels and the member states is a challenge to the process of discussing and responding to petitions that we will have to deal with in the coming years. cooperation with other parliamentary committees is a key element in the efficiency and effectiveness of the activities of the committee on petitions. in 2019, 65 petitions were sent to other committees for their opinions and 351 were forwarded for information. the input and expertise of colleagues from other committees is therefore necessary to respond fully to the questions and requests of the petitioners. among the important initiatives that have been indirectly inspired by the petitioners going to the european parliament is the proposal for a directive on the abolition of seasonal time changes and the repeal of directive 2000/84. the committee on petitions prepared an opinion for the report on this matter, which was based on numerous complaints from citizens calling for the abolition of this burdensome obligation. the commission has also received a number of petitions on brexit in 2019 and has also received a number of petitions on the protection of the rights of eu citizens before and after the uk's withdrawal. the committee on petitions has done a very good job here and its work has helped to ensure that citizens' rights remain one of parliament's main priorities in the negotiations between brussels and london. to sum up the work of the committee on petitions in 2019, two institutions that play a very important role in the process of responding to petitions should be mentioned. it is, of course, the european commission which provides us with its expertise in the hearings and discussions with the petitioners, but special thanks go to the european ombudsman. the best proof that this partnership is working was the re-election of ms emily o'reilly to the post last december. the committee on petitions was responsible for the process of analysing and hearing all the candidates. 2019 was a challenging year for the committee on petitions, due to a break in our work during the elections and the constitution of the new parliament. but i'm convinced that the time we had at our disposal was very well spent. this is due, of course, to the work of our president, mrs dolores montserrat, and the commitment of all the members of the committee, but also to the professionalism of the staff of our secretariat, who deserve our sincere thanks. i hope that parliament will also give a positive assessment of their work and accept the report presented by the committee on petitions on the activities of the committee over the past year.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, i thank the rapporteur, mr złotowski, for this comprehensive report, which will contribute to further strengthening relations between the commission and the committee on petitions (peti). as discussed during our structured dialogue two weeks ago, we are constantly working to improve our cooperation. i strongly believe the ability to petition is a fundamental right for all citizens and a key element of parliamentary democracy. today, more than ever against the backdrop of the ongoing covid-19 crisis, petitions are an effective channel for direct contact and open dialogue concerning problems affecting the daily lives of europeans. even before the pandemic, the use of petitions was on the increase, up by 11.23% in 2019 compared to the previous year. this was due in part to the more visible and accessible european parliament petitions web portal. many of the petitions were in the environmental, health and social sectors. let me assure you that the commission takes the work of this committee very seriously. we continue our efforts to improve the speed, quality and pertinence of our contributions to your replies to petitioners’ concerns. this includes the modernisation of our it tools for handling petitions and streamlining the underlying procedures. in addition, the commission is increasingly represented at your meetings by commissioners and high-ranking officials, especially when it comes to sensitive petitions. to put it in numbers, in the first year of our mandate, two commissioners, three directors-general, four deputy directors-general, 10 directors and 22 heads of unit participated in debates in the peti committee. this trend will continue in the coming year. i take note of the section of your report on the issue of petitions and infringement procedures. as you know, the primary purpose of the infringement procedure is to make sure that member states comply with eu law. it cannot be used to alter the specific legal situation of an individual or provide for individual means of redress. therefore, we believe that individual complaints pointing to the incorrect application of eu law are often better addressed by the appropriate mechanisms at national level. this approach leaves the commission more able to focus its resources on addressing systemic issues. i can assure you that we remain fully committed to fruitful cooperation in addressing infringement-related petitions. in this spirit, the commission welcomes the committee’s opinion on identifying potential systemic breaches of eu law with the petitions it receives. however, the decision on whether or not to investigate and pursue an instance of suspected non-compliance with eu law must remain within the commission’s discretionary power. the commission holds the burden of proof in bringing cases before the court of justice, and it must focus its efforts on issues that will bring the most benefit to eu citizens. i stress that, where petitions are not within the remit of the european union, we should be very honest and upfront in explaining this to petitioners.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals. i would like to thank my colleague, mr kosma złotowski, and the other shadow rapporteurs for their cooperation in this report. we have managed to produce an objective report that has not been politicised unnecessarily, and the work of the committee in 2019 is very well reflected. petitions are a sign of a mature democracy. any citizen, regardless of social status, can complain without fear of problems in the practical application of union law. petitions are a seismograph of public sentiment and at the same time a monitoring instrument that helps to strengthen the transparency and accountability of the institutions. the committee on petitions is the closest thing to the citizen in the day-to-day running of parliament. parliament received 1 357 petitions in 2019. this shows that eu citizens and residents continued to make use of the right to petition despite the break in the european elections. the number of users supporting one or more petitions on parliament's online portal for petitions has even increased compared to 2018, reaching a total of 28 075, which is very encouraging. what can we do better? it is a bit unfortunate that petitioners are still not sufficiently informed about the reasons for the inadmissibility of a petition. this can be a learning process for both sides: for us, but also, and above all, for the applicant. the citizens can learn from this to do better next time. a continuous public debate on the union's fields of activity is therefore essential to raise public awareness of the right to petition the european parliament. the committee on petitions should therefore also have an active press and communications service and an active social media presence to increase the visibility of the committee's work. it is up to us to deal with the petitions as quickly as possible and to give appropriate answers. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. it is not always clear to the citizen whether his problem is at the regional, national or eu level. here the committee on petitions needs to communicate and cooperate better with the national authorities, committees on petitions and ombudsmen. i would like to thank the secretariat of the committee on petitions and my colleagues for their work. i wish everyone a merry christmas and a healthy new year. and as i always say elsewhere, there is still much to be done, let us get on with it!
on behalf of the s&d group. madam president, article 227 of the treaty on the functioning of the european union provides, as one of the fundamental rights of citizens, for the ability to exercise their right to petition this house on matters affecting them within the european union. this gives enormous value to the committee on petitions, which is an instrument of citizen empowerment and, at the same time, a democratic instrument that defends the european values that inspire the union. and it certainly also gives us the opportunity to know better the problems of the citizens. we must therefore firmly defend the value of the committee on petitions and do so without undermining any rights, giving citizens a fair hearing under the rules we have given ourselves, serving as a platform or amplifier for the voices of those who are not always heard, those who are not attended to, those who suffer injustices or inequalities due to negligence or indifference. and we must do so in a balanced way, in accordance with objective criteria and not in the name of other tactical interests which can only harm european citizens. this house has passed important measures, the result of citizen petitions. the resolution on additional funding for biomedical research on myalgic encephalomyelitis, the result of a petition submitted in 2019 by the affected persons themselves, is a very relevant example. we have also succeeded in putting a stop to abuses by some airlines or other abuses in the area of infringement of european workers' rights. we've also put batteries in institutions, administrations. and in this connection, and to conclude, i would like to thank the european commission for the work it is doing and extend my thanks to commissioner dalli, also for her involvement. i would even like to ask you for a little more so that we can speed up the processing of certain requests that require urgency. thank you, too, to the colleagues and the house services for their annual work.
i am speaking on behalf of the renew group. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we write the peti annual report not just to present data, but above all to learn from it. the most frequent topics of petitions were again last year the environment, fundamental rights and justice. so these are issues to which we must pay particular attention. we also see that 30% of petitions are still considered inadmissible. this shows that many citizens are not clear about the areas in which the eu is active and that we need to educate them. it is also a sign of great confidence and hope in the european parliament that citizens turn to us with all kinds of problems. we must live up to that trust; we must not abuse it. the committee on petitions is a bridge between citizens and decision-makers. it would therefore be sensible to involve the committee on petitions directly in the legislative process, so that the good lessons learned from the petitions are not lost. the committee on petitions also deals not only with petitions but also with european citizens' initiatives in some cases, and it is important that they are taken seriously. the fact that a million signatures are collected from at least seven member states is a sign of the importance of the issue and of the expectations that many citizens have of the european union. if the elected representatives of the european parliament support this proposal, then the commission should see this as a clear mandate to present a legislative proposal. i thank rapporteur kosma złotowski and all my colleagues for their cooperation and i ask for your support for this successful report without further amendments. thank you.

merry christmas and a peaceful new year.

in writing. the state of play in the negotiations between the eu and the uk on a future trade agreement is worrying. the latest signals received indicate significant deadlocks that risk affecting the approval of this agreement so necessary for both parties. unfortunately, the lack of transparency on the negotiations that have taken place raises serious questions and does not allow the european parliament and citizens to analyse in detail the elements discussed and to be able to support the negotiations with a view to their swift approval by the european legislator. more worryingly, eu member states are not sufficiently prepared for a no-deal scenario. the business environment, academic cooperation or european citizens are at risk of being seriously affected. the right-wing government in romania has done nothing to prepare for this scenario. there is no legal regulation adopted, no financial support package for companies affected by the situation, no crisis support cell for citizens, absolutely nothing that the eu has recommended. i call on the negotiators and decision-makers on both sides to get more involved in resolving this situation. nobody can afford a failure but at the same time, nobody can afford a bad deal. the solution is in our hands, in the will and the ability to move forward and have this trade agreement.
